403A Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (An oxidizing material hazard that can intensify fire or cause a violent reaction): under WHMIS 2015, the flame-over-circle pictogram warns that a product can provide oxygen or cause or intensify a fire even without a direct ignition source. It is a distinct hazard class from flammables, corrosives, and compressed gases, each of which has its own separate pictogram. Recognizing it quickly matters because oxidizers must be stored well away from flammable materials on site.
  2. The correct answer is B (Approximately three metres, or about ten feet, above the working surface below): most Canadian provincial regulations set roughly three metres as the general trigger height at which fall protection becomes mandatory near an unprotected edge. Below that height, other controls may apply depending on the jurisdiction and the specific hazard present. A carpenter should always confirm the exact threshold and requirements in the province where the work is taking place.
  3. The correct answer is B (A block plane, which trims and smooths wood edges with a low blade angle): a block plane has a flat sole and a low-angle adjustable blade designed specifically for smoothing surfaces and trimming end grain with light, controlled shavings. A coping saw cuts curves, a rasp removes material aggressively rather than smoothing it, and an auger bit bores round holes. None of those other tools produce the smooth, planed finish described.
  4. The correct answer is A (Keeps the kerf open to stop the wood from pinching and kicking back): the riving knife sits directly behind the blade and holds the freshly cut kerf open as the workpiece passes through, preventing the wood from closing in on the blade and being thrown back at the operator. It does not guide the cut, measure depth, or control the guard, which are separate functions of other saw components. This makes it one of the most important safety features on a table saw.
  5. The correct answer is A (13.33 board feet, based on the given thickness, width, and length): board feet are calculated as thickness in inches multiplied by width in inches multiplied by length in feet, divided by 12. Here that is 2 times 8 times 10, divided by 12, which equals 13.33 board feet. Getting this formula right is essential for accurate lumber ordering and cost estimating on any framing job.
  6. The correct answer is D (Eleven-sixteenths of an inch, found by converting to a common sixteenth denominator): three-eighths of an inch converts to six-sixteenths, and adding five-sixteenths gives eleven-sixteenths total. Converting both fractions to a common denominator before adding is the reliable method for this kind of trade math. Skipping that step is a common source of measurement errors on the job site.
  7. The correct answer is C (A hidden feature or one located below grade, not visible in this view): dashed lines are a standard drafting convention used to show elements that exist but cannot be seen directly in the current view, such as a footing below a slab or a beam hidden above a ceiling. Solid lines are used for visible edges and features. Recognizing this convention helps a carpenter correctly interpret what is actually being shown on a drawing.
  8. The correct answer is A (Using wet cutting methods or an attached dust extraction system): wet cutting suppresses silica dust at the source by keeping it from becoming airborne, and dust extraction attachments capture particles before a worker can inhale them, which is far more effective than personal protective equipment alone. Safety glasses and a cloth dust mask do not adequately filter fine silica particles. Increasing blade speed has no protective effect and can increase dust generation.
  9. The correct answer is C (Approximately 2268 kilograms per worker attached to the anchor): a non-engineered fall-arrest anchor must be rated to support approximately 2268 kilograms, roughly 5000 pounds, per attached worker to provide an adequate safety margin during a fall-arrest event. This figure comes from standard fall-protection practice used across Canadian job sites. An anchor rated for a lower load could fail under the dynamic forces generated by an actual fall.
  10. The correct answer is B (The tongue of the square, the shorter arm of the two): on a standard framing square, the longer arm is called the body or blade, and the shorter arm is called the tongue. Both arms are marked with graduated scales used together for layout tasks such as stair stringers and roof rafters. Knowing this terminology is basic to reading any layout instructions that reference the square's parts.
  11. The correct answer is C (The blade binding or pinching within the cut being made): kickback most commonly happens when the material closes in on the blade during a cut, causing the spinning blade to grab the material and throw it, and the saw, back toward the operator. A sharp blade and a properly functioning guard actually reduce this risk rather than cause it. Feeding material too slowly is generally safer than feeding it too quickly or forcing it through a binding cut.
  12. The correct answer is B (0.625 inches, expressed to the nearest thousandth of an inch): five-eighths of an inch converts to 0.625 inches when expressed as a decimal, found by dividing 5 by 8. Being able to convert common trade fractions to decimals quickly is useful when working with suppliers or tools that use decimal measurements. The other listed values do not match this conversion.
  13. The correct answer is B (12 feet long on the actual finished building): at a scale of 1/4 inch equals 1 foot, each 1/4 inch on the drawing represents one actual foot. A 3 inch measurement on the drawing contains twelve 1/4 inch increments, so the wall is 12 feet long in reality. Misreading an architect's scale is a common source of layout error, so checking the math this way is a useful habit.
  14. The correct answer is A (5 feet between the two marked points on the layout): the 3-4-5 method relies on the Pythagorean relationship, where a triangle with legs of 3 feet and 4 feet must have a diagonal, or hypotenuse, of exactly 5 feet if the corner is truly square. If the measured diagonal is longer or shorter than 5 feet, the corner needs to be adjusted before proceeding. This method is a fast, reliable way to check squareness without specialized equipment.
  15. The correct answer is D (A benchmark, a fixed elevation point used as a constant reference): a benchmark is a permanent or semi-permanent point of known elevation that a survey crew or carpenter refers back to throughout a project to check grades, foundation heights, and floor levels. Batter boards and grade stakes are temporary layout aids rather than fixed elevation references. Property pins mark legal boundaries, not construction elevations.
  16. The correct answer is D (Permits are generally required for new structures and most significant alterations): across Canadian municipalities, a building permit is generally required before starting new construction or making significant structural or use changes to an existing building. Requirements vary by municipality and by the size or type of structure, so a carpenter should always confirm local rules before work begins. Assuming no permit is needed can lead to costly stop-work orders or forced removal of completed work.
  17. The correct answer is A (Count the wall length, spacing, and openings from the drawing carefully): an accurate material takeoff starts with carefully reading the actual dimensions, stud spacing, and openings shown on the drawing rather than guessing or estimating. Skipping openings or estimating without the drawing leads to over-ordering or shortages on site. This careful first step is the foundation for an accurate lumber order and cost estimate.
  18. The correct answer is B (A laser level or builder's level, used to compare elevations): a laser level or builder's level lets a carpenter compare elevations across a distance by shooting a level reference line to multiple points, confirming whether foundation piers are all set at the same height. A framing square checks angles, a plumb bob checks vertical alignment, and a chalk line snaps straight lines, none of which measure elevation across a distance. Choosing the right layout tool for the task avoids costly framing errors later.
  19. The correct answer is C (A hinged door and the direction its swing will travel): a break in the wall line combined with a quarter-circle arc is the standard architectural symbol for a hinged door, with the arc showing the direction and radius of the door's swing. Windows are typically shown differently, usually without a swing arc, and beams or electrical panels use entirely different symbols. Reading this symbol correctly helps a carpenter frame the rough opening in the right location and orientation.
  20. The correct answer is A (A standard hinged door, shown by its quarter-circle swing symbol): the shape drawn at symbol 1 on the plan is the classic quarter-circle swing arc used on floor plans to represent a hinged door and the direction it opens. A window would be drawn without that swing arc, and a beam pocket or closet would use different plan symbols entirely. Reading the plan correctly at this location tells the carpenter exactly what rough opening to frame.
  21. The correct answer is D (So the layout lines survive excavation and can be restored): batter boards are deliberately set back from the actual building corners so that the string lines marking the true building lines are not disturbed by excavation equipment digging the foundation trench. Once excavation is complete, the strings can be re-stretched between the batter boards to re-establish the exact corner points. This is a practical layout technique, not a code setback requirement.
  22. The correct answer is B (Datum dimensioning, where every measurement runs from one shared point): datum dimensioning, sometimes called baseline dimensioning, measures every dimension from a single common reference point rather than from the previous dimension. This reduces the accumulation of small measuring errors that can occur with chain dimensioning, where each dimension is measured from the one before it. Recognizing which method a drawing uses helps a carpenter transfer dimensions accurately during layout.
  23. The correct answer is C (To cover normal waste, cutoffs, and minor errors during construction): adding a waste percentage to a material takeoff accounts for the normal cutoffs, minor mistakes, and material damage that occur during any real construction process. Without this allowance, a crew commonly runs short mid-job, causing costly delays waiting for additional material. This is standard estimating practice across residential and commercial framing work.
  24. The correct answer is D (160 square feet, calculated from the given room dimensions): floor area is found by multiplying a room's width by its length, and 10 feet multiplied by 16 feet equals 160 square feet. This calculation uses only the dimensions given for the room and does not depend on the window shown on the plan. Being able to calculate area quickly from plan dimensions is a basic estimating skill.
  25. The correct answer is D (The section view, showing a vertical cut through the assembly): a section view shows a vertical slice through a building at a specific cut line, revealing how the floor, wall, and roof assemblies stack together and relate to one another. A floor plan shows a horizontal view from above, and an elevation shows an exterior face, neither of which reveals the internal vertical assembly the way a section does. Section views are essential for understanding framing details that plans and elevations cannot fully show.
  26. The correct answer is C (Grid lines, used to establish a consistent structural reference system): grid lines, labelled with a consistent system of numbers and letters, let anyone reading the drawing set locate the same column or wall on any sheet in the package. Contour lines describe site elevation changes, property lines mark legal boundaries, and centre lines only mark the middle of individual members, none of which serve this cross-referencing purpose. Grid lines are especially important for coordinating structural and mechanical drawings on larger projects.
  27. The correct answer is C (It produces stronger, more durable concrete with less excess water): lowering the water-cement ratio reduces the excess water left behind after hydration, which means fewer voids form in the cured concrete and the resulting mix is both stronger and more durable. A higher ratio makes concrete easier to place but weakens the finished product. A carpenter should never let a crew add extra water to a load simply to make it easier to pour into tight formwork.
  28. The correct answer is B (The workability, or fluidity, of the fresh concrete mix): a slump test measures how much a sample of fresh concrete settles after the cone is lifted, which indicates how workable, or fluid, the mix is at that moment. It does not directly measure compressive strength, air content, or aggregate proportions, which require separate tests. A low slump suits stiff flatwork mixes, while a higher slump flows more easily into narrow forms and around dense rebar.
  29. The correct answer is B (It gives water room to expand into as it freezes, resisting scaling): air-entrained concrete contains millions of microscopic air bubbles that give trapped water somewhere to expand into when it freezes, preventing the internal pressure that would otherwise cause the surface to scale and spall. This is especially important on exterior flatwork exposed to Canadian freeze-thaw cycles and de-icing salt. Code requires air entrainment for exterior exposed concrete across most of the country for exactly this reason.
  30. The correct answer is D (When the air temperature is expected to drop below five degrees Celsius): cold-weather concreting precautions are generally triggered when the air temperature is expected to drop below about five degrees Celsius during the curing period, since hydration slows significantly in cold conditions. Precautions can include heated water and aggregate, insulating blankets, and sometimes accelerating admixtures. Concrete that freezes before gaining adequate strength suffers permanent, irreversible damage.
  31. The correct answer is D (Approximately 24 to 48 hours after the concrete has been placed): as a general guide, always confirmed against the project's engineering requirements and local code, typical wall forms are left in place for roughly 24 to 48 hours in normal conditions, enough time for the concrete to support its own weight and resist minor handling damage. Forms carrying construction loads, such as shoring under a suspended slab, need to stay in place considerably longer. Stripping forms too early risks sagging, cracking, or a damaged finished surface.
  32. The correct answer is C (They resist the outward pressure of wet concrete pushing the two faces apart): form ties run through the wall thickness specifically to hold the two opposing form faces together against the outward hydrostatic pressure that wet concrete exerts as it fills the form. Walers distribute that tie load evenly across the sheathing, and strongbacks keep tall forms rigid, but the ties themselves are what resist the outward push. Without adequate ties, formwork can bulge or blow out during a pour.
  33. The correct answer is C (Dowels, bars that tie together two separate adjoining concrete pours): dowels are short lengths of rebar left projecting from one concrete pour so they can be embedded into a second, adjoining pour, structurally tying the two together across what would otherwise be a cold joint. Chairs support the rebar cage at the correct height, walers distribute tie loads, and strongbacks stiffen tall formwork, none of which describe projecting bars linking two pours. Dowels are commonly used between a footing and the wall poured on top of it.
  34. The correct answer is D (The bar's diameter in millimetres, giving 15M a diameter near 16mm): the number in the Canadian metric rebar designation approximates the bar's diameter in millimetres, so a 15M bar is roughly 16 millimetres in diameter, and a 10M bar is roughly 11.3 millimetres. The letter M simply denotes that this is the metric bar designation system. Residential foundation work commonly uses 10M or 15M bar depending on what the engineered drawings specify.
  35. The correct answer is B (Cover, the protective distance of concrete surrounding the embedded steel): cover is the term for the required depth of concrete surrounding embedded rebar, protecting the steel from moisture and corrosion while positioning it where it does the most structural good. Chairs and spacers are used specifically to maintain correct cover during the pour. Slump, cure time, and set time all describe entirely different properties of the concrete itself, not the position of the reinforcing steel.
  36. The correct answer is D (A strip footing, spreading the load across enough soil area): a strip footing is a continuous concrete element sized to spread a building's load over enough soil area to stay within the soil's bearing capacity, with a foundation wall built on top of it. This is the most common foundation approach across Canadian residential and light commercial construction. Pile caps, grade beams, and raft slabs are all used in specific situations but are far less common for typical residential work.
  37. The correct answer is D (A slab-on-grade foundation, poured directly on the prepared soil): a slab-on-grade foundation skips a full foundation wall and basement, pouring a single thickened-edge or monolithic slab directly on grade, which is common in warmer climates with shallower frost depths or where a basement is not wanted. A strip footing with a full basement wall is a different, deeper approach, and a preserved wood foundation still requires a wall assembly above a footing. This approach suits the scenario described because it eliminates the basement entirely.
  38. The correct answer is A (To prevent seasonal soil freezing and thawing from heaving the footing): water in the soil expands when it freezes, and if a footing sits above the local frost line, seasonal freezing and thawing will heave the footing up and down, cracking the foundation and everything built on it. Frost depth requirements vary significantly across Canada, so a carpenter must confirm the specific local minimum before excavation. This has nothing to do with concrete set time or drainage tile placement.
  39. The correct answer is C (Damp-proofing, a basic coating that resists moisture migration through the wall): damp-proofing is typically a bituminous coating applied to the wall exterior that resists ordinary moisture migration but is not designed to hold back significant hydrostatic pressure. A full waterproofing membrane provides more robust, continuous protection where a high water table is expected. Weeping tile, rigid insulation, and interior vapour barriers all serve different, related but distinct purposes in a foundation's moisture management system.
  40. The correct answer is C (To lock the wall to the footing and resist lateral sliding): a keyway formed into the top of a footing, along with dowels projecting up from the footing, locks the wall to the footing so the two act as one unit and resist lateral and shear forces trying to slide the wall off its base. It has nothing to do with drainage, reducing concrete volume, or creating an expansion joint. This detail is especially important on sloped sites or where lateral soil pressure is significant.
  41. The correct answer is A (Approximately 2.08 cubic metres, rounded to the supplier's nearest increment): applying a 10 percent waste allowance to 1.888 cubic metres gives 1.888 multiplied by 1.10, which equals approximately 2.08 cubic metres. Ready-mix suppliers typically deliver in defined truckload increments, so this figure would be rounded up slightly when placing the order. Running short mid-pour and needing a second truck creates a cold joint, which is worse than ordering a small amount extra.
  42. The correct answer is C (Pressure-treated lumber rated for contact with concrete and ground): the sill plate sits in direct contact with concrete and the moisture associated with it, so it must be pressure-treated lumber rated for that exposure, since untreated lumber would decay under those conditions. Kiln-dried, finger-jointed, and rough-sawn green lumber are all used elsewhere in framing but are not appropriate for direct concrete contact. This is one of the first details checked when framing begins on top of a foundation.
  43. The correct answer is A (It seals concrete surface irregularities and blocks air infiltration underneath): a sill gasket is a foam strip laid along the top of the foundation wall specifically to seal minor irregularities in the concrete surface and block air infiltration between the foundation and the wood framing above. It is not a structural connector, a vapour barrier for the whole wall, or a load path for seismic and wind forces, those functions are handled by the anchor bolts and the framing itself. This small detail matters for both energy efficiency and moisture control.
  44. The correct answer is A (The king stud, a full-length stud nailed to the jack stud): the king stud runs the full height of the wall from the bottom plate to the top plate immediately beside the opening, and the shortened jack stud is nailed to it while carrying the header's load down to the bottom plate. A cripple stud is a short filler stud above or below an opening, a rim joist belongs to the floor system, and the header itself is the horizontal member spanning the opening, not the vertical member beside it. Together the king stud and jack stud form the main structural support at the sides of a framed opening.
  45. The correct answer is B (A rim joist, sized and treated the same as the joists it caps): a rim joist caps the ends of the field joists around the perimeter of a floor system, ties the whole floor system together as a unit, and provides a nailing surface for sheathing and the wall framing above. It is sized like the joists it caps rather than treated as simple trim. A ledger, a trimmer joist, and a strongback all serve different specific purposes elsewhere in floor and formwork construction.
  46. The correct answer is B (10 studs total, including both end studs on the wall): a 12 foot wall equals 144 inches, and dividing by 16 inch on-centre spacing gives 9 stud spaces, which requires 10 studs when both end studs are included. This is the standard way to estimate stud count for a plain wall section with no openings. Openings would add jack studs, king studs, and cripple studs on top of this base count.
  47. The correct answer is B (Slope, expressed as rise over a 12-inch run, such as 6 in 12): roof slope, sometimes called pitch in everyday trade language, is expressed as the number of inches a roof rises vertically for every 12 inches it runs horizontally, written as a ratio such as 6 in 12. Span describes total horizontal roof width, overhang describes how far a rafter extends past the wall, and ridge height describes vertical distance to the peak, all of which are different measurements. Reading slope correctly is essential for calculating rafter length and cutting proper angles.
  48. The correct answer is A (A birdsmouth, a combined seat and heel cut at the plate): a birdsmouth is the notch cut into the underside of a rafter where it bears on the top wall plate, combining a level seat cut and a plumb heel cut so the rafter sits flat against the plate while keeping its full depth above the notch. A plumb cut at the ridge and a tail cut at the overhang are different cuts made elsewhere on the same rafter. A gusset is a connector plate used on trusses, not a cut made into solid lumber.
  49. The correct answer is A (Unit rise and unit run, describing each individual step's dimensions): unit rise and unit run describe the vertical and horizontal dimensions of a single individual step, repeated consistently up the flight, as distinct from total rise and total run, which describe the entire staircase from bottom to top. Headroom and going are separate stair terms describing clearance and a different measurement convention used in some codes. Nosing and riser describe physical stair components rather than the layout measurements themselves.
  50. The correct answer is D (The stringer, the sloped board supporting the treads and risers): a stringer is the sloped structural board, typically one placed near each side of the staircase, that carries the individual treads and risers along its length. A riser is the vertical board between treads, a tread is the horizontal surface stepped on, and a nosing is the small overhang at the front edge of a tread, none of which describe the sloped supporting board itself. Stringers are usually cut from 2x12 lumber or built as an engineered assembly for wider staircases.
  51. The correct answer is B (Approximately 8 feet of total rise from the wall plate to the ridge): For an 8 in 12 pitch, the rafter rises 8 inches for every 12 inches of horizontal run. Over a 12 foot run, that works out to 8 inches multiplied by 12, or 96 inches, which equals exactly 8 feet. Confirming this rise before cutting rafters helps a carpenter select the correct rafter length and ridge height.
  52. The correct answer is D (The roof rises 6 inches in height for every 12 inches of horizontal run): Roof pitch notation always states the vertical rise over a 12 inch base measurement of horizontal run. A 6 in 12 pitch is a moderate slope commonly seen on many residential roofs across Canada. Reading pitch correctly is essential before calculating rafter length, cutting angles, or ordering roofing material.
  53. The correct answer is A (The hip rafter, which frames the external corner of the sloped roof): On a hip roof, the hip rafter runs diagonally from the corner of the wall plate up to the ridge, forming the sloped line at each external corner. It differs from a valley rafter, which frames an internal corner where two roof planes meet instead. Jack rafters then land against the hip rafter to complete the roof surface on either side.
  54. The correct answer is C (The common rafters, which run square between the plate and the ridge): Common rafters run the full distance from the wall plate to the ridge at a right angle to both members, requiring only a plumb cut and a seat cut. Hip, valley, and jack rafters all involve compound or bevelled cuts because they meet the roof at an angle rather than square. Common rafters make up the bulk of a simple gable roof's framing.
  55. The correct answer is B (A jack rafter, which spans a shortened distance against a hip or valley): A jack rafter is a shortened version of a common rafter that spans only between a hip or valley rafter and either the wall plate or the ridge. Because it lands against an angled member, it requires a compound cut where it meets the hip or valley. Jack rafters are typically cut in matching sets that get progressively shorter as they approach the corner.
  56. The correct answer is A (It ties the rafter pair together to resist wind uplift and ridge racking forces): A collar tie installed in the upper third of a pair of opposing rafters primarily resists wind uplift and helps prevent the ridge from racking or separating under wind load. Gravity load spreading, the outward thrust pushing the rafter pair apart at the wall plate, is instead resisted by a rafter tie or ceiling joist positioned low in the assembly, where it has far more leverage against that thrust. Collar ties are typically spaced at set intervals along the length of the roof.
  57. The correct answer is D (The tread, which is the horizontal surface a person steps on directly): The tread is the horizontal surface of a stair step where a person places their foot while climbing or descending. The riser is the vertical piece closing the gap between two treads, and the stringer is the structural side member that supports both. Knowing these terms precisely matters when laying out and cutting a staircase on site.
  58. The correct answer is C (The stringer, which is the structural side member supporting the stair run): The stringer is the notched or routed structural side member of a stairway that carries the treads and risers along the full length of the run. A typical straight run stair uses two or three stringers depending on tread width and expected load. Correctly laying out the stringer notches is one of the most critical steps in building a safe, comfortable staircase.
  59. The correct answer is A (The unit rise, which is the vertical height measured for a single step): The unit rise is the vertical distance from the top of one tread to the top of the next tread on a stairway. It must stay consistent step to step, since even a small variation between risers creates a serious trip hazard for anyone using the stairs. The unit rise works together with the unit run to determine a comfortable and code compliant stair angle.
  60. The correct answer is D (A roof truss, which is a shop-built triangulated member with connector plates): A roof truss is a prefabricated structural roof member engineered off site, with metal connector plates joining its webs and chords into a rigid triangulated shape. Because trusses are engineered to specific loads and spans, they arrive with their own manufacturer layout drawing that must be followed on site. Trusses are commonly used instead of stick framed rafters on many modern residential roofs.
  61. The correct answer is B (Approximately 56 inches of total rise from the wall plate to the ridge): For a 4 in 12 pitch, the rafter rises 4 inches for every 12 inches of horizontal run. Over a 14 foot run, that works out to 4 inches multiplied by 14, or 56 inches of total rise. Lower pitches like this one are common on additions where headroom or roofline matching is a concern.
  62. The correct answer is C (10 inches, which is a common tread run used on many standard stairs): The diagram shows a stairway with a total rise of 108 inches divided across 16 equal risers, using a tread run of 10 inches for each step. A 10 inch tread run is a common and comfortable dimension used on many standard residential and commercial stairways. Consistent tread run from step to step is just as important for safety as consistent riser height.
  63. The correct answer is C (It stops liquid water from entering while still allowing trapped vapour to escape): A weather resistive barrier is designed to block liquid water from penetrating the wall assembly while still allowing water vapour trapped within the wall to escape outward. It works together with, rather than replacing, proper flashing at windows, doors, and other penetrations. Most weather resistive barriers are not intended to serve as the wall's primary air barrier on their own.
  64. The correct answer is D (A rainscreen, which allows drainage and drying to occur behind the cladding): A rainscreen is the drained and vented airspace created behind exterior cladding by installing furring strips over the weather resistive barrier. This airspace lets any water that gets past the cladding drain freely and allows the wall assembly to dry out rather than trapping moisture against the sheathing. Rainscreen assemblies are increasingly common on Canadian exterior walls in wetter climates.
  65. The correct answer is B (Lap siding, where each course overlaps the course installed directly below it): Lap siding is installed with each course overlapping the course below it, so only the lower edge of each piece is left exposed to the weather. This overlapping pattern sheds water down and off the wall rather than allowing it to sit at horizontal joints. Board and batten and panel siding profiles use different overlap or joint details entirely.
  66. The correct answer is A (Sill flashing first, followed by the jambs, then the head last): At a window rough opening, the sill flashing is installed first, followed by the jamb flashing, with the head flashing installed last so it laps over the top of the jambs. This shingled overlap sequence ensures water draining down the wall is always directed out and over the layer below it rather than behind it. Getting this order wrong is a common source of hidden water damage around windows.
  67. The correct answer is D (Head flashing, which is installed directly above the window or door opening): Head flashing is installed directly above a window or door opening to intercept water running down the wall and direct it out away from the top of the opening. It differs from step flashing, which is used along a sloped roof to sidewall junction, and from counter flashing, used mainly with masonry details. Proper head flashing installation is essential to keeping water out of the rough opening.
  68. The correct answer is C (Self-adhered ice and water protection membrane installed under the shingles): Self-adhered ice and water protection membrane is installed at eaves and valleys in cold climates specifically to seal around fasteners and resist water that backs up under shingles when an ice dam forms. Standard building paper and house wrap do not provide the same self-sealing protection against backed-up water. This membrane is a standard requirement on most Canadian roofs in regions that experience regular ice damming.
  69. The correct answer is A (The exposure, which is the visible portion left uncovered on each course): The exposure is the portion of each asphalt shingle course that remains visible to the weather once the course above has been installed and overlapped it. Manufacturers specify a standard exposure for each shingle product, and following it consistently keeps the roof watertight and visually uniform. The headlap, by contrast, refers to the hidden overlapped portion rather than the visible exposure.
  70. The correct answer is D (Open metal valley flashing or a properly woven shingle valley detail): At a roof valley, open metal valley flashing or a properly woven shingle valley is the standard method used under asphalt shingles to carry the concentrated flow of water safely down the joint and away from the roof. A simple drip edge alone does not provide enough protection at this high water volume location. Valley details are one of the most failure prone areas of a shingled roof if installed incorrectly.
  71. The correct answer is B (It directs roof runoff outward away from the wall instead of behind the cladding): Kick-out flashing is installed at the bottom of a step flashed junction to divert roof runoff outward, away from the wall, rather than allowing it to run behind the cladding and into the wall assembly. Without it, water concentrated at that point is a common cause of hidden rot inside the wall below the junction. Kick-out flashing is a small detail that prevents a disproportionately large amount of water damage.
  72. The correct answer is A (The eavestrough, which collects and channels rainwater toward a downspout): The eavestrough is the horizontal trough installed along the eaves to collect rainwater running off the shingles and channel it toward a downspout rather than letting it fall directly to the ground. The fascia and soffit are separate components located near the eave but serve different functions entirely. A properly sloped and sized eavestrough is important for managing water away from the foundation.
  73. The correct answer is C (The soffit, which closes the underside of the overhang and is often vented): The soffit is the horizontal panel installed under a roof overhang, running between the fascia and the wall, and it is often perforated or vented to allow air into the attic space. Adequate soffit ventilation works together with roof or ridge venting to help control attic moisture and temperature. The frieze and rake board are separate trim pieces located elsewhere on the exterior.
  74. The correct answer is D (The rake board, which trims the sloped gable edge of the roof): The rake board is the vertical trim board that runs along the sloped edge of a gable roof, covering the exposed ends of the roof sheathing and any rafter tails along that slope. The fascia board, by comparison, trims the level horizontal eave edge rather than the sloped gable edge. Getting rake and fascia details right is important for both weather protection and finished appearance.
  75. The correct answer is B (It directs water away from the fascia and into the eavestrough below): Drip edge is a formed metal strip installed along the eaves and rakes before shingles go on, and its function is to direct water away from the fascia board and into the eavestrough rather than letting it run down behind the fascia. It works alongside, not instead of, ice and water protection membrane at vulnerable locations. Skipping drip edge is a common cause of rotted fascia boards over time.
  76. The correct answer is C (Board and batten siding, with narrow strips covering the joints between boards): Board and batten siding is a vertical exterior siding style built from wide boards with narrow battens fastened over each joint to cover the gaps between them. This pattern creates the rustic, farmhouse style appearance the homeowner is describing, and differs from horizontal profiles like Dutch lap or channel rustic siding. Board and batten remains a popular choice on both traditional and modern Canadian exteriors.
  77. The correct answer is C (Casing, which is the trim installed around the perimeter of a door or window): Casing is the interior trim installed around the perimeter of a door or window opening, covering the gap between the jamb and the surrounding wall finish. Baseboard and chair rail are installed at completely different heights on the wall and serve different purposes. Casing is typically mitred at the corners for a clean, finished appearance.
  78. The correct answer is A (Backing, which provides a solid anchoring surface for a future fixture): Backing is extra lumber or blocking installed inside a wall cavity specifically to provide a solid surface for fastening a future fixture such as a cabinet, grab bar, or television mount. It is planned and installed before the drywall goes up, since it is very difficult to add after the wall is finished. Fire blocking and cripple studs serve entirely different structural or fire safety purposes.
  79. The correct answer is B (The nosing, which projects past the riser and rounds off the tread edge): The nosing is the trim piece on the exposed edge of a stair tread that projects slightly beyond the riser below it, rounding off the edge where a person's foot first makes contact. It also adds a small amount of usable tread depth without increasing the overall stair footprint. The skirt board and fascia stringer are separate finishing pieces used elsewhere on a staircase.
  80. The correct answer is D (Baseboard, which covers the joint between the wall finish and the floor): Baseboard is the horizontal trim board installed around a room at floor level, covering the joint between the finished wall surface and the top edge of the flooring material. It is installed after the finished flooring goes down so that it can be scribed and fitted tightly against the floor. Casing and chair rail are installed at different locations and serve different purposes on the wall.
  81. The correct answer is A (Stiles and rails, the vertical and horizontal members forming the face frame): Stiles and rails are the vertical and horizontal solid wood members that make up a face frame cabinet's front frame, surrounding the door and drawer openings. Stiles run vertically and rails run horizontally, and together they provide the surface for hanging doors and mounting drawer hardware. Toe kicks, valances, and filler strips are separate cabinet components with different functions.
  82. The correct answer is C (The toe kick, the recessed base area set back from the cabinet face): The toe kick is the recessed area at the bottom of a base cabinet, set back several inches from the cabinet doors above it, allowing a person to stand comfortably close to the counter. It is a standard feature on nearly all modern base cabinets. The apron and valance are separate trim elements found elsewhere on cabinetry or window installations.
  83. The correct answer is B (It provides a smooth, stable, and appropriately rated surface for the finish flooring): An underlayment panel such as plywood or cement board is added over the subfloor sheathing to provide a smooth, stable, and appropriately rated surface for the specific finish flooring being installed. Tile and cement board underlayment need a rigid, moisture tolerant surface that standard subfloor sheathing alone may not reliably provide. Underlayment requirements vary depending on the finish flooring product and its manufacturer instructions.
  84. The correct answer is D (How securely each drywall panel is held to the framing to prevent sagging): Fastener pattern spacing controls how securely each drywall panel is held to the framing members behind it, which directly affects whether the panel will sag or the fasteners will eventually pop through the finished surface. Manufacturer instructions and the building code both specify maximum spacing limits for screws or nails on walls and ceilings. Following the correct pattern is especially important on ceilings, where sagging is more likely under the panel's own weight.
  85. The correct answer is A (Chair rail, a horizontal moulding installed partway up the wall at waist height): Chair rail is a horizontal moulding installed on an interior wall at approximately waist height, historically intended to protect the wall finish from the backs of chairs pushed against it. Picture rail is installed much higher, near the top of the wall, for a different purpose entirely. Chair rail remains a common decorative detail in dining rooms and hallways today.
  86. The correct answer is B (Skirt board, which covers the cut ends of treads and risers along the wall): The skirt board is the finished trim board installed along the wall side of an enclosed staircase, covering the rough cut ends of the treads and risers where they meet the wall. It gives the staircase a clean, finished appearance on the side that does not have an open stringer or balustrade. Handrail returns and newel caps are separate finishing details elsewhere on the stair.
  87. The correct answer is D (Plinth block, which lets casing and baseboard meet cleanly at floor level): A plinth block is a small decorative block installed where a door casing meets the baseboard, slightly wider and thicker than both, allowing the two different trim profiles to meet cleanly without a difficult mitred joint. It is a traditional detail still used in many finish carpentry projects today. Rosette blocks and ferrule blocks serve entirely different purposes elsewhere in finish work.
  88. The correct answer is C (A filler strip, which closes a gap at the end of a cabinet run): A filler strip is a narrow piece of matching cabinet material added at the end of a cabinet run to close a gap against an adjoining wall that is not perfectly straight or square. It allows the adjacent door or drawer to open freely without striking the wall. Stile extensions and scribe rails are separate cabinet fitting techniques used in different situations.
  89. The correct answer is A (Acoustic underlayment, mainly to cushion the floor and reduce impact sound transfer): Acoustic underlayment is a thin foam or felt roll installed beneath floating laminate or engineered wood flooring mainly to cushion the floor underfoot and reduce impact sound transmission to the space below. It is a different product from cement backer board or a structural subfloor panel, which serve entirely different purposes. Many laminate and engineered flooring products require a specific underlayment to maintain the manufacturer warranty.
  90. The correct answer is B (Leg or side casing, the vertical pieces running the full height of the opening): Leg or side casing refers to the two vertical pieces of an interior door casing that run the full height of the opening on either side, meeting the horizontal head casing at mitred corners. Together the head casing and the leg casings form the complete trim assembly around the opening. Apron trim and mitre returns are separate finish carpentry details used in other locations.
  91. The correct answer is A (Confirm whether the wall is load bearing before any demolition work begins): Before removing any interior partition wall, the most important first step is to confirm whether the wall is load bearing, since removing a load bearing wall without proper temporary support or a new structural beam can cause serious structural problems. This assessment should happen before any cutting, prying, or demolition work begins. Skipping this step is one of the most common and dangerous mistakes made during interior renovations.
  92. The correct answer is D (The wall runs perpendicular to the floor joists above a similar wall or beam): A wall running perpendicular to the floor joists above and sitting directly over a similar wall or beam on the floor below is a reliable general indicator that the wall may be load bearing. The presence of a door opening, the wall's paint colour, or which floor it is on are not reliable indicators of load bearing status on their own. A professional assessment is still recommended whenever there is genuine doubt.
  93. The correct answer is B (They temporarily carry the loads above while the permanent structure is installed): Temporary shoring is installed to carry the structural loads from above while the load bearing wall or its header is being removed and a new permanent structural member is being installed. Once the new beam or header is properly installed and load transferred to it, the temporary shoring is removed. Shoring is a critical safety step whenever a load bearing element is temporarily out of the load path.
  94. The correct answer is C (Current requirements apply to the altered work even though the rest was built earlier): When a carpenter alters a structural element or a fire separation during a renovation, current building code requirements apply to that altered work even though the rest of the building may have been built under older rules that no longer apply. Simply matching the existing older construction can leave the altered area non-compliant with today's minimum safety standards. This principle applies broadly across structural, fire separation, and life safety related renovation work.
  95. The correct answer is A (Suspected asbestos containing material such as old vermiculite or textured ceiling finish): Suspected asbestos containing material, such as old vermiculite insulation or textured stipple ceiling finish found in an older building, should prompt a carpenter to stop work immediately and arrange for testing before disturbing the material further. Disturbing asbestos containing material without proper precautions can release hazardous fibres into the air. New materials clearly installed within recent years do not carry the same historical asbestos risk.
  96. The correct answer is B (It limits the spread of dust and airborne particles to unaffected areas): A temporary dust barrier and negative air containment setup limits the spread of dust and airborne particles from the demolition area to unaffected parts of an occupied building during a renovation. It does not increase structural capacity and does not replace the need for personal respiratory protection for workers inside the containment. Proper containment is especially important in occupied buildings such as hospitals, schools, or apartment buildings.
  97. The correct answer is D (Ongoing or past water leakage causing moisture damage and possible mould growth): Soft, discoloured, and spongy subfloor sheathing found around a toilet flange most likely indicates ongoing or past water leakage that has caused moisture damage and possibly mould growth in that area. This is not normal wear from foot traffic, and it usually requires cutting out and replacing the damaged subfloor once the leak source is identified and corrected. Ignoring this condition can lead to further structural damage over time.
  98. The correct answer is C (Removing mould without fixing the moisture source usually allows it to return): Removing visible mould without first identifying and correcting the moisture source that is feeding its growth usually allows the mould to return once the area is closed back up. Addressing the moisture source is the only way to permanently resolve the underlying problem rather than just its visible symptom. Mould remediation and moisture source repair typically need to happen together as part of the same renovation scope.
  99. The correct answer is B (A header sized to carry the load down to the jack studs beside it): When cutting a new doorway opening into an existing load bearing wall, a header sized to carry the load above the opening down to the jack studs on each side must generally be installed to transfer the structural load safely around the new opening. The header size depends on the span of the opening and the load it needs to carry. A ledger fastened only to drywall or a plate with no support would not safely carry that load.
  100. The correct answer is A (Shoring, temporary structural support installed to carry loads during a repair): Shoring is the correct general term for temporary structural support installed under a floor or roof to carry loads from above while the permanent framing members below are being repaired or replaced. Bridging and strapping serve entirely different purposes related to floor joist stiffness and connector strapping rather than temporary load support. Proper shoring is essential any time permanent structural members are temporarily removed or weakened during a repair.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (The product can burn skin, damage eyes, or corrode metal on contact and needs careful handling): the corroded surface and hand pictogram inside the red diamond specifically identifies a corrosive hazard under the WHMIS/GHS system. This pictogram, combined with the signal word Danger, tells the worker the product needs gloves, eye protection, and careful handling before the container is even opened. Confusing it with the flame or gas cylinder pictograms could lead a worker to skip the skin and eye protection that this specific hazard actually requires.
  2. The correct answer is D (9.25 feet, found by dividing 3 inches by 12 and adding it to 9): converting a feet-inch measurement to decimal feet means dividing the inch portion by 12 and adding the result to the whole feet. Here, 3 divided by 12 equals 0.25, so 9 feet 3 inches becomes 9.25 feet. Simply moving the decimal point on the inch value, as in options A and B, does not perform the actual division and produces an incorrect result.
  3. The correct answer is A (The vent pipe has not been engineered or rated to carry fall arrest loads and could fail): an anchor point for a personal fall arrest system must be engineered or rated to hold the forces generated in a fall, never chosen just because it looks solid. A vent pipe is designed to carry the weight of the pipe and flashing, not the sudden dynamic load of a falling worker. Using an unrated anchor defeats the purpose of the harness and lanyard entirely, since the anchor itself can tear loose.
  4. The correct answer is C (Hearing protection such as plugs or muffs, worn for the full shift): cumulative noise exposure from tools like framing nailers and circular saws causes hearing loss that builds up gradually and does not show symptoms until years later. Hearing protection needs to go on before the tool starts and stay on for the duration of noisy work, not just during moments that feel uncomfortably loud. Waiting until ringing ears signal a problem means the damage has already begun.
  5. The correct answer is B (4 feet from the wall, one foot out for every four feet of height): the 4:1 rule sets the base distance at one foot of horizontal offset for every four feet of vertical height to the point of contact. With a 16 foot height, dividing by 4 gives a required base offset of 4 feet. Setting the base too close, as in option A, risks the ladder tipping backward, while setting it too far out risks the base kicking out from under the climber.
  6. The correct answer is A (The unsupported board sagged and closed on the blade, pinching it in the kerf): kickback commonly happens when material is supported only at the ends, allowing the middle to sag under its own weight as the cut progresses and close the kerf onto the blade. That pinching binds the blade and can throw the saw back toward the operator with real force. Supporting the material properly along its length, rather than just at the ends, removes the sag that causes this specific kickback pattern.
  7. The correct answer is D (Bend at the knees, keep the back straight, and hold the load close): proper lifting technique keeps the load close to the body, bends at the knees rather than the waist, and keeps the back straight through the whole lift. Twisting while carrying a load, as in option B, is one of the most common causes of back strain and should be replaced by pivoting the feet instead. For a load this heavy, a two person lift or a hand truck is often the smarter choice entirely.
  8. The correct answer is C (The Safety Data Sheet binder kept on site for the products in use): WHMIS ties together supplier labels, worker training, and Safety Data Sheets, and the SDS is where the detailed handling, storage, and first aid information for a specific product lives. Every regulated jobsite using hazardous products is required to keep SDS documents accessible to workers, not just rely on the short label text. Knowing where that binder is kept before an incident happens is part of basic WHMIS competency.
  9. The correct answer is D (12 board feet, found using thickness times width times length divided by 12): the board foot formula is thickness in inches times width in inches times length in feet, divided by 12. Multiplying 2 by 8 by 9 gives 144, and dividing by 12 gives 12 board feet. Skipping the final division by 12, as shown in option C, produces a number four times too large.
  10. The correct answer is A (A guardrail system and a toe board to contain tools and debris): a scaffold with an open side above the height threshold needs guardrails to protect workers from falling and toe boards to stop tools and material from rolling off the edge onto anyone below. Warning tape and a single mid rail do not provide the passive physical barrier a proper guardrail system provides. With pedestrians below, containing falling material with a toe board is just as important as protecting the workers on the platform.
  11. The correct answer is B (Bump mode fires on every nose contact while the trigger is held, raising double-fire risk): sequential trip mode requires one full trigger pull and one contact per nail, while bump mode fires every time the nose touches material as long as the trigger stays depressed. That makes bump mode faster for repetitive nailing but carries a much higher risk of an unintended second nail firing on recoil or an accidental bump. Crews trade some of that built-in safety margin for speed whenever they switch modes.
  12. The correct answer is C (Remove the gloves, since loose material can pull a hand into the blade): gloves protect hands from splinters and chemical contact during general handling, but they come off around spinning equipment such as a table saw blade or a drill chuck. Loose glove fabric can catch on the blade or workpiece and pull the hand in before the worker can react. Bare hands actually give better feedback and control for guiding narrow stock past a spinning blade.
  13. The correct answer is A (Eliminating the hazard, such as doing the work from the floor below instead): the hierarchy of controls ranks elimination or substitution first, followed by physical barriers such as guardrails, with a personal fall arrest system as a lower option used when the first two are not practical. Doing the work from a completed floor or the ground instead of the exposed edge removes the fall hazard entirely rather than just managing it. A harness and lanyard only come into the plan once elimination and guardrails have genuinely been ruled out.
  14. The correct answer is D (A framing square, whose body and tongue meet at a 90 degree heel): the framing square has a long arm called the body, typically 24 inches, and a shorter arm called the tongue, typically 16 inches, meeting at a 90 degree corner. Beyond checking right angles at a wall corner, it also carries printed rafter tables and is used for laying out stair stringers. A combination square and chalk line serve other layout purposes but are not the tool built specifically around this 90 degree body-and-tongue geometry.
  15. The correct answer is B (Nobody stands or walks under a suspended load while it is being moved): basic rigging awareness requires that no one stand or walk beneath a suspended load, regardless of how the rigging looks or how briefly the load will be overhead. Rigging failures happen without warning, and the consequences of a falling truss bundle are severe. A signal person coordinates the lift using standardized hand signals from a position clear of the load path, not from underneath it.
  16. The correct answer is A (15 feet, since 9 squared plus 12 squared equals 15 squared): this is the 3-4-5 method scaled up by a factor of 3, giving legs of 9 and 12 feet. Checking the theorem, 9 squared is 81 and 12 squared is 144, and 81 plus 144 equals 225, which is 15 squared. A measured diagonal of exactly 15 feet between the two points confirms the corner is a true 90 degrees, since any other reading means the layout needs adjustment.
  17. The correct answer is D (A push stick, keeping the carpenter's hands well clear of the blade): a table saw requires a riving knife or splitter and blade guard to prevent kickback, and a push stick keeps hands clear on narrow rips where fingers would otherwise pass close to the spinning blade. This is especially important on a half inch wide strip, where there is very little material between the blade and the carpenter's hand. Gloves add risk near a spinning blade rather than removing it, so a push stick is the correct tool here.
  18. The correct answer is B (Treated lumber should never be burned, since burning concentrates its preservative chemicals in ash and smoke): pressure-treated lumber contains preservative chemicals that become concentrated and hazardous when the wood is burned, contaminating the smoke and the resulting ash. This makes burning unsafe regardless of the barrel's location or what other material is mixed in. Treated lumber offcuts need to go through regular waste disposal channels instead, never a site burn pile.
  19. The correct answer is A (A hard hat, to protect against tools or material that could fall from above): a hard hat protects against falling objects and is required anywhere overhead work is happening, which is exactly the situation described with a crew sheathing the roof directly above. Boots, dust masks, and high-visibility vests each address a different hazard, but none of them protect the head from a tool or piece of material dropped from the level above. The overhead activity is the specific trigger for hard hat use in this scenario.
  20. The correct answer is C (Maintaining three points of contact, since an armload of lumber occupies both hands): safe ladder climbing requires maintaining three points of contact, meaning two hands and a foot or two feet and a hand, at all times. Carrying an armload of lumber up the ladder ties up both hands, leaving the apprentice with nothing to grip the ladder if they lose balance. Material should be hauled up separately, such as with a rope and bucket or by having someone hand pieces up, rather than carried by hand while climbing.
  21. The correct answer is B (0.625 inches, the standard decimal equivalent of five eighths): five eighths of an inch converts to 0.625 in decimal form, one of the common fraction-to-decimal conversions a carpenter needs to recall instantly when reading a tape measure. Simply reading the fraction's digits as a decimal, as in option A, does not perform an actual conversion and gives a meaningless result. Confusing five eighths with three quarters or one half, as in options C and D, would shift a cut by a noticeable and costly amount.
  22. The correct answer is D (The energy source must be isolated, locked out, and tagged before work begins): lockout and tagout requires physically isolating the energy source, locking the isolating device so it cannot be reactivated, and tagging it to identify who performed the lockout and why. Simply switching the saw off and leaving it plugged in, as in option A, still allows the machine to be started accidentally by another person. A coworker holding a switch is not a substitute for a physical lock, since attention can lapse at any moment.
  23. The correct answer is C (An impact driver, which delivers rotational hammering force for long structural screws): an impact driver delivers rotational hammering force that drives long structural screws far faster than a standard drill, with far less risk of camming out the screw head under load. A drill still suits clean holes and smaller fasteners where torque control matters more than raw driving power. A reciprocating saw is built for cutting, not driving fasteners, and is not a substitute tool here.
  24. The correct answer is A (Use a hand truck or get a second person to help move the sheet): a sheet of 3/4 inch plywood is well within the range where a hand truck or a second person is the smarter choice over a solo lift, given the strain a full sheet places on the back and arms. Dragging material along the ground risks damaging both the plywood and whatever surface it crosses. Knowing your own limits and using help or equipment is part of preventing the back strains that are extremely common on construction sites.
  25. The correct answer is D (Roughly 3 metres, about 10 feet, though specific hazards can trigger it lower): while the exact trigger height is set by each province's own regulation, the widely used benchmark across Canadian construction is roughly 3 metres, or about 10 feet. Some jurisdictions and specific hazards, such as fixed ladders or work near an excavation, trigger the requirement at a lower height than that general benchmark. Fall protection planning should start well before a height simply looks dangerous, using this benchmark as a starting reference rather than a strict cutoff.
  26. The correct answer is B (Use mud sills under the base to spread the load on soft ground): a scaffold needs a stable, level base, and mud sills are used under the base on soft ground to spread the load and prevent the legs from sinking or shifting. Guardrails address fall protection at height, not the stability of the base itself. Driving legs directly into soft mud without a sill leaves the scaffold vulnerable to settling unevenly as the ground compresses under load.
  27. The correct answer is A (10 feet, since 8 squared plus 6 squared equals 10 squared): the run and rise of a roof form the two legs of a right triangle, with the rafter length as the hypotenuse. Squaring 8 gives 64 and squaring 6 gives 36, and 64 plus 36 equals 100, which is 10 squared. This is the same Pythagorean relationship behind the 3-4-5 squaring method, applied here to find the actual rafter length rather than to check a corner.
  28. The correct answer is C (Danger indicates a more severe hazard than the less severe hazard indicated by Warning): under WHMIS, a supplier label carries a signal word alongside the pictogram, hazard statement, and precautionary statement, and Danger is used for the more severe hazards while Warning is used for less severe ones. This distinction helps a worker quickly gauge relative risk before even reading the full hazard statement. The signal word is not tied to a specific hazard category like flammability, but to the severity level within whatever hazard the pictogram identifies.
  29. The correct answer is D (Safety glasses or a face shield, since the operation throws sparks and debris): safety glasses or a face shield are required for any operation that throws debris, and grinding is a clear example alongside cutting and nailing. Hearing protection and a dust mask may also be appropriate depending on conditions, but they do not address the specific eye injury risk from flying sparks and metal fragments. Eye protection is the piece of PPE most directly tied to this particular hazard.
  30. The correct answer is B (Kept off the trigger entirely until the nose is pressed against the work): safe pneumatic nailer handling requires the trigger finger to stay off the trigger until the nose of the tool is actually pressed against the workpiece. Resting a finger on the trigger while walking or carrying the tool risks an accidental discharge if the nose bumps into something or someone. This rule applies regardless of firing mode and is one of the most basic nailer safety habits a carpenter needs to build.
  31. The correct answer is C (A dull edge needs more force and is more likely to slip off the line): a sharp chisel is actually safer than a dull one, because a dull edge requires more applied force to cut and is more likely to slip off the intended line under that extra pressure. That slip is what causes injuries, not the sharpness of the edge itself. A sharp, properly maintained chisel cuts with controlled, predictable force and stays on the line the carpenter intends.
  32. The correct answer is A (The rigging is rated for the weight of the load being lifted): basic rigging awareness requires confirming the rigging is rated for the actual load before a lift begins, since using undersized or mismatched rigging can result in a sudden failure mid lift. Past successful use on a different lift does not confirm the rating is adequate for this specific load's weight. Colour and age of the equipment have no bearing on whether it can safely carry the panel bundle.
  33. The correct answer is B (10 board feet, found using thickness times width times length divided by 12): multiplying 1 inch thick by 12 inches wide by 10 feet long gives 120, and dividing by 12 gives 10 board feet. Option D shows the result before that final division, which is a common mistake that overstates the true board foot count by a factor of 12. One board foot equals a piece 1 inch thick, 12 inches wide, and 12 inches long, which is the reference this formula is built around.
  34. The correct answer is D (Air testing and a standby attendant, since it may qualify as a confined space): crawl spaces, mechanical pits, and some formwork cavities can qualify as confined spaces with restricted entry and a potential hazardous atmosphere, requiring air testing and a standby attendant before anyone enters. This need does not disappear just because the space is common in residential renovation work. Simply telling a coworker where someone is working does not provide the atmospheric testing or rescue readiness a genuine confined space entry requires.
  35. The correct answer is A (Refuse, since a scaffold should never be moved with a worker standing on it): a scaffold should never be moved with someone standing on it, unless it is a mobile tower specifically rated for that purpose with its wheels locked first. Repositioning a standard scaffold with a worker still on the platform risks a fall from an unstable, moving structure. The correct approach is to have everyone climb down before the scaffold is pushed into its new position.
  36. The correct answer is C (Refuse, since the guard should never be wedged or taped open): the lower blade guard should retract smoothly as the blade enters the material and spring back to cover it the instant the cut is finished, and it should never be wedged or taped open. A guard held open permanently leaves the spinning blade exposed at all times, removing the built-in protection the saw was designed with. Any catching issue should be addressed by inspecting and maintaining the guard, not by disabling it.
  37. The correct answer is D (Improve ventilation and wear the respiratory protection the SDS recommends for the task): engineered wood products off-gassing formaldehyde are a real WHMIS-relevant hazard for the installing carpenter, not just a future occupant concern, and the SDS exists precisely to guide the correct protective response. Reading the label and SDS before opening or installing the product tells the worker what PPE and ventilation the task actually needs. A single small window rarely provides adequate ventilation for sustained work in an enclosed space with this kind of off-gassing material.
  38. The correct answer is B (7.25 inches, the actual dressed width of a nominal 2x8 board): a nominal 2x8 actually measures 1.5 inches by 7.25 inches after milling removes material during the planing process. Using the nominal 8 inch figure instead of the actual 7.25 inch dimension would throw off every layout calculation built on this header's real size. Confusing a 2x8's actual width with a 2x6's actual width, as in option D, is exactly the kind of mix-up that costs marks and, on a real job, costs accuracy.
  39. The correct answer is A (Pivot the feet to turn the whole body instead of twisting the spine): proper lifting technique specifically warns against twisting while carrying a load, and the correct alternative is to pivot the feet so the whole body turns together. Twisting the spine under load, even briefly, is one of the most common causes of back strain on a jobsite. Holding a heavy load at arm's length, as in option D, actually increases the strain rather than protecting the back.
  40. The correct answer is C (Cover or guard the opening, since an unguarded hole is a fall hazard regardless of activity): an unguarded floor opening remains a fall hazard whether or not work is actively happening there, and it needs to be covered or guarded, such as with a secured cover rated for the load or a guardrail system around the opening. Caution tape and warning signs are not physical barriers and do not stop someone from stepping into the hole, especially in low light or when focused on another task. Passive protection like a rated cover addresses the hazard for everyone on site, not just workers who remember a sign.
  41. The correct answer is D (A fine-tooth blade, which reduces splintering when cutting plywood): blade selection changes with the material being cut, and a fine-tooth blade is specifically chosen for plywood to reduce splintering along the cut edge. A general combination blade works fine for rough framing lumber but leaves a rougher edge than plywood with a visible finish requires. Metal stud and cement board blades are built for entirely different materials and would not improve the plywood cut quality.
  42. The correct answer is A (A health hazard, such as a respiratory sensitizer, indicated by the starburst on the silhouette): the health hazard pictogram shows a starburst on a human silhouette and is used for products such as respiratory sensitizers, distinct from the flame, corrosion, or gas cylinder pictograms that indicate other hazard categories. Recognizing this specific pictogram tells the carpenter the product poses a longer-term or systemic health risk rather than an immediate physical hazard like fire. This distinction matters for choosing the right PPE and ventilation for the task.
  43. The correct answer is B (132 square feet, the room area plus a 10 percent waste allowance): the base area of the room is 10 feet times 12 feet, or 120 square feet, and 10 percent of that is 12 square feet. Adding that allowance to the base area gives a total order quantity of 132 square feet. Ordering the exact area with no allowance, as in option A, risks running short once cuts, mistakes, and pattern matching are accounted for.
  44. The correct answer is D (Close the gap, since scaffolds need full planking with no gaps a foot could go through): scaffold safety requires full planking with no gaps large enough for a foot to slip through, since a gap like this creates a real trip and fall hazard on the working platform. Marking the hazard with tape does not remove the risk of a foot going through the gap during normal movement. A guardrail addresses falls off the edge of the platform, not a gap within the platform itself.
  45. The correct answer is C (The air supply should be disconnected before clearing the jam): pneumatic nailer safety requires disconnecting the air supply before clearing a jam, since a tool still connected to compressed air can fire unexpectedly while fingers are near the nose. Simply pointing the tool away, as in option D, does not eliminate the risk to the carpenter's own hands during the clearing process. Holding the trigger down or reloading the magazine before the air is disconnected only increases the danger rather than reducing it.
  46. The correct answer is A (Using clear, standardized hand signals recognized by both the signal person and operator): a signal person coordinates directions with the equipment operator using clear, standardized hand signals so there is no ambiguity about stop, up, or down during a lift. Informal or improvised gestures create room for misinterpretation exactly when clarity matters most. Standardized signals work reliably even in loud site conditions where shouted instructions or a phone message would not be practical or safe.
  47. The correct answer is B (About 3.7 cubic yards, dividing the cubic footage by 27 cubic feet per yard): converting 6 inches to 0.5 feet and multiplying length times width times depth gives 20 times 10 times 0.5, or 100 cubic feet. Since one cubic yard equals 27 cubic feet, dividing 100 by 27 gives roughly 3.7 cubic yards. Treating cubic feet and cubic yards as the same unit, as in option C, would badly overstate the concrete order.
  48. The correct answer is D (Waterproof gloves and rubber boots rated for contact with wet concrete): wet concrete is caustic and can cause chemical burns with prolonged skin contact, so waterproof gloves and rubber boots rated for that exposure are the correct PPE choice for this task. Standard leather gloves and everyday boots are not designed to keep wet concrete off the skin over an extended pour and finish. Treating concrete as harmless until it cures, as in option B, ignores the real chemical hazard it poses while still wet.
  49. The correct answer is C (Guardrails passively protect anyone in the area without needing individual action): a guardrail system is a physical barrier that passively protects anyone in the area, which is a real advantage in a high-traffic location used by multiple trades throughout the day. A personal fall arrest system only protects the individual wearing it and correctly clipped in, and depends on that worker remembering to clip in every single time. For a shared walkway like this, the guardrail removes that dependence on repeated individual action.
  50. The correct answer is A (A reciprocating saw, built for demolition and rough cutting through mixed materials): a reciprocating saw handles demolition and rough cutting through mixed materials, embedded nails, or awkward angles that a circular saw or mitre saw cannot reach. A mitre saw and table saw are precision tools meant for clean, accurate cuts in a fixed setup, not rough demolition work. A finish nailer is a fastening tool entirely, with no cutting function at all.
  51. The correct answer is A (The product is corrosive and can burn skin, eyes, or damage material on contact): The WHMIS pictogram showing a hand and a surface being corroded represents the corrosive hazard class. Workers handling this product need gloves, eye protection, and should review the SDS for first aid steps before use. Recognizing this pictogram quickly prevents skin and eye exposure on the jobsite.
  52. The correct answer is D (Install a guardrail system around the opening as a passive physical barrier): The hierarchy of controls favours elimination or substitution first, then a passive physical barrier like a guardrail, before a personal fall arrest system is used. A guardrail protects everyone in the area without relying on any individual worker using equipment correctly. Personal fall arrest becomes the choice only when a guardrail is not practical for the task.
  53. The correct answer is B (4 feet from the wall, following the one-foot-out-per-four-feet-up ratio): The 4:1 rule sets the base out one foot of distance for every four feet of vertical height to the contact point, so 16 feet of height calls for a 4 foot base offset. A base set too close makes the ladder prone to tipping backward, while a base set too far out risks the ladder kicking out at the bottom. Following this ratio keeps the ladder at a safe working angle.
  54. The correct answer is C (It identifies who performed the lockout so nobody reactivates the machine unexpectedly): The tag communicates who locked the machine out and why, so a coworker does not flip the switch back on while someone's hand is inside the machine. The lock physically prevents reactivation while the tag communicates the reason to anyone else on site. Together they protect the worker performing the blade change from an unexpected startup.
  55. The correct answer is A (That air testing has occurred and a standby attendant is in place for entry): A confined space with restricted entry and potential hazardous atmosphere requires air testing before entry and a standby attendant while someone is inside. Skipping this step risks a worker being overcome by an unsafe atmosphere with no one aware or able to help. This applies to crawl spaces, mechanical pits, and similar cavities that meet the confined space definition.
  56. The correct answer is B (A framing square, since it carries printed rafter tables for common roof pitches): The framing square, also called a steel square, has rafter tables printed on its body that let a carpenter read common rafter lengths per foot of run directly off the tool. This is a technique built on heavily in roof framing work. The other tools listed serve different layout purposes and do not carry rafter length tables.
  57. The correct answer is D (A speed square, which holds position against the board edge and reads angles directly): A speed square's lipped fence hooks over the board edge to hold the tool steady while marking, and its printed degree scale lets a carpenter read an angle directly without extra calculation. This makes it faster for everyday marking than a framing square for this kind of task. The other tools listed are not designed for quick angle marking on a board edge.
  58.
