The correct answer is C (The unsupported middle of the board sagged and closed the kerf onto the blade): Kickback commonly happens when material closes on the blade as it is cut, which is more likely when a board is supported only at the ends rather than near the cut. The sagging middle pinches the blade in the kerf and the saw jumps back toward the operator. Supporting material properly near the cut reduces this risk significantly.
  59. The correct answer is B (A riving knife or splitter positioned directly behind the blade in the kerf): A table saw requires a riving knife or splitter, along with a blade guard, to prevent the cut pieces from closing back together and pinching the blade, which causes kickback. A push stick additionally keeps the operator's hands clear of the blade on narrow rips. This specific pinch hazard is not addressed by a mitre gauge, dado stack, or outfeed roller.
  60. The correct answer is A (Bump mode fires faster but carries a higher risk of an accidental double-fire): In sequential trip mode, the tool fires one nail per full trigger pull and contact with the work, while bump mode fires every time the nose contacts material while the trigger stays held. This makes bump mode faster for repetitive work but raises the risk of an unintended double-fire. Both modes still require the same basic trigger discipline and safe pointing rules.
  61. The correct answer is C (The product presents a more severe hazard than one labelled with the word Warning): WHMIS supplier labels use the signal word Danger for more severe hazards and Warning for less severe ones. This lets a worker judge relative severity at a glance before opening the container or reading the full SDS. The signal word is one part of a label that also includes the hazard statement and precautionary statement.
  62. The correct answer is D (16 board feet, calculated as 2 times 8 times 12, divided by 12): The board foot formula is thickness in inches times width in inches times length in feet, divided by 12. Multiplying 2 by 8 by 12 gives 192, and dividing 192 by 12 gives exactly 16 board feet. This formula is the standard way rough lumber is priced and estimated in quantity.
  63. The correct answer is A (9.25 feet, from 9 plus 3 divided by 12 equalling 0.25): Converting a feet-inch measurement to decimal feet means dividing the inch portion by 12 and adding it to the whole feet. Three divided by 12 equals 0.25, so 9 feet 3 inches becomes 9.25 feet. This conversion is useful for takeoffs and calculator-based estimating work.
  64. The correct answer is B (8 and 1/4 inches, from 3/8 plus 7/8 equalling 10/8, which carries a whole inch): Adding 3/8 and 7/8 gives 10/8, which simplifies to 1 and 2/8, or 1 and 1/4. Adding that to the whole numbers, 5 plus 2 plus 1, gives 8, plus the remaining 1/4, for a total of 8 and 1/4 inches. Carrying the extra whole inch forward correctly is the step that trips up a rushed calculation.
  65. The correct answer is D (7.25 inches, the standard dressed width for nominal 2x8 lumber): Rough lumber is cut to the nominal size, then planed smooth on all four sides, which removes material so the actual size is smaller than the name suggests. A nominal 2x8 measures 1.5 by 7.25 inches once dressed. Every layout calculation, including header sizing, must use this actual dressed dimension rather than the nominal number.
  66. The correct answer is C (Before the tool starts, since cumulative hearing loss builds up over years unnoticed): Hearing protection matters more than most apprentices expect, because tools like a circular saw or framing nailer running for hours cause cumulative hearing loss that does not show up until years later. Plugs or muffs need to go on before the tool starts, not after ringing begins. Waiting for symptoms means the damage has already started.
  67. The correct answer is A (Bend at the knees, keep the back straight, and pivot the feet instead of twisting): Proper lifting technique keeps the load close to the body, bends at the knees rather than the waist, keeps the back straight, and avoids twisting by pivoting the feet. This directly addresses back strain, one of the most common manual material handling injuries on construction sites. A heavy beam segment is also a case where getting help or using equipment is often the smarter choice.
  68. The correct answer is B (They coordinate directions with the equipment operator using standardized hand signals): A signal person coordinates directions with the equipment operator using clear, standardized hand signals so there is no ambiguity about stop, up, or down. Nobody should stand or walk under a suspended load at any point during the lift. This coordination is a core part of basic rigging awareness whenever panels, trusses, or beams are lifted by crane.
  69. The correct answer is C (Burning concentrates the preservative chemicals in the resulting ash and smoke): Treated lumber should never be burned on site because burning concentrates its preservative chemicals in the ash and smoke, creating a real inhalation and contact hazard. Offcuts should be disposed of through regular waste channels instead. This is a specific hazard distinct from ordinary wood waste.
  70. The correct answer is D (A fine-tooth blade, which reduces splintering when cutting plywood sheet goods): Blade selection changes with the material, and a fine-tooth blade is specifically suited to plywood because it reduces splintering along the cut edge compared to a general combination blade. A combination blade is better suited to general framing lumber, while specialty blades handle metal studs or cement board. Choosing the right blade protects the finished surface quality of the panel.
  71. The correct answer is B (Tools can roll or fall through the gap and strike someone working below): Scaffolds need full planking with no gaps a foot could go through, and toe boards specifically exist to stop tools from rolling off onto someone below. A gap in the planking combined with missing toe boards creates a direct falling-object hazard. Guardrails address a different hazard, workers falling from an open side.
  72. The correct answer is A (A laser level projects a level reference across the room, saving time on the transfer): A laser level projects a level or plumb reference across a room, saving time transferring elevations or marks between multiple points compared to repeatedly checking with a spirit level. A carpenter still needs basic levelling knowledge to catch a laser bumped out of calibration, so that knowledge is not eliminated. A spirit level remains standard equipment for smaller, close-range checks.
  73. The correct answer is D (The chisel is likely dull, and a dull edge slips more easily under the extra force): A sharp chisel is safer than a dull one because a dull edge needs more force to cut and is more likely to slip off the line, increasing injury risk. Needing unusually high pressure is a sign the edge needs sharpening before continuing. This applies whether the chisel is used with a mallet or by hand pressure alone.
  74. The correct answer is C (A box nail, which has a thinner shank than a common nail and resists splitting): Box nails have a thinner shank than common nails, making them the better choice for material prone to splitting, such as thin or brittle trim stock. Common nails are the general framing choice, while galvanized and ring-shank nails address corrosion resistance and withdrawal resistance instead. Matching the fastener to the material's splitting tendency prevents wasted material and rework.
  75. The correct answer is A (An impact driver, which delivers rotational hammering force for long structural screws): An impact driver delivers rotational hammering force that drives long structural screws faster and with less risk of camming out the screw head compared to a standard drill. A drill remains better suited to clean holes and smaller fasteners where torque control matters more than raw driving speed. Neither a reciprocating saw nor a nailer is designed for driving screws.
  76. The correct answer is B (The Safety Data Sheet, kept in a binder accessible to workers on site): WHMIS ties together labels, Safety Data Sheets with detailed handling and first aid information, and worker training. Knowing where the SDS binder is kept on site lets a worker find detailed first aid guidance beyond what fits on a product label. This access is a real safety requirement, not paperwork for its own sake.
  77. The correct answer is C (A reciprocating saw, built to handle demolition and cutting through mixed materials): A reciprocating saw handles demolition and rough cutting through mixed materials, embedded nails, or awkward angles that a circular saw or a stationary mitre saw cannot reach. A mitre saw and table saw are both stationary tools designed for accurate, controlled cuts on prepared material rather than rough demolition work. Choosing the tool suited to the actual task avoids damaging the equipment or the worker.
  78. The correct answer is D (252 square feet, from multiplying 14 feet by 18 feet directly): Area calculations for flooring or sheathing use length times width, so 14 feet multiplied by 18 feet equals 252 square feet. This raw area figure is then used alongside a sheet's coverage area to estimate how many sheets to order. A waste factor for cutting and job-site damage is typically added on top of this base calculation.
  79. The correct answer is B (The product is flammable and must be kept away from ignition sources): The flame pictogram inside the red diamond border specifically identifies a flammable product hazard. In a small, poorly ventilated space, this makes ventilation and eliminating ignition sources especially important while working. Recognizing this pictogram before opening the container is part of using WHMIS as intended.
  80. The correct answer is A (Raise the concern with the site supervisor or the trades involved before framing): Communicating clearly and raising a concern early, such as a dimension conflict between drawings, prevents the kind of conflict that costs everyone time later. Framing a wall before confirming a mechanical chase location risks costly rework. Raising the issue is part of the job, not an inconvenience to the schedule.
  81. The correct answer is D (A field level hazard assessment, a real legal requirement on most sites, not a formality): Signing off on a daily hazard assessment, sometimes called a field level hazard assessment, is a real legal requirement on most commercial and many residential sites. It covers the specific hazards a worker expects to face that day for their task. Treating it as a formality to breeze through misses its actual purpose.
  82. The correct answer is C (A spirit level, which checks both level and plumb using its bubble vials): A spirit level checks level, meaning horizontal, and plumb, meaning vertical, using bubble vials built into the tool. A door jamb needs both conditions confirmed before final fastening so the door operates correctly. A chalk line, plumb bob, and tape measure each serve a narrower, different layout purpose.
  83. The correct answer is B (40 cubic feet, from multiplying 20 by 2 by 1 directly): Volume calculations for concrete quantities use length times width times depth, so 20 feet times 2 feet times 1 foot equals 40 cubic feet. This raw cubic foot figure would then typically be converted to cubic yards by dividing by 27, since concrete is priced and ordered by the yard. Getting this base multiplication right avoids over or under-ordering material.
  84. The correct answer is A (The corner is not square and one of the lines needs to be adjusted): A true 90 degree corner produces a diagonal of exactly 5 feet when the legs measure 3 feet and 4 feet, since 3 squared plus 4 squared equals 5 squared. A diagonal reading longer than 5 feet means the corner is racked out of square, and a line needs adjusting until the diagonal reads exactly 5 feet. This is a direct, practical application of the Pythagorean theorem.
  85. The correct answer is C (Set the blade depth to just slightly more than the material thickness): Setting blade depth to just slightly more than the material thickness, along with supporting material properly and using a firm two-handed grip, reduces kickback risk. A blade set unnecessarily deep exposes more blade below the material than needed for a clean cut. This is a simple adjustment that meaningfully changes the saw's safety margin.
  86. The correct answer is D (Gloves should come off around spinning equipment, since loose material can pull a hand in): Gloves protect hands from splinters and chemical contact, but they come off around spinning equipment like a table saw blade or drill chuck, because loose glove material can be caught and pull a hand into the machine. This is an exception to otherwise routine glove use on site. Matching PPE to the specific hazard of each task matters as much as wearing PPE at all.
  87. The correct answer is A (The strings can be removed and replaced during excavation without losing the reference): Because batter boards sit back from the real corners, the strings can be taken down and reattached repeatedly during excavation without losing the building line reference. A plumb bob dropped from the string intersection re-establishes the exact corner point whenever needed. This setup protects the layout work from being disturbed by excavation equipment.
  88. The correct answer is B (They allow the exact corner to be quickly re-established if original stakes are disturbed): Offset stakes are set at a known, fixed distance from the actual building line so the exact corner can be quickly re-established with a tape measure at any point during excavation, even after original stakes and strings are disturbed by equipment. This protects the layout work already completed. They serve a layout function distinct from fencing, utilities, or benchmark elevation.
  89. The correct answer is D (The difference in elevation between the two points, which is 0.7 feet here): Differential levelling finds the difference in elevation between two points using a level instrument and a graduated rod, and subtracting one rod reading from the other gives that elevation difference. Here, 4.2 feet minus 3.5 feet equals 0.7 feet of elevation difference between the two points. This is how a carpenter confirms a footing form depth or foundation level around a building.
  90. The correct answer is C (The product's SDS, which provides handling information for this specific hazard): Engineered wood products off-gassing formaldehyde is one of the WHMIS-relevant hazards a carpenter runs into regularly, alongside solvent adhesives and spray foam. The SDS carries detailed handling information relevant to this kind of exposure risk. Checking it before working with the product in an unventilated space is a practical safety step, not paperwork.
  91. The correct answer is B (A hard hat, required anywhere overhead work creates a risk of falling objects): A hard hat protects against falling objects and is required anywhere overhead work is happening, which directly applies here since trusses are being installed above the ground-floor worker. The other PPE options address different hazards not specifically created by this overhead work scenario. Matching PPE to the actual hazard present is the underlying principle being tested.
  92. The correct answer is D (A chalk line, which coats a string in chalk powder and snaps it against the surface): A chalk line snaps a long, straight reference line across a floor or wall plate by coating a retractable string in chalk powder and snapping it against the surface. This makes it the practical tool for marking a long straight line quickly, compared to the shorter or single-point references the other tools provide. It is a standard step before laying out wall positions on a subfloor.
  93. The correct answer is A (Roughly 3 metres, about 10 feet, though some hazards trigger it at a lower height): The widely used benchmark across Canadian construction is roughly 3 metres, about 10 feet, though the exact trigger height is set by each province's regulation and some jurisdictions or specific hazards, like fixed ladders, trigger it lower. Fall protection planning should start well below anything that simply looks dangerous. This benchmark helps a carpenter recognize when planning is required.
  94. The correct answer is C (Use mud sills under the scaffold legs to create a stable, level base on soft ground): A scaffold needs a stable, level base, sometimes requiring mud sills on soft ground, before it is safe to work from. Skipping this step on visibly soft or uneven ground risks the scaffold settling unevenly under load. This is a setup step distinct from planking, guardrails, or toe boards, which address different hazards.
  95. The correct answer is B (Measuring both full diagonals corner to corner and confirming they are exactly equal): After all four corners are individually squared, the whole rectangle gets a final check using the diagonal measurement method, where both full diagonals should be exactly equal on a true rectangle. If one diagonal is longer, the whole layout is out of square even if each corner looked close individually, because the error spreads across the shape. This final check catches errors the individual corner checks alone can miss.
  96. The correct answer is B (A waste factor of 5 to 10 percent covers cutting around openings and job-site damage): Sheet goods are calculated by dividing the total area by a single sheet's area, then adding a waste factor, typically 5 to 10 percent, for cutting around openings and job-site damage. This is a separate step from the raw area division and is not automatically built into it. Skipping the waste factor risks running short of material mid-job.
  97. The correct answer is A (Planning cuts and ordering material lengths that divide efficiently into needed pieces): Minimizing offcuts through better cut planning, such as ordering material lengths that divide efficiently into the pieces actually needed, reduces waste rather than just sorting it after the fact. This good material planning saves money for the contractor and reduces landfill waste. It reflects the kind of practical judgment the exam tests across every domain, not just a fact to memorize.
  98. The correct answer is C (A combination square, which can also work as a depth gauge for repeated marks): A combination square checks 90 and 45 degree angles, sets consistent marking depths, and works as a depth gauge or straightedge. This makes it well suited to marking the same shallow groove depth repeatedly across multiple identical panels. The other tools listed are built for different layout tasks and are not designed as adjustable depth gauges.
  99. The correct answer is B (20 feet, since one inch on paper equals 4 feet in real life at this scale): At 1/4 inch equals 1 foot, one inch on paper equals 4 feet in real life, so a 5 inch measurement on the page represents 5 times 4, or 20 feet, on the actual building. This conversion is especially useful when a photocopied or reduced print throws off a scale ruler reading. Knowing the conversion by hand protects against relying on a distorted printed scale.
  100. The correct answer is D (Flag the discrepancy to the site supervisor before building it into the framing): A good habit is adding up every individual dimension in a string and checking it against the overall dimension shown for that run, and when the numbers do not match, that gets flagged to the site supervisor before it is built into the framing. Catching a dimension error on paper costs nothing, while catching it after the wall is framed costs labour and material. Guessing which number is correct risks building the error into the structure.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Footing sizes, foundation wall thickness, pier locations, and anchor bolt spacing details): A foundation plan shows footing sizes, foundation wall thickness, pier locations, and anchor bolt spacing, along with any foundation-level framing like beam pockets. Room layouts belong to the floor plan, property boundaries belong to the site plan, and roof pitch belongs to the elevations.
  2. The correct answer is D (A benchmark, a fixed reference point of known elevation on site): A benchmark is a fixed reference point of known elevation, such as a protected stake, a permanent survey marker, or a fire hydrant, that every other elevation on the job is measured from. Batter boards and offset stakes are set later using the benchmark, not the other way around.
  3. The correct answer is A (A door, with the arc showing the direction the door swings open): A door is drawn as a line for the door leaf with a quarter circle arc showing the swing direction, which is exactly what the diagram shows at symbol 1. A window is instead shown only as a break in the wall line, with no arc at all.
  4. The correct answer is C (Exactly 5 feet, the calculated hypotenuse of a 3-4-5 triangle): Since 3 squared plus 4 squared equals 25, and the square root of 25 is 5, a triangle with 3 and 4 foot legs has a true right angle only when the diagonal measures exactly 5 feet. Any other diagonal reading means the corner is out of square and needs adjustment.
  5. The correct answer is D (The corner is slightly out of square and one building line must be adjusted): A 6-8-10 triangle should produce a diagonal of exactly 10 feet when the corner is truly square, so a reading of 10 feet 2 inches means the angle is greater than 90 degrees. The string line is swung inward until the diagonal reads exactly 10 feet 0 inches.
  6. The correct answer is B (20 feet, found by multiplying the paper measurement by the scale factor): At 1/4 inch equals 1 foot 0 inches, one inch on paper represents 4 feet on the actual building, so 5 inches on the page represents 5 times 4, or 20 feet. This conversion works by hand even on a reduced-size photocopy where a scale ruler reading would be unreliable.
  7. The correct answer is B (Flag the discrepancy to the site supervisor before any framing is built to it): A mismatch between a dimension string's individual figures and its overall callout means something on the drawing is wrong, and the correct move is to flag it to the site supervisor before it gets built into the framing. Catching the error on paper costs nothing compared to catching it after the wall is framed.
  8. The correct answer is C (24 feet, matching the dimension labeled along the left edge shown): The dimension line running along the left edge of the floor plan diagram labels the room's length directly, which is shown as 24 feet. Reading this dimension off the diagram before cutting the rear wall avoids relying on a guessed or remembered figure.
  9. The correct answer is A (Batter boards, set back outside each corner at a consistent elevation): Batter boards are the horizontal boards set back outside each corner at a consistent elevation, usually the height of the top of the foundation, with strings pulled between them to mark the actual building lines. Setting them back from the real corners lets strings be removed and restrung during excavation without losing the reference.
  10. The correct answer is D (A plumb bob, dropped from the string intersection to the ground): A plumb bob dropped from the intersection of two strings re-establishes the exact corner point on the ground below, which is why batter boards are set back rather than placed at the actual corners. This lets the strings be taken down and restrung repeatedly during excavation without losing the layout.
  11. The correct answer is C (Perform levelling work around the site that used to require two people): A rotating laser level projects a full 360 degree level plane that a receiver on a grade rod can pick up anywhere around the site, letting one person read elevations that used to need a second person sighting through an optical instrument. This is especially useful outdoors over long distances.
  12. The correct answer is A (2.15 feet higher, the difference between the two rod readings taken): Subtracting the second reading from the benchmark reading gives 4.25 minus 2.10, which equals 2.15 feet. Because a lower rod reading means the ground at that point sits closer to the instrument's level line, the second point is 2.15 feet higher than the benchmark.
  13. The correct answer is B (The direction the door swings open, away from its frame): The quarter circle arc drawn from a door's leaf line shows the swing direction, telling a reader which way the door clears the opening when it is opened. It does not indicate hinge side, plumb condition, or rough opening height, which come from other drawing information.
  14. The correct answer is C (It affects drainage away from the building and keeps framing away from moisture): The finished floor elevation controls the sill height above finished grade, which is what allows water to drain away from the building and keeps wood framing away from ground moisture. Every subsequent foundation and framing elevation on the job gets checked against this one number.
  15. The correct answer is A (Setbacks, the minimum distances required from the property lines by bylaw): Setbacks are the minimum distances a structure must be kept from the front, rear, and side property lines under the municipal zoning bylaw, and the site plan shows these distances so the building is not placed in violation of them. They are separate from the building code's own technical requirements.
  16. The correct answer is A (It is likely load-bearing or exterior, confirmed against the structural drawings): A heavy or hatched double line typically indicates a load-bearing or exterior wall, while thinner unhatched lines usually mark interior partitions, but line weight alone should never be the final word. The structural drawings are always the safest confirmation of which walls actually carry load.
  17. The correct answer is C (A north arrow, orienting the building relative to true or plan north): A north arrow appears somewhere on the site and floor plans to orient the building on the lot, letting a carpenter confirm which direction the structure faces relative to the street and property lines. Detail callouts and door swing arcs serve entirely different purposes on the drawing set.
  18. The correct answer is C (10 feet, matching the width dimension labeled along the bottom edge): The dimension line running along the bottom edge of the floor plan diagram labels the room's overall width as 10 feet, which is the length the top plate for that wall needs to match. Cutting to this dimensioned figure keeps the plate consistent with the drawn layout.
  19. The correct answer is A (Look up the referenced detail sheet for blocking and precise dimensions): A split circle or hexagon with a number and sheet reference is a detail callout, telling the reader where to find the enlarged detail drawing that explains that specific condition. That detail sheet shows blocking, headers, and dimensions the floor plan never had room for at its own scale.
  20. The correct answer is A (The specifications, describing material grades and required installation standards): Specifications, often called the specs, are a separate written document accompanying the drawings that spells out material grades, installation standards, and performance requirements, such as nailing patterns, that are impractical to write directly on a drawing. Where the drawings show what and where, the specs describe how and to what standard.
  21. The correct answer is D (A change order, a documented and approved change made after construction started): A change order is a change made after construction has started, and it needs to be documented and approved before the work proceeds, protecting both the tradesperson and the project's schedule and budget. An addendum, by contrast, is an official change issued before a contract is even signed.
  22. The correct answer is A (25 studs, found by converting to inches, dividing by spacing, and adding one): Converting 32 feet to inches gives 384 inches, and dividing by the 16 inch spacing gives 24; adding one for the starting stud brings the count to 25. In practice a few more studs are still added for the far corner and any partition tie-in before the order is finalized.
  23. The correct answer is B (A window, drawn only as a straight break in the wall line): A window is drawn as a break in the wall line with additional frame lines but no leaf and no swing arc, which matches the straight break line shown at symbol 1. A door would instead show a leaf line paired with a quarter circle swing arc.
  24. The correct answer is D (120 linear feet, matching three times the wall's linear footage total): A wall with one bottom plate and a double top plate needs roughly three times the wall's linear footage in plate stock, so 3 times 40 feet equals 120 linear feet. An allowance for waste and splices is added to this base figure before the material is actually ordered.
  25. The correct answer is B (11 sheets, the base coverage figure increased by the 10 percent waste factor): Dividing the 320 square foot area by the 32 square foot coverage of a single sheet gives 10 sheets before waste. Adding the 10 percent waste factor brings the order up to 11 sheets, covering cuts around openings and any job-site damage.
  26. The correct answer is C (12 board feet, found from thickness times width times length divided by 12): Multiplying thickness times width times length gives 2 times 6 times 12, which equals 144, and dividing by 12 gives 12 board feet. This same formula applies to any piece of solid dimension lumber regardless of its actual size.
  27. The correct answer is A (2 cubic yards, the cubic foot volume divided by 27 to convert): Converting 6 inches to 0.5 feet and multiplying 54 by 2 by 0.5 gives 54 cubic feet. Dividing 54 by 27 gives exactly 2 cubic yards, which is the quantity that should be ordered since concrete is priced and delivered by the yard.
  28. The correct answer is B (13 feet, matching the width dimension labeled along the bottom edge): The dimension line along the bottom edge of the floor plan diagram labels the room's overall width as 13 feet, regardless of how many door symbols sit along that wall. Reading the dimensioned figure directly avoids estimating the wall length from the drawing scale alone.
  29. The correct answer is C (It reduces waste by grouping similar cuts instead of cutting one piece per board): A cutting list plans how to get needed pieces out of ordered stock with minimal waste, grouping similar-length cuts from the same piece wherever possible rather than cutting one piece per board and leaving unusable offcuts. It is a separate planning step from the takeoff itself.
  30. The correct answer is C (The project faces stop-work orders, fines, and possibly reopening finished work): A building permit is the mechanism by which the authority having jurisdiction confirms a proposed structure meets the applicable code, zoning bylaws, and safety requirements before construction begins. Building without one exposes the owner and contractor to stop-work orders, fines, and the risk of opening finished work back up for inspection.
  31. The correct answer is A (A footing inspection confirming size, depth, and reinforcement against the drawings): A footing inspection typically happens after the forms and reinforcing steel are in place but before concrete is poured, confirming size, depth, and reinforcement against the approved drawings. A framing inspection happens much later, after the structural framing itself is complete.
  32. The correct answer is B (A framing inspection, since this is the last point framing is fully visible): A framing inspection happens after the structural framing, sheathing, and rough-in work are complete but before the walls are closed in, since this is the last point the framing itself is fully visible. Closing the walls before this inspection makes the framing impossible to verify afterward.
  33. The correct answer is C (11 feet, matching the length dimension labeled along the left edge): The dimension line along the left edge of the floor plan diagram labels the room's length as 11 feet, which combines with the width dimension along the bottom edge to calculate the perimeter. Both dimensioned figures are needed together for an accurate baseboard material order.
  34. The correct answer is C (Each province adopts the model code into law with its own local amendments): The National Building Code of Canada is a model code, and each province and territory adopts it into law with its own amendments and, in some cases, its own separate provincial code built on the same foundation. A detail acceptable in one province may need to be built differently in another because of a local amendment.
  35. The correct answer is A (Any on-site change to the approved set should be documented and possibly resubmitted): Crews build from the approved, stamped set of drawings reviewed and authorized by the permitting authority, and any changes made on site that were not part of that set need to be documented and, if significant, resubmitted as a revision. An undocumented verbal change puts the project at risk during inspection.
  36. The correct answer is C (An occupancy classification, assigned by the building code based on building use): Occupancy classification is a broad category the building code assigns based on how a building will be used, residential, business, mercantile, and so on, and it directly affects which fire separation, exiting, and structural loading requirements apply. It is assigned by the code, not by the zoning bylaw.
  37. The correct answer is C (0.667, found by dividing the 8 inch rise by the 12 inch run): Dividing the 8 inch rise by the 12 inch run gives 8 divided by 12, which is approximately 0.667. This decimal slope is the foundation for later rafter length and angle calculations that build directly on the pitch notation shown on the elevation drawings.
  38. The correct answer is D (A window opening, drawn only as a break in the wall line): A window is drawn only as a straight break line across the wall with frame lines, since it has no leaf and no swing direction to show. A pocket door or bifold closet door would still be drawn with some leaf indication, unlike the plain break shown here.
  39. The correct answer is A (20 feet, the square root of 16 squared plus 12 squared added together): Squaring the two legs gives 16 squared plus 12 squared, which is 256 plus 144, or 400, and the square root of 400 is exactly 20. A diagonal reading other than 20 feet means the deck frame is out of square and needs adjustment before decking goes down.
  40. The correct answer is D (The overall layout is still out of square and needs adjustment before forms go in): On a true rectangle, both diagonals must be exactly equal in length, so a difference between the two readings means the whole layout is out of square even though each corner looked close individually. The error spreads across the shape and needs to be corrected before the foundation forms are set.
  41. The correct answer is D (A small measuring error has less effect on the calculated angle with longer legs): Any multiple of the 3-4-5 ratio always produces a true right angle because the ratio between the legs and the hypotenuse never changes. Accuracy improves with a larger triangle because a small measuring error has proportionally less effect on the calculated angle when the legs are longer.
  42. The correct answer is B (The site plan, showing the structure's location relative to the street and lot): The site plan shows where the building sits on the lot: property lines, setbacks, the structure's location relative to the street, existing grade contours, and utility connections. The foundation plan and floor plan instead describe the structure itself rather than its placement on the lot.
  43. The correct answer is D (9 feet, matching the width dimension labeled along the bottom edge): The dimension line along the bottom edge of the floor plan diagram labels the room's overall width as 9 feet, which is the linear length the wall's plate order needs to be based on before applying the standard three-times multiplier for a bottom plate and double top plate.
  44. The correct answer is D (An elevation drawing, showing the exterior face straight on from outside): Elevations show the exterior faces as if you were standing outside looking straight at each wall, giving roof pitch, window and door heights, siding materials, and finished grade lines. A section instead shows a vertical cut through the building's assembly rather than its exterior face.
  45. The correct answer is B (A section drawing, a vertical cut showing wall and floor assembly depths): Sections are vertical cuts through the building showing how the assembly goes together from footing to ridge, and are where a carpenter finds wall heights, floor assembly depths, and insulation details. A floor plan or elevation alone does not carry this level of vertical assembly information.
  46. The correct answer is B (A schedule, a table listing every unit by its code, size, and type): Schedules are tables, usually for doors and windows, listing every unit by a code such as D1 or W3 with its size, type, and hardware. Specifications instead describe material grades and installation standards in narrative form rather than as a per-unit table.
  47. The correct answer is D (36 feet, found by multiplying the paper measurement by the scale factor): At 1/8 inch equals 1 foot 0 inches, one inch on paper represents 8 feet in real life, so 4.5 inches on the page represents 4.5 times 8, or 36 feet. Calculating this by hand avoids the error an architect's scale ruler would introduce on a reduced-size print.
  48. The correct answer is B (Symbol 2, because it has no leaf line and no swing arc at all): A window is drawn as a plain break in the wall line with no leaf and no swing arc, which matches symbol 2 exactly. Symbol 1's leaf line and quarter circle arc are the standard symbol for a door, not a window.
  49. The correct answer is D (The corner is slightly out of square and one building line must be adjusted): A true 9-12-15 triangle produces a diagonal of exactly 15 feet when the corner is square, so a reading of 15 feet 3 inches means the angle is greater than 90 degrees. One building line needs to be adjusted until the diagonal reads exactly 15 feet before the layout is accepted.
  50. The correct answer is B (Counted individually and separately, tied to the header schedule for that opening): King studs, jack studs, and cripples are counted separately from the header schedule rather than folded into the basic on-centre spacing formula used for regular field studs. Each opening is worked out individually so the header and trimmer requirements match the actual rough opening size.
  51. The correct answer is B (Exactly 5 feet measured between the two marks): the 3-4-5 method applies the Pythagorean theorem directly, since 3 squared plus 4 squared equals 25 and the square root of 25 is 5. Any reading longer or shorter than 5 feet means the corner is out of square and one building line needs adjusting. The same ratio holds true for any multiple, such as 6-8-10 or 9-12-15.
  52. The correct answer is D (A door, shown by a leaf line and a swing arc): doors are drawn as a line representing the door leaf with a quarter circle arc showing which way the door swings when open. A window would instead appear as a break in the wall line with added frame lines and no swing arc. Reading the swing direction correctly matters for clearance and hardware planning before framing the rough opening.
  53. The correct answer is A (2x10 S-P-F at 16 inches on centre, listed with a 15'-8" maximum span): the table shows S-P-F 2x10 joists at 16 inch spacing rated for 15 feet 8 inches, which covers the 15 foot clear span, while the 2x8 at the same spacing only reaches 12 feet 6 inches. Wider spacing at 24 inches on centre reduces the same size joist's rated span to 13 feet 5 inches, so spacing matters. Douglas Fir-Larch would also work but is not the minimum size needed since S-P-F already satisfies the span.
  54. Wall thickness and stud spacing are shown elsewhere on the plan or in the specs, not at the window callout itself. Height for the same unit would be found in the window schedule.
  55. Symbol 1 in the bottom wall is the door, identifiable by its leaf line and quarter circle swing arc. Distinguishing the two symbols correctly matters before framing the rough openings for each unit.
  56. The correct answer is D (Slightly greater than 90 degrees, requiring the string to swing inward): a true right angle produces a diagonal of exactly 10 feet 0 inches for the 6-8-10 ratio, so a longer reading of 10 feet 2 inches means the angle has opened up past square. The string line is swung inward until the diagonal reads exactly 10 feet. Leaving the extra 2 inches uncorrected would carry the error into every wall built on that corner.
  57. This final check catches an error that corner-by-corner squaring alone can miss. Forms should not be poured until both diagonals agree exactly.
  58. The correct answer is A (The direction the door swings when it is opened): the quarter circle arc on a door symbol shows which way the door leaf clears when opened, telling the framer and later trades which side needs clearance. Door height and hardware details are found in the door schedule, not the swing arc itself. Fire ratings for a wall assembly come from the specs or structural drawings, not the door symbol.
  59. The correct answer is A (It serves as the fixed reference elevation every other elevation on the job is measured from): elevations are transferred around the site from the benchmark using a builder's level or laser level, and every footing depth, foundation height, and finished floor elevation is checked against it. Losing track of the benchmark means every elevation on the job loses its reference. It is why the point gets driven and protected before excavation starts.
  60. The correct answer is D (So the strings can be removed and replaced during excavation without losing the reference): because the boards sit outside the actual excavation area, the strings can come down and go back up repeatedly as work proceeds, and a plumb bob dropped from the string intersection re-establishes the exact corner point every time. Placing them right at the corners would mean losing the reference the moment digging starts. Offset stakes serve the same purpose later in the job.
  61. The correct answer is C (It beeps or shows an indicator when centred on the level beam): the rotating head projects a 360 degree level plane, and the receiver signals when it is centred on that plane so the rod reading at that point gives the elevation. This lets one person do levelling work that used to require two people with an optical instrument. It also works outdoors over long distances where a hand level is not practical.
  62. The correct answer is B (The rough opening width required for the window unit shown): a dimension called out directly beside a window symbol on a floor plan almost always gives the rough opening size the framer must build to, not an unrelated distance. Ceiling height and corner offsets are dimensioned separately elsewhere on the drawing set. Confirming the schedule against this callout avoids ordering the wrong size unit.
  63. The correct answer is A (It sets the sill height needed above finished grade for proper drainage and moisture separation): the finished floor elevation ties directly to keeping wood framing away from ground moisture and to drainage away from the building, and every later foundation and framing elevation gets checked against it. Getting it wrong early carries the error through the whole structure. It is also referenced against the elevation of a neighbouring building or the street.
  64. The correct answer is D (The difference in elevation between point one and point two): differential levelling works by subtracting one rod reading from the other, since both readings are taken along the same level line of sight, so the difference is purely a vertical elevation change. This is how a carpenter confirms a footing form is at the correct depth or that a foundation wall is level all the way around. Rod readings alone say nothing about horizontal distance.
  65. The correct answer is C (A window unit sized to the 40 inch dimension shown): a window is drawn as a break in the wall line with frame lines added and no swing arc, distinguishing it from a door symbol. The 40 inch figure at that break gives the rough opening width for the unit. A structural post or closet opening would be called out with its own distinct symbol and label.
  66. The correct answer is B (Setbacks, which are typically shown on the site plan): setbacks are the minimum distances a structure must be kept from the property lines under the municipal zoning bylaw, and the site plan shows these distances along with the building's location relative to the lot and street. Building layout has to respect these limits before batter boards or offset stakes go in. Ignoring them can force a structure to be relocated or rebuilt after the fact.
  67. The correct answer is D (12 feet, since 1 inch on paper equals 4 feet on the building): at a scale of 1/4 inch equals 1 foot 0 inches, 1 inch on paper equals 4 feet on the actual building, so a 3 inch measurement represents 3 times 4, or 12 feet. Knowing this hand conversion matters because a photocopied or reduced-size print throws off a scale ruler reading even though the printed dimension text would not. Multiplying by an unrelated figure like the page width does not follow the actual scale relationship at all.
  68. The correct answer is A (A door, identified by its leaf line and swing arc): doors are drawn as a leaf line with a quarter circle arc showing which way the door swings when opened, which is exactly what symbol 1 shows.   69. Catching a dimension error on paper costs nothing, but framing it in first costs labour and material to correct. Guessing which number is right, or splitting the difference, risks building the error into the structure.
  71. The correct answer is D (The rough opening width for the window unit shown): a window symbol is a break in the wall line with added frame lines, and a dimension called out right at that break gives the rough opening width the framer must build to. Sill height and header height for the same unit are typically found in a section drawing or the window schedule, not at this callout. Overall wall length is dimensioned separately along the full wall run.
  72. The correct answer is C (12 board feet, using thickness times width times length divided by 12): board footage equals thickness in inches times width in inches times length in feet, divided by 12, so 2 times 6 times 12 is 144, and 144 divided by 12 is 12 board feet. This formula lets a carpenter convert any solid dimension lumber piece into a comparable ordering unit. Skipping the divide by 12 step is a common arithmetic mistake that overstates the quantity by a factor of 12.
  73. The correct answer is A (2.0 cubic yards, since 54 cubic feet divided by 27 equals 2.0): multiplying 45 feet by 1.5 feet by 0.8 feet gives 54 cubic feet, and since concrete is ordered and priced by the yard, dividing by 27 converts that to exactly 2.0 cubic yards. Using the actual 0.8 foot depth rather than rounding it up avoids over-ordering. Dividing by the wrong number, such as 20, produces a volume that does not match how concrete is actually sold.
  74. Reading the correct row for each specific connection matters because mixing them up under-fastens or over-fastens the assembly.
  75. Ordering only the base 30 sheets leaves no margin for cutting around openings or damaged stock. The waste factor is applied after the base division, not before.
  76. In practice one or two more studs are added for the far corner and any partition tie-in. King studs, jack studs, cripples, and headers for any openings are counted separately from this base figure.
  77. Cross checking this figure against the door schedule confirms the correct unit was ordered.
  78. An allowance for waste and splices still needs to be added on top of this base figure. Ordering only 100 or 50 linear feet would leave the wall short a full plate member.
  79. The correct answer is D (0.75, calculated by dividing the 9 inch rise by the 12 inch run): a roof pitch expressed as rise over a 12 inch run converts directly to a decimal slope by dividing the rise by the run, so 9 divided by 12 equals 0.75. This is different from a 6 in 12 roof, which works out to 0.50, and an 8 in 12 roof, which works out to about 0.67. This decimal slope is the foundation for every rafter length and angle calculation.
  80. The correct answer is C (A window unit, distinguished from a door by having no swing arc): windows are drawn as a break in the wall line with added frame lines and, on some sets, a sill line, but with no quarter circle swing arc, which is the key detail separating them from doors. A door would show a leaf line and an arc curving into the room. Getting this distinction right prevents framing a window rough opening where a door was actually intended.
  81. The correct answer is B (2-2x12, since it is the smallest listed size rated past 7'-6"): the table shows a 2-2x10 header only reaching 6 feet 11 inches, which falls short of the 7 foot 6 inch opening, while the 2-2x12 is rated to 8 feet 1 inch, which covers it. The 2-2x8 falls even further short at 5 feet 8 inches. The 3-2x10 would also work but is oversized compared to the smaller 2-2x12 that already satisfies the span, wasting material.
  82. The correct answer is D (It confirms the proposed structure meets the applicable code, bylaws, and safety requirements): a permit is the mechanism by which the authority having jurisdiction reviews the proposed structure against the building code, zoning bylaws, and safety requirements before construction begins, not after. Building without one exposes the owner and contractor to stop-work orders, fines, and the risk of opening completed work back up for a later inspection. It has nothing to do with ownership or material pricing.
  83. The correct answer is A (After the forms and reinforcing steel are placed but before the concrete pour): a footing inspection confirms size, depth, and reinforcement against the approved drawings, and it has to happen while the reinforcing steel is still visible, which is before concrete is poured over it. Waiting until after the pour would make the reinforcement impossible to verify without breaking out the concrete. The framing inspection is a separate, much later checkpoint.
  84. The correct answer is C (Because this is the last point the framing itself is fully visible): once drywall or sheathing closes in the walls, the structural framing, sheathing, and rough-in work behind it can no longer be directly inspected without opening it back up. Scheduling the inspection at this stage lets the inspector verify everything against the approved drawings while it is still exposed. The footing inspection is a separate, earlier checkpoint tied to a different stage of construction.
  85. The correct answer is B (Because each province adopts the code with its own local amendments applied): the National Building Code is a model code, and each province and territory adopts it into law with its own amendments and, in some cases, its own separate provincial code built on the same foundation. This is why a detail acceptable in one province may need to be built differently in another. It is not a voluntary guideline, and it applies to residential construction as well as commercial.
  86. The correct answer is D (20 inches, read from the correct 3000 psf soil bearing capacity row): at 3000 psf soil bearing capacity, the table shows a 16 inch footing rated for 6,000 pounds per linear foot, which falls short of the required 6,500 pounds, while the 20 inch footing at the same soil capacity is rated for 7,500 pounds, which satisfies it. Reading the 2000 psf row instead would apply the wrong soil bearing assumption for this site. Using the narrower 16 inch footing at 3000 psf would under-size the foundation for the stated load.
  87. The correct answer is C (It affects which fire separation, exiting, and structural loading requirements apply): occupancy classification is a broad category the building code assigns based on intended use, and it drives which fire separation, exiting, and structural loading requirements the design and framing must satisfy. A residential occupancy and a mercantile occupancy can carry very different requirements even for a similar-sized building. Setbacks and finished floor elevation are determined separately from occupancy classification.
  88. The correct answer is A (8 feet maximum spacing with a 9 inch minimum embedment depth): the table row for a 2x6 sill plate with 5/8 inch bolts lists a maximum spacing of 8 feet and a minimum embedment of 9 inches. The 6 foot spacing and 7 inch embedment values instead apply to the 1/2 inch bolt rows in the table, a different combination. Using the wrong row would either under-anchor the sill plate or waste material with unnecessarily deep embedment.
  89. The correct answer is A (Addenda before signing, and change orders after construction has already started): addenda are official written changes issued before a contract is signed, correcting the original bid documents, while change orders are changes made after construction begins and need to be documented and approved before work proceeds. Mixing these terms up matters because the approval process and paperwork trail differ between the two. Both protect the tradesperson and the project's budget and schedule.
  90. The correct answer is D (Ask, since the contract usually states an order of precedence for conflicts): where drawings and specs conflict, the contract usually states which document governs, and the correct move on site is to ask rather than guess which one to follow. Assuming one document always wins, or averaging the two requirements, risks building something that satisfies neither the design intent nor the specified standard. Getting clarification in writing also protects the tradesperson if the conflict is questioned later.
  91. The correct answer is C (Find the enlarged detail drawing that explains that specific condition): a detail callout tells the reader where to find the enlarged, larger-scale detail sheet that explains a specific condition, such as blocking and headers at a bay window corner that the floor plan has no room to show at its own scale. That detail sheet gets checked before anything is cut. Ignoring the callout risks missing dimensions or assembly information the floor plan never included.
  92. The correct answer is B (It orients the building correctly on the lot relative to north): the north arrow orients the building on the lot, letting a carpenter align the site layout, sun exposure, and utility connections correctly against the site plan. Setback distances, load-bearing status, and drawing scale are each shown by separate symbols and callouts elsewhere on the set. Confusing the orientation on a sloped or oddly shaped lot can shift the whole layout in the wrong direction.
  93. The correct answer is B (Which way the door clears when it is fully opened): the quarter circle arc on a door symbol shows the swing direction, telling the reader which way the door clears when open, information that matters for furniture placement, accessibility clearances, and adjacent trades. Door height, fire rating, and hardware are instead found in the door schedule or the specifications. The arc alone communicates nothing about those separate details.
  94. The correct answer is D (Select Structural S-P-F, since it is rated highest in that species at 1650 psi): within the Spruce-Pine-Fir rows shown, Select Structural is rated at 1650 psi versus 1150 psi for No. 1/No. 2, making it the higher rated grade in that species. Select Structural Douglas Fir-Larch rates even higher at 1900 psi, but the specification calls for Spruce-Pine-Fir specifically, not a different species. Ordering the wrong species would not satisfy a specification written around S-P-F.
  95. The S-P-F 2x6 at 16 inches on centre uses a different spacing than what was specified for this job. The 2x4 size falls far short of the span regardless of species.
  96. The correct answer is B (It plans how to get the needed pieces out of stock with minimal waste): a cutting list plans how to get the needed pieces out of ordered lumber and sheet stock with the least waste, grouping similar-length cuts from the same piece of stock rather than cutting one piece per board and leaving unusable offcuts. It works alongside, not instead of, the original material takeoff. Occupancy classification and finished floor elevation are unrelated planning steps handled separately.
  97. The correct answer is D (So the exact corner can be quickly re-established after equipment disturbs the stakes): the original corner stakes and batter board strings sit inside or right at the working area and are frequently disturbed by excavation equipment, so offset stakes at a known fixed distance let the crew quickly re-establish the exact corner at any point in the job. Setbacks are a separate zoning requirement, not something offset stakes satisfy on their own. Utility locations and concrete volume are unrelated to why offset stakes are set.
  98. The correct answer is A (King studs, jack studs, cripples, and headers from the header schedule): the base stud calculation from wall length divided by spacing covers only the field studs, and king studs, jack studs, and cripples at each door and window opening need to be counted separately from the header schedule, along with the header itself. Skipping this step under-orders the material actually needed to frame the openings. Plate stock, sheathing, and anchor bolts are each counted through their own separate takeoff steps.
  99. The correct answer is C (Finding the length of a hip rafter or a deck diagonal): the Pythagorean theorem's legs-and-hypotenuse relationship shows up constantly in layout work beyond squaring corners, including finding a hip rafter length or checking that a deck or addition is square to an existing structure. Roof pitch conversion uses a simple rise over run division rather than the theorem itself. Finished floor elevation and occupancy classification are unrelated to this geometric relationship.
  100. The correct answer is B (26 sheets, rounding the base count plus 8 percent waste up): dividing 768 square feet by the 32 square foot area of one sheet gives a base count of exactly 24 sheets, and adding an 8 percent waste factor brings that to 25.92, which rounds up to 26 full sheets since a partial sheet cannot be ordered. A waste factor always adds material for cutting and job site damage, it never reduces the order. Using a 25 percent waste factor instead of the stated 8 percent would over-order well beyond what this job needs.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (135 feet, three times the wall's linear footage for one bottom plate and two top plates): A standard stud wall uses one bottom plate and a double top plate, which is three separate plate runs along the wall's full length. Multiplying the 45 foot wall length by three gives 135 feet of plate stock needed before any waste or splice allowance is added. Estimators always add extra afterward for cutting losses and joints, but the base calculation is the three-times figure.
  2. The correct answer is C (25 studs, before adding extra for the far corner or a partition tie-in): Converting 32 feet to inches gives 384 inches, and dividing by the 16 inch spacing gives 24 full spaces. Adding one stud for the starting point brings the base count to 25 studs. Extra studs for the far corner, partition intersections, and window or door framing get added separately from this baseline figure.
  3. The correct answer is C (27 sheets, after applying the waste allowance to the base sheet count): Dividing 800 square feet by the 32 square foot area of one sheet gives a base count of 25 sheets. Applying an 8 percent waste allowance to that figure, 25 times 1.08, gives 27 sheets. Estimators round the waste-adjusted figure up rather than down so the crew never runs short mid-job.
  4. The correct answer is B (20 board feet, from multiplying 2 by 10 by 12 and dividing by 12): The board footage formula multiplies thickness in inches by width in inches by length in feet, then divides by 12. For this piece, 2 times 10 times 12 equals 240, and 240 divided by 12 equals 20 board feet. This formula lets a crew price and order solid dimension lumber consistently regardless of the piece's actual size.
  5. The correct answer is B (It groups similar-length cuts from the same piece of stock to minimize waste): A cutting list plans how the needed pieces come out of ordered lumber and sheet stock so similar-length cuts are grouped from the same board wherever possible. This approach leaves fewer unusable offcuts than cutting one piece per board as each length comes up. Planning cuts this way directly reduces material cost on a job with many different framing lengths.
  6. The correct answer is B (It lets the authority having jurisdiction confirm the design meets code before work starts): A building permit is the mechanism by which the local authority having jurisdiction confirms a proposed structure meets the applicable building code, zoning bylaws, and safety requirements before construction begins. Building without one exposes the owner and contractor to stop-work orders, fines, and the risk of opening finished work back up for inspection. Owner approval of the drawings has no bearing on the code compliance a permit confirms.
  7. The correct answer is A (After the forms and reinforcing steel are placed but before concrete is poured): A footing inspection typically happens after the forms and reinforcing steel are in place but before the concrete is placed, so the inspector can confirm size, depth, and reinforcement against the approved drawings while everything is still visible. Calling for the pour before that inspection is signed off risks having to expose or remove hardened concrete. This checkpoint exists specifically because rebar and form dimensions cannot be verified once concrete covers them.
  8. The correct answer is D (After framing, sheathing, and rough-ins are done but before walls are closed in): A framing inspection happens after the structural framing, sheathing, and rough-in work are complete but before the walls are closed in with insulation and drywall, since this is the last point the framing itself is fully visible. Scheduling it later means the inspector cannot see the structure without opening finished walls back up. This checkpoint protects both the owner and the code official from hidden framing defects.
  9. The correct answer is C (Document it as a change order and get it approved before the work proceeds): A change order is a change made after construction has started, and it needs to be documented and approved before the work proceeds, protecting both the tradesperson and the project's budget and schedule. An addendum is a different mechanism used only for changes issued before a contract is signed. Proceeding on a verbal request alone leaves no record if a dispute comes up later.
  10. The correct answer is B (A benchmark, which every subsequent elevation on the site is measured against): A benchmark is a fixed reference point of known elevation, often a permanent survey marker, fire hydrant, or a protected stake, that every other elevation on the job gets measured from. Elevations are transferred from it around the site using a builder's level or laser level. Batter boards, offset stakes, and screed guides all serve later layout and finishing purposes but do not establish the original elevation reference.
  11. The correct answer is C (A rotating laser level, picked up anywhere on site by a grade rod receiver): A rotating laser level projects a full 360 degree level plane that can be picked up anywhere around the site by a receiver mounted on a grade rod, letting one person do levelling work that used to require two. The receiver indicates when it is centred on the beam, and the rod reading at that point gives the elevation. A hand level and a string line level both work only over short sighting distances and are not suited to a large open site.
  12. The correct answer is D (The top of the foundation is level and at the correct elevation all around): Before framing begins, the crew checks that the foundation walls and sill plates are level around the entire perimeter and that the top of the foundation sits at the correct elevation, since even a small amount of unlevelness transfers directly into the floor system above. This check happens before the sill plate is fastened, not after. Waiting for full 28 day strength is not required to proceed with sill plate installation.
  13. The correct answer is B (0.75 metres, found by subtracting the smaller rod reading from the larger one): Differential levelling finds the elevation difference between two points by subtracting one rod reading from the other, since both readings are taken against the same level line of sight. Here, 2.10 metres minus 1.35 metres equals 0.75 metres. The footing form location reads a larger number on the rod, which means that point sits lower than the benchmark by 0.75 metres.
  14. The correct answer is A (The workability, meaning how stiff or fluid the mix is for placing and finishing): A slump test packs a sample into a slump cone, lifts the cone straight up, and measures how far the concrete settles, in millimetres, which indicates how stiff or fluid the mix is. A low slump suits flatwork needing a tooled finish, while a higher slump flows more easily into narrow forms and dense rebar cages. Compressive strength and air content are separate properties tested with different methods entirely.
  15. The correct answer is B (Extra water raises the water-cement ratio and lowers the concrete's final strength): The water-cement ratio, the weight of water divided by the weight of cement, is the single biggest factor controlling both strength and workability, and every litre added beyond the design mix lowers compressive strength. Adding water to solve a placement problem trades long-term strength for short-term convenience. A water-reducing admixture is the correct way to improve flow without lowering strength.
  16. The correct answer is B (A water reducer, which allows a lower water-cement ratio while keeping workability): A water reducer allows a lower water-cement ratio while keeping the workability needed to flow around dense reinforcement, giving the crew a strong, easily placed mix without adding extra water. Retarders and accelerators change how fast the concrete sets rather than how easily it flows. Air-entraining agents address freeze-thaw durability, not placement workability.
  17. The correct answer is A (Pour A will likely be weaker than pour B once both are fully cured): A higher slump without a water-reducing admixture generally means more water was used relative to the same amount of cement, raising the water-cement ratio. A higher water-cement ratio makes the mix easier to place and finish but weakens the final product once cured. Pour A's easier flow is therefore a sign of lower strength, not higher strength, compared to pour B.
  18. The correct answer is D (Use heated water and aggregate along with insulating blankets over the fresh concrete): Cold-weather concreting, generally triggered when temperatures are expected to drop below 5 degrees Celsius during curing, calls for heated water and aggregate in the batch and insulating blankets or enclosures over the fresh concrete to keep it above about 10 degrees Celsius until it gains sufficient strength. Concrete that freezes before it gains adequate strength suffers permanent damage from disrupted hydration. Adding extra water or stripping forms early both work against strength development, not toward it.
  19. The correct answer is C (Air-entrained concrete resists surface scaling from repeated freeze-thaw cycles and de-icing salt): Air-entrained concrete has millions of microscopic air bubbles distributed through the mix, giving water inside somewhere to expand into when it freezes, which matters a great deal across Canadian winters. Ordinary concrete exposed to freeze-thaw cycles and de-icing salt will scale and spall at the surface within a few winters. Air entrainment is a durability measure for exposed conditions, not a strength or curing-speed advantage.
  20. The correct answer is C (Plastic shrinkage cracking from hot weather, addressed by fogging the surface or using retarders): Hot-weather concreting brings rapid moisture loss and fast setting that can cause plastic shrinkage cracking before finishing is complete. Crews respond by shading the work, fogging the surface, or using retarders to buy extra finishing time. Freeze-thaw scaling and cold joints are separate problems tied to cold temperatures and pour sequencing, not to hot-weather moisture loss.
  21. The correct answer is A (The cold, fast pump pour, since a faster fill rate and cooler concrete both raise pressure): Form pressure increases with the rate of pour and decreases as temperature rises, because warmer concrete sets faster and behaves more like a solid sooner, reducing the time it exerts hydrostatic pressure on the forms. A mass pour filled quickly on a cold day generates far higher pressure than the same wall poured slowly on a warm afternoon. Formwork braced for one set of conditions can fail if the pour rate or temperature changes without adjusting bracing to suit.
  22. The correct answer is A (Spreaders, temporary blocks between the form walls that are pulled as the pour reaches them): Spreaders are temporary blocks set between the footing form walls to hold the correct width until the concrete is placed, and they are pulled out as the pour reaches them so nothing is left embedded. Form ties, walers, and strongbacks are all components used on taller wall formwork rather than a simple footing form. A bowed footing form from missing or poorly spaced spreaders gives an inconsistent width that can compromise bearing capacity.
  23. The correct answer is C (A form tie, which connects the two form faces and resists outward pressure): Form ties run through the wall thickness and connect the two form faces together, resisting the outward pressure of the wet concrete that would otherwise push the faces apart as the pour rises. Walers distribute that tie load evenly across the sheathing, and strongbacks keep a tall or heavily loaded form straight and rigid. A spreader is a temporary footing-form component, not a permanent wall-form tension component.
  24. The correct answer is A (Strongbacks, vertical members that make the whole form act as one rigid unit): Strongbacks are vertical members added over the walers on tall or heavily loaded forms to keep everything straight and true, making the whole assembly act as one rigid unit rather than flexing panel by panel. Simply adding more ties addresses tension across the wall thickness but does not stiffen the panel assembly the way a strongback does. A sill gasket is a framing component used at the top of a cured foundation, not a formwork stiffener.
  25. The correct answer is D (A screed, which strikes off excess concrete to establish a flat, level surface): A screed is a straight board or specialized tool dragged across the top of the edge forms to strike off excess concrete and establish a flat, level surface before finishing tools take over. The edge forms themselves are set to the finished floor elevation and act as the screed guide. Walers, batter boards, and strongbacks all serve different purposes on wall forms or site layout, not slab screeding.
  26. The correct answer is C (A prefabricated panel system, faster to set and strip and reused many times): Prefabricated panel systems use modular steel-framed or aluminum-framed panels, typically 2 feet wide, that pin together on site and are reused hundreds of times before wearing out, giving a faster setup and a more consistent finish. Built-in-place forms remain common for one-off shapes and repairs but are not designed for hundreds of reuses. Preserved wood foundations and insulated concrete forms are wall assembly types, not reusable panel formwork systems.
  27. The correct answer is D (A sonotube or reusable steel column form, plumbed and braced carefully): Column forms are typically round or square sonotubes or reusable steel or fibreglass forms that must be perfectly plumbed and braced, since any lean shows immediately in a vertical element. Plank forms and edge forms are used for footings and slabs, not vertical columns. Prefabricated wall panels are built for flat foundation walls, not round columns.
  28. The correct answer is D (It keeps the concrete from bonding to the form face and gives a smoother finish): A release agent, or form oil, keeps concrete from bonding to the form face, protects the panel from moisture damage, and gives a smoother finished surface. Well-maintained forms treated this way can be reused dozens of times, which matters directly to a contractor's material costs. Form oil has no effect on the water-cement ratio, curing time, or the need for a separate waterproofing membrane.
  29. The correct answer is D (It has to hold its shape against concrete placed in a sloped, stepped configuration): Formwork for stairs is more specialized because it needs to hold its shape against concrete placed in a sloped, stepped configuration, unlike the straight vertical or horizontal surfaces of a wall or footing form. Reinforcement is still needed in a concrete stair the same as other structural elements. A simple edge-and-screed method works for a flat slab, not a stepped stair form.
  30. The correct answer is B (As little as 24 hours, since footing forms carry no significant load once set): Footing forms, carrying no load beyond the concrete's own weight and no longer structural once the concrete has taken initial set, can often be stripped in as little as 24 hours under normal conditions. This is always confirmed against the project's engineering requirements and local code before proceeding. Shoring under a suspended slab needs to remain in place far longer since it carries construction loads the concrete cannot yet support on its own.
  31. The correct answer is C (It should stay in place much longer, sometimes a week or more, until strength develops): Formwork under a slab or beam that must carry construction loads before the concrete has developed full strength may need to stay in place for a week or more, sometimes longer in cold weather, since the concrete needs enough strength to safely span and carry loads on its own. This is a much longer interval than the roughly 24 hours often used for a footing form. Stripping early just to keep a schedule risks the concrete sagging, cracking, or losing its finished shape.
  32. The correct answer is A (Extend the stripping time well beyond the normal summer figures because curing slows in the cold): Cold temperatures slow curing dramatically, so stripping times in winter need to be extended well beyond the summer numbers used in normal conditions. A carpenter should never strip forms early just to keep a schedule, since a form stripped too soon can let the concrete sag, crack, or lose its finished shape. The 24 to 48 hour figure assumes ordinary temperature conditions, not a cold snap.
  33. The correct answer is C (A metric bar size, where the number approximates the bar's diameter in millimetres): Canadian rebar sizing uses a metric bar designation system where the number approximates the bar's diameter in millimetres and the M denotes metric bar, so a 15M bar is roughly 16 millimetres in diameter. Residential foundation work commonly uses 10M or 15M bar for walls and footings. The designation says nothing directly about cover distance, bar spacing, or yield strength, which are all specified separately.
  34. The correct answer is B (Chairs and spacers, which hold the rebar cage up off the form face correctly): Chairs and spacers, small plastic or wire supports, hold the rebar cage up off the form face or the ground at the specified height and keep it from settling during the pour. Without them, the cage can settle and leave inadequate cover, producing a weaker section than designed. Dowels, keyways, and welded wire mesh serve different reinforcement functions and do not maintain rebar position within a wall form.
  35. The correct answer is B (The mesh needs lifting into the middle or upper part of the slab during placement): Welded wire mesh is commonly used in slabs-on-grade to control crack width, but it needs to be lifted into the middle or upper portion of the slab thickness during the pour, not left lying on the ground where it does almost nothing structurally. Leaving it flat on the gravel base defeats its purpose entirely, since it ends up at the very bottom of the finished slab. Mesh is a recognized slab reinforcement method, not something avoided in residential work.
  36. The correct answer is A (Dowels, which tie the two pours together and transfer loads across the cold joint): Dowels are short lengths of rebar set to project out of one concrete pour so they can be embedded into a second, adjoining pour, tying the two together structurally and transferring loads across the cold joint where the earlier pour has already hardened. Between a footing and the wall poured on top, dowels keep the assembly acting as one continuous unit. Chairs, stirrups, and form ties all serve different reinforcement or formwork functions.
  37. The correct answer is A (Steel is embedded near the tension face because concrete is weak in tension there): Concrete is strong in compression but weak in tension, and rebar is embedded in concrete specifically to carry the tension loads the concrete alone cannot handle. Positioning the steel near the tension face puts it where it does the most structural good, while cover still protects it from moisture and corrosion. This placement decision is a structural one, not simply a matter of construction convenience.
  38. The correct answer is C (A pile foundation, using individual piles driven or drilled down to a stable bearing stratum): Pile foundations use individual concrete, steel, or timber piles driven or drilled down to a stable bearing stratum, used where surface soils are too weak or unstable to support a conventional footing, or on sloped sites where a continuous footing would need excessive excavation. Slab-on-grade, strip footings, and preserved wood foundations all still depend on the near-surface soil having adequate bearing capacity. None of those alternatives solves the weak-soil problem the way piles do.
  39. The correct answer is A (A preserved wood foundation, built from pressure-treated lumber on a gravel footing pad): Preserved wood foundations use pressure-treated lumber and plywood, built much like a conventional framed wall, resting on a gravel footing pad rather than a poured concrete footing.
