They are a recognized, code-permitted option particularly suited to remote locations where trucking in ready-mix concrete is difficult or expensive. Insulated concrete forms, pile foundations, and slab-on-grade all still require substantial concrete delivered to site.
  40. The correct answer is D (Insulated concrete form, a stay-in-place foam formwork system filled with concrete): Insulated concrete form, or ICF, is a stay-in-place foam formwork system filled with concrete that also provides built-in insulation as part of the wall assembly itself. A conventional poured concrete wall needs separate rigid insulation added afterward to achieve the same result. Preserved wood, pile, and slab-on-grade foundations do not provide this same built-in insulated wall assembly.
  41. The correct answer is D (A slab-on-grade foundation, common where a basement is not wanted and frost depths are shallow): Slab-on-grade foundations skip the full foundation wall and basement, pouring a single thickened-edge or monolithic slab directly on grade, common where a basement isn't wanted, in warmer climates with shallower frost depths, or for garages and additions. Pile foundations address weak or sloped soils, not cost or climate alone. A strip footing with a full wall effectively builds a basement, which this scenario is trying to avoid.
  42. The correct answer is B (A waterproofing membrane, a more robust, continuous barrier built for hydrostatic pressure): Waterproofing membranes are a more robust, continuous barrier used where hydrostatic pressure or a high water table is expected, unlike ordinary damp-proofing, which is a bituminous coating meant to resist moisture migration under normal conditions. A vapour barrier addresses interior moisture in framing, not exterior groundwater pressure. A keyway is a structural connection detail and has no waterproofing function.
  43. The correct answer is B (Damp-proofing, a coating applied to resist ordinary moisture migration through the wall): Damp-proofing is a coating, typically bituminous, applied to the wall exterior to resist ordinary moisture migration, appropriate for typical soil moisture conditions without significant hydrostatic pressure. Waterproofing membranes are reserved for higher-risk conditions such as a high water table. Rigid insulation and a drainage board serve thermal and membrane-protection purposes, not the moisture-resisting coating itself.
  44. The correct answer is D (Foundation drainage, or weeping tile, which collects and carries groundwater away before it builds up pressure): Foundation drainage, commonly called weeping tile or drain tile, is a perforated pipe laid around the footing perimeter, wrapped in filter fabric and bedded in clear stone, collecting groundwater and carrying it away before it can build up hydrostatic pressure against the wall. A waterproofing membrane protects the wall surface directly but does not carry water away. A sill gasket and a keyway serve entirely different functions elsewhere in the assembly.
  45. The correct answer is D (Clear stone, wrapped with filter fabric to keep fine soil particles out of the pipe): Weeping tile is bedded in clear stone and wrapped in filter fabric, which lets groundwater reach the perforated pipe easily while keeping fine soil particles from clogging it over time. Compacted clay would block water movement entirely, defeating the drainage system's purpose. Concrete and rigid foam are used elsewhere in the foundation assembly, not as pipe bedding material.
  46. The correct answer is C (The wall has no lateral support until floor framing goes on, so it needs bracing): A foundation wall poured and cured on its own has no lateral support at the top until the main floor system is framed on top of it, so backfilling too early or too aggressively can push the wall in or crack it. Standard practice is to brace the wall temporarily, or better, get the main floor framing in place first, and backfill in stages with compaction. Backfilling in a single full-depth pass ignores this lateral support requirement entirely.
  47. The correct answer is A (Backfill in stages with compaction, rather than dumping the full depth in one aggressive pass): Backfilling against an unbraced wall too early, or too aggressively with heavy equipment, can push the wall in or crack it before it has lateral support from the floor system above. Standard practice is to backfill in stages with compaction rather than dumping the full depth in one pass. A cured wall is still vulnerable to this kind of lateral load until it has proper bracing or floor framing in place.
  48. The correct answer is A (Stripping time depends on the load the form must carry once removed, often much sooner): Formwork stripping time is a separate question from full curing, depending on what the form is holding up and what load will be applied once it is removed. Footing forms carrying no significant load can often strip in as little as 24 hours, long before 28 day strength is reached. Confirming stripping time against the project's engineering requirements and local code, not against the 28 day benchmark alone, is the correct field practice.
  49. The correct answer is C (1.2 cubic metres, after converting dimensions to metres and adding the waste allowance): Converting the dimensions gives 9.14 metres long, 0.4572 metres wide, and 0.254 metres deep, and multiplying those together gives roughly 1.06 cubic metres of raw volume. Applying a 10 percent waste allowance, 1.06 times 1.10, gives about 1.17 cubic metres, which rounds up to an order of 1.2 cubic metres since suppliers deliver in defined increments. Running short mid-pour and forcing a second truck and a cold joint is worse than ordering slightly more than the bare minimum.
  50. The correct answer is D (4.8 cubic metres, after converting dimensions to metres and adding the waste allowance): Converting the dimensions gives 6.096 metres by 7.315 metres by 0.1016 metres, and multiplying those together gives roughly 4.53 cubic metres of raw volume. Applying a 5 percent waste allowance, 4.53 times 1.05, gives about 4.76 cubic metres, which rounds up to an order of 4.8 cubic metres since suppliers deliver in defined increments. Ordering to the raw figure alone risks running short once minor over-excavation and normal spillage are accounted for.
  51. The correct answer is C (Chairs, small supports that hold the rebar cage at the specified height): chairs are small plastic, wire, or concrete supports set beneath and around the reinforcing steel to hold it off the subgrade or form face at the exact height called for on the drawings. Walers and strongbacks brace the outside of wall forms rather than positioning steel, and spreaders hold footing form walls apart, not the rebar itself. Getting the chair height right is what keeps the steel where it does the most structural good once concrete is placed.
  52. The correct answer is A (So seasonal soil freezing and thawing beneath it cannot heave and crack the foundation): water in the soil expands as it freezes, and if a footing sits within the zone that freezes each winter, that expansion can heave the footing unevenly and crack the foundation above it. Placing the footing base below the local frost depth keeps it resting on soil that stays at a stable temperature year-round. This has nothing to do with curing strength, bracing needs, or exterior waterproofing, which are separate concerns.
  53. The correct answer is D (Resist the outward pressure pushing the two form faces apart as concrete fills the wall): wet concrete behaves like a fluid and exerts significant lateral (hydrostatic) pressure on the form faces as the wall fills, and form ties are the tension members that hold the two sides of the form together against that pressure. Rebar cover is maintained by chairs and spacers, not ties, and ties have no role in slump or in the footing-to-wall dowel connection. Undersized or improperly spaced ties are a common cause of form blowouts during a pour.
  54. The correct answer is B (A wider footing spreads the building load over more area to suit the low bearing capacity): a footing transfers the building's load into the soil, and the pressure that soil experiences equals the load divided by the footing's contact area, so widening the footing reduces the pressure on each square foot of soil. Soft clay has a lower safe bearing capacity than firmer soils, so it needs that lower pressure to avoid excessive settlement. Footing width and frost depth are separate design considerations, and footing width absolutely does depend on soil type.
  55. The correct answer is A (A continuous waterproofing membrane, built for hydrostatic pressure exposure): a wall this deep below grade with a high water table is exposed to real hydrostatic pressure, meaning groundwater can actively push against and through the wall, which requires a continuous waterproofing membrane rather than a lighter coating. A bituminous damp-proofing coating alone is only adequate where hydrostatic pressure is not expected. Interior vapour barriers do nothing to stop exterior groundwater intrusion at this depth.
  56. The correct answer is D (It protects the steel from corrosion while positioning it where it does the most structural good): concrete cover keeps the reinforcing steel embedded far enough from the surface that moisture and air are less likely to reach it and cause corrosion, while also placing the steel at the depth within the footing where it best resists the tension forces the concrete alone cannot handle. Cover distance is not primarily about concrete quantity, curing speed, or drainage. Specified cover distances come directly from code or engineering drawings and should not be reduced in the field.
  57. The correct answer is B (About 24 hours after the pour, once initial set has occurred): footing forms are non-structural once the concrete has taken its initial set, and in normal conditions that typically happens within about a day, well before the concrete reaches its full 28-day design strength. Stripping after only an hour risks damaging the still-plastic concrete, while waiting a full week or 28 days is unnecessary for a form that carries no ongoing load. Cold weather extends this timeline significantly, which is why weather conditions are always checked before stripping.
  58. The correct answer is C (The carpenter must confirm the specific frost depth requirement for that municipality): frost depth varies significantly across Canada based on regional climate, and the applicable requirement is set locally through the provincial or municipal building code adoption rather than one single national number. A carpenter cannot assume a frost depth from a previous job applies elsewhere. A deeper frost depth requires a deeper footing, not a shallower one, and frost depth is just as relevant to footings as it is to slabs in unheated or seasonal structures.
  59. The correct answer is A (Approximately 1.26 cubic metres, after converting all three dimensions to metres): converting 20 inches to about 0.508 metres, 8 inches to about 0.203 metres, and 40 feet to about 12.19 metres, then multiplying width by depth by length gives roughly 1.26 cubic metres. The other options are simple multiples or fractions of this correct figure, which is exactly the kind of arithmetic slip that happens when a dimension is left in the wrong unit or a factor of two is dropped or doubled. This base figure would still need a waste allowance added before ordering.
  60. The correct answer is C (It locks the wall to the footing so both resist lateral and shear forces together): the keyway is a groove cast into the wet footing that the wall concrete later fills, and dowels projecting up from the footing tie directly into the wall's reinforcement, together mechanically interlocking the two pours so they act as one continuous structural unit against lateral and shear forces. This detail is about structural connection, not crack control in a later slab, thermal movement, or rebar cover depth. Without it, the wall-to-footing joint would rely on bond alone, which is much weaker.
  61. The correct answer is B (The site has a milder climate with less severe ground freezing): frost depth requirements are set based on how deep the ground actually freezes in a given region, so a shallower required frost depth directly reflects a milder climate with less severe winter freezing. It says nothing on its own about water table height or soil bearing capacity, which are separate site conditions investigated independently. A colder climate would require a deeper frost depth, not a shallower one.
  62. The correct answer is C (Typically 10M or 15M bar, sized for standard residential foundations): standard residential foundation walls and footings are generally reinforced with 10M or 15M bar under Canada's metric rebar designation system, which is adequately sized for typical residential loads without being oversized. Larger bars like 20M, 25M, 30M or 35M are reserved for heavier structural applications such as commercial or engineered structures carrying much greater loads. The 5M and 8M designations referenced in one option are not standard sizes used in this context.
  63. The correct answer is A (Lift it into the middle or upper portion of the slab thickness during the pour): welded wire mesh is only effective at controlling shrinkage cracking if it ends up within the body of the slab rather than sitting on the ground, so correct field practice is to lift and hook the mesh up into the middle or upper portion of the slab thickness as concrete is placed around it. Leaving it flat on the subgrade defeats its purpose entirely, and placing it after the slab has cured or only at the edge forms would not reinforce the slab body at all. This is a commonly cited field mistake worth watching for.
  64. The correct answer is D (Once the wall is temporarily braced or the main floor framing is in place): a freshly cured foundation wall with nothing resisting it at the top has no lateral support against the pressure of backfill soil, so it must either be temporarily braced or have the main floor framing installed before backfilling proceeds safely. Backfilling immediately after only 24 hours of curing, or with no bracing at all regardless of schedule pressure, risks pushing the wall inward or cracking it. Waiting the full 28 days with still no bracing does not solve the lateral support problem either.
  65. The correct answer is B (It would increase significantly, requiring tighter tie spacing and stronger bracing): a faster pour rate builds up hydrostatic pressure in the form more quickly because concrete near the bottom has less time to begin setting and stiffening before more weight is added above it, and colder temperatures slow that setting process further, compounding the effect. This combination of fast pumped placement and cold weather is exactly the scenario that calls for tighter tie spacing and stronger bracing than a slow, warm-weather pour would need. Pump-placed concrete does not inherently contain less water, and this pressure increase affects the wall forms specifically, not just the footing.
  66. The correct answer is C (About 53 inches below grade, including the safety margin and footing depth): the footing bottom must sit at the 40-inch frost line plus the 4-inch safety margin, reaching 44 inches, and the 9-inch footing depth is added below that point, so the excavation must reach 44 plus 9 equals 53 inches below grade. The other options either skip the footing depth, skip the safety margin, or add an amount beyond what the stated figures actually require. Getting this addition right on site avoids under-excavating and having to redo the dig.
  67. The correct answer is D (Add a water-reducing admixture at the batch plant before delivery): a water-reducing admixture improves the flow and workability of concrete around dense rebar without adding extra mixing water, which would otherwise raise the water-cement ratio and weaken the cured concrete. Adding water on site is exactly the practice that should be avoided for this reason. A retarder only slows set time and does not primarily address flow, and shifting the sand-to-aggregate ratio changes mix design in ways that are decided by the mix designer, not adjusted informally on site.
  68. The correct answer is A (The stiffness or fluidity of the concrete mix): the slump test measures how far a sample settles after the supporting cone is removed, which directly reflects the mix's workability, ranging from a stiff, low-slump mix to a fluid, high-slump mix. It does not directly measure 28-day compressive strength, entrained air content, or rebar cover depth, all of which are checked by separate tests or measurements. Slump is checked on site specifically because workability needs to be confirmed before or during placement, not after curing.
  69. The correct answer is B (A bituminous damp-proofing coating applied to the exterior wall): where there is no hydrostatic pressure and the soil drains well, a lighter bituminous damp-proofing coating is generally adequate to resist normal soil moisture against a shallow crawlspace wall. A full continuous waterproofing membrane is reserved for conditions with real hydrostatic pressure, which this scenario specifically rules out. Skipping exterior moisture protection entirely, or relying on rigid insulation with no coating beneath it, both leave the wall vulnerable to moisture over time.
  70. The correct answer is C (Air-entrained concrete mixed specifically for this driveway application): air-entrained concrete contains microscopic air bubbles that give freezing water inside the concrete somewhere to expand into, which is essential for a slab exposed to Canadian freeze-thaw cycling and de-icing salt exposure. An ordinary mix with only a retarder or accelerator added does not address this durability concern at all. A low-slump mix with no admixtures also fails to provide the freeze-thaw protection this application specifically needs.
  71. The correct answer is A (Below about 5 degrees Celsius during the curing period): cold-weather concreting protection generally becomes necessary once air temperatures are expected to drop below about 5 degrees Celsius during the curing period, since concrete's hydration and strength gain slow significantly as temperatures approach freezing. Waiting until temperatures reach 0 degrees Celsius or lower is too late, since strength gain has already been compromised well before the water itself would freeze. The 15 degree Celsius threshold in another option is far too conservative for normal practice.
  72. The correct answer is D (About 10 degrees Celsius until the concrete gains sufficient strength): during cold-weather protection, the concrete's internal temperature is generally kept above roughly 10 degrees Celsius until it has gained enough strength to safely withstand freezing without permanent damage. Allowing it to sit near 0 degrees Celsius still risks slowed or halted hydration, and a negative temperature would actively risk freezing the mix water. A target of 25 degrees Celsius is unnecessarily high and not the standard minimum threshold used for this protection period.
  73. The correct answer is C (Permanently weak, porous concrete caused by disrupted hydration reactions): when fresh concrete freezes before it has gained adequate strength, the water within it expands and disrupts the hydration reactions and internal structure that would otherwise have produced dense, strong concrete, and this damage does not reverse once the concrete later thaws. It does not result in faster strength gain, no lasting effect, or improved future freeze-thaw resistance, all of which describe the opposite of what actually happens. This is exactly why cold-weather protection measures are taken before the temperature threshold is reached, not after damage occurs.
  74. The correct answer is B (Shading the work, fogging the surface, or using a retarder): plastic shrinkage cracking happens when the exposed concrete surface loses moisture faster than bleed water can replace it, and shading the work, fogging the surface with a fine mist, or using a retarder to slow the surface's early stiffening all reduce that moisture loss rate. Adding more water to the mix weakens the concrete and does not solve the evaporation problem. Removing forms early or adding more accelerator would make the drying and cracking risk worse, not better, in hot windy conditions.
  75. The correct answer is D (28 days, the period specifications typically use as the benchmark): compressive strength requirements in concrete specifications are conventionally stated as the strength the concrete is expected to reach after 28 days of curing, even though concrete gains a large share of its ultimate strength well before that point. Referencing 7 days or 3 days would only capture early, partial strength gain, and 90 days goes well beyond the period specifications typically use as their benchmark. This 28-day standard lets different mixes and projects be compared on a consistent basis.
  76. The correct answer is A (24 to 48 hours in typical non-cold conditions): wall forms holding up only their own weight and minor handling loads, with no suspended structural load to carry, can typically be stripped within about 24 to 48 hours under normal, non-cold conditions once the concrete has reached adequate early strength. Stripping after only 4 to 6 hours risks damaging still-plastic concrete, while waiting 10 to 14 days or 6 to 8 weeks is unnecessarily conservative for this kind of non-load-bearing form. Cold weather would extend this timeline well beyond the normal range.
  77. The correct answer is B (The slab needs enough strength on its own to safely span and carry loads): a suspended slab must develop sufficient strength to span between its supports and carry construction loads on its own before the shoring beneath it can be removed, which takes considerably longer than the time needed for a footing that carries no span at all. Aesthetics play no role in this decision. Suspended slabs do not cure faster than footings under the same conditions, and shoring removal timing is directly tied to the concrete's actual strength gain, not unrelated to it.
  78. The correct answer is C (Hold the correct footing width until the concrete is placed): spreader blocks are temporary spacers set between the two footing form walls to hold them the correct distance apart, maintaining the specified footing width until concrete fills the form and the spreaders are removed or displaced. They play no reinforcing role, no sealing role at the footing-to-wall joint, and no screeding role for finishing the surface. Without spreaders, the form walls could shift inward or outward under the weight and pressure of the pour.
  79. The correct answer is D (Prefabricated modular steel-framed or aluminum-framed panel systems reused many times): on a project with many repetitive standard walls, prefabricated modular panel systems set up and strip faster than built-in-place plywood forms and produce a more consistent finish across every wall, since the same panels are reused repeatedly with minimal rebuilding. Built-in-place plywood forms exclusively would slow the schedule considerably on repetitive work. Slab edge forms and sonotube column forms are designed for entirely different applications and are not suited to forming full-height walls.
  80. The correct answer is A (Walers, horizontal members that run along the outside of the panels): walers are the horizontal members running along the outside face of a wall form, spreading the concentrated load from individual form ties evenly across the sheathing panels rather than letting that load punch through at isolated points. Strongbacks are vertical members added over the walers, not horizontal ones. Spreaders and chairs serve entirely different purposes in footing forms and rebar positioning, not in distributing tie loads on a wall form.
  81. The correct answer is C (Strongbacks, vertical members keeping a tall form straight and rigid): strongbacks are vertical members added over the horizontal walers on tall or heavily loaded wall forms, tying the walers together so the entire form assembly stays straight and acts as one rigid unit rather than bowing or flexing panel by panel. Walers themselves run horizontally and distribute tie load, which is a different function. Dowels and screeds serve entirely different purposes unrelated to keeping a tall form rigid during the pour.
  82. The correct answer is B (Keep concrete from bonding to the form and give a smoother finish): a release agent coats the form face so the cured concrete does not bond to the plywood, which both protects the form for reuse and produces a smoother, more consistent finished concrete surface once stripped. It has no effect on the concrete's compressive strength or curing time, and it does not replace the structural function of form ties in any way. Skipping this step risks damaging both the form panel and the concrete surface when the form is removed.
  83. The correct answer is A (A screed, dragged across the edge forms to strike off concrete): a screed is the straight board or tool dragged across the top of the edge forms, set to the finished floor elevation, to strike off excess concrete and leave the slab surface at the correct level before final finishing. Walers and strongbacks are wall-form components unrelated to slab finishing, and spreaders hold footing forms apart rather than striking off a slab surface. Screeding is typically followed by floating and troweling to complete the finished surface.
  84. The correct answer is D (It must hold its shape against concrete placed in a stepped, sloped configuration): stair formwork has to hold a stepped, sloped shape under the lateral pressure of wet concrete, which is geometrically more demanding to brace and hold true than a straightforward flat wall or footing form. It absolutely carries lateral pressure at each step and requires careful bracing and staking, contrary to two of the answer choices, and it cannot simply reuse the flat screed technique used on an open slab pour since there is no single flat plane to strike off. This complexity is why stair forms are often built and checked especially carefully before the pour begins.
  85. The correct answer is B (Any lean in a vertical column shows immediately once the concrete is cast): a column form that is even slightly out of plumb will cast a visibly leaning column once the concrete cures, and because a column stands as an isolated vertical element rather than being tied into a long wall run, there is nothing nearby to visually or structurally disguise a small error. Column forms do require bracing before the pour and do carry hydrostatic pressure from the wet concrete just like wall forms, so those claims are incorrect. Sonotubes also wear out with repeated reuse and are not indefinitely reusable.
  86. The correct answer is C (The bar's diameter in millimetres, its actual cross-section size): in Canada's metric rebar designation system, the number in a bar size like 15M approximates the bar's diameter in millimetres, giving a direct sense of its actual cross-sectional size. It does not indicate the bar's length, the concrete cover distance required around it, or the bar's weight per metre, all of which are separate pieces of information found elsewhere on engineered drawings or in reference tables. Recognizing this convention lets a carpenter quickly gauge relative bar sizes on a drawing.
  87. The correct answer is A (Increased risk of moisture reaching and corroding the reinforcing steel): reduced concrete cover means less protective concrete stands between the outside environment and the embedded steel, which increases the risk that moisture and air eventually reach the steel and begin corroding it over time. This is a durability issue, not a strength or curing-speed benefit, so the other options describing faster curing or higher strength are incorrect. Corroding rebar can eventually crack and spall the surrounding concrete as rust products expand, making this a serious long-term defect.
  88. The correct answer is D (Dowels set to project from one pour into the other): dowels are steel bars set to project from the already-cured wall into the location where the new slab will be poured, physically tying the two separate pours together across the cold joint so they act more like one connected structure. A sill gasket, release agent, and screed all serve entirely different purposes and play no role in structurally connecting two separate concrete pours. Without dowels, a cold joint relies only on the weak bond between old and new concrete.
  89. The correct answer is C (Strip footing paired with an insulated concrete form (ICF) wall): an insulated concrete form system uses stay-in-place foam formwork that is filled with concrete and remains in place afterward as built-in exterior insulation, exactly matching the description of a conventional basement wall with built-in insulation replacing standard plywood forms. A slab-on-grade has no basement wall at all, a pile foundation addresses poor soil conditions rather than insulation, and a preserved wood foundation uses treated lumber rather than concrete and foam formwork. ICF has become common in Canadian residential basement construction specifically for this insulation benefit.
  90. The correct answer is B (Slab-on-grade foundation poured directly on grade with no basement): a slab-on-grade foundation is poured directly at or near ground level with no basement space beneath it, which matches a simple garage addition in an area with a shallow frost depth and no interest in below-grade space. A strip footing with a full basement wall, a deep pile foundation, or a preserved wood foundation over a crawlspace all involve more excavation, structure, or below-grade space than this situation calls for. Slab-on-grade is a common, economical choice for exactly this kind of simple structure.
  91. The correct answer is A (Pile foundation, using piles driven or drilled to bearing soil): where surface soils are weak and sloped terrain makes reaching solid ground with a conventional footing impractical, piles are driven or drilled down through the poor soil until they reach a stable bearing stratum, transferring the building's load to that deeper, stronger layer. A slab-on-grade or strip footing both still rely on adequate near-surface bearing capacity, which this site does not have. A preserved wood foundation resting on a shallow gravel pad would face the same weak-soil problem as a conventional footing.
  92. The correct answer is D (A preserved wood foundation (PWF) resting on a gravel pad): a preserved wood foundation uses pressure-treated lumber and plywood built on a gravel footing pad instead of poured concrete, which is a recognized, code-permitted alternative particularly useful where trucking in ready-mix concrete is difficult or costly, such as a remote cabin site. Pile foundations, ICF strip footings, and monolithic slabs all still depend on delivered concrete in some form. PWF construction has a long track record in remote and rural Canadian building for exactly this reason.
  93. The correct answer is B (A narrower footing can carry the same building load): dense gravel with high bearing capacity can safely support more load per square foot than soft clay, so a narrower footing on that site can still spread the building's load within safe limits, unlike the wider footing that soft clay would require for the same load. High bearing capacity does not automatically require a deeper frost depth or a thicker foundation wall, since those are governed by separate factors like climate and structural design. Confirming soil bearing capacity through a geotechnical report is exactly how a footing width gets sized appropriately for the site.
  94. The correct answer is C (Weeping tile, a perforated pipe collecting groundwater around the footing): weeping tile is the perforated pipe, typically wrapped in filter fabric and bedded in clear stone, that runs around the footing perimeter to collect groundwater and carry it away before it can build up pressure against the foundation wall. A sill gasket, keyway, and vapour barrier all serve entirely different functions elsewhere in the assembly. Weeping tile is a standard drainage component on foundations in most of Canada given typical groundwater conditions.
  95. The correct answer is A (Reduces heat loss while keeping the wall assembly warmer and drier): exterior rigid insulation reduces heat loss through the below-grade wall, and adding a drainage board over it directs groundwater down and away from the wall assembly rather than letting it sit against the insulation or wall surface, together keeping the assembly both warmer and drier. This combination does not replace the separate function of weeping tile at the footing, nor does it increase the required footing width. It also does not eliminate the need for damp-proofing, which addresses a different moisture pathway than surface drainage does.
  96. The correct answer is D (Brace the wall or frame the floor first, then backfill in stages with compaction): a wall with no lateral support at its top needs either temporary bracing or the main floor framing installed before backfilling begins, and even then backfill is placed in stages with compaction rather than dumped all at once, to avoid overloading the wall suddenly. Backfilling the full depth in a single pass with heavy equipment, or backfilling immediately regardless of bracing just to keep the schedule moving, both risk pushing the wall inward or cracking it. This staged, braced approach is standard practice specifically to protect an unsupported wall.
  97. The correct answer is C (Seal minor irregularities in the concrete and block air infiltration): a sill gasket is a thin foam strip laid along the top of the foundation wall beneath the sill plate to seal small surface irregularities in the concrete and block air infiltration at that joint. It does not provide the primary structural connection, which comes from the anchor bolts, and it does not replace the need for those anchor bolts either. It also does not waterproof the entire foundation wall, which is a separate, more extensive scope of work handled elsewhere in the assembly.
  98. The correct answer is B (Transfer wind and seismic loads from the framing down through the foundation): anchor bolts set into the concrete during the pour and later bolted through the sill plate are primarily there to transfer lateral wind and seismic loads from the framed structure above down through the sill plate into the foundation, holding the building down and in place. They play no role in controlling crack width or providing rebar cover within the wall itself. A keyway is a separate footing-to-wall detail and is not replaced by anchor bolts, which serve the sill-to-foundation connection instead.
  99. The correct answer is A (Approximately 4.53 cubic metres, after converting all three dimensions): converting 24 feet to about 7.315 metres, 20 feet to about 6.096 metres, and 4 inches to about 0.102 metres, then multiplying length by width by thickness gives roughly 4.53 cubic metres. The other options represent common calculation errors, such as doubling or halving the correct figure or dropping a conversion step partway through. This base volume would still need a waste allowance added before placing the actual concrete order.
  100. The correct answer is D (5 to 10 percent above the calculated base figure): a typical waste allowance added to a calculated concrete volume for a footing is around 5 to 10 percent, covering minor over-excavation, uneven subgrade, small amounts of form leakage, and normal spillage during placement. Ordering with no extra volume at all risks running short mid-pour, which is a serious problem with concrete's limited working time, while a 25 to 30 percent or 50 percent allowance is far more than normal practice requires and wastes material and money. Getting this allowance right on the order avoids both a costly extra delivery and unnecessary overage.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (To resist the outward pressure of wet concrete pushing the two form faces apart): Form ties run through the wall thickness and connect the two form faces so they cannot spread apart under the hydrostatic pressure of the fresh pour. Walers then distribute that tie load evenly across the sheathing rather than concentrating it at individual fasteners. Without ties, the pressure of the wet concrete would push the panels outward and blow out the form.
  2. The correct answer is B (Seasonal frost heave will crack the footing and everything built on it): Water in the soil beneath a footing that sits above the frost line expands as it freezes and contracts as it thaws each season. That repeated movement heaves the footing up and down, cracking the footing and everything built on top of it. This is exactly why the National Building Code and provincial codes set minimum footing depths based on local frost penetration data.
  3. The correct answer is C (Walers, horizontal members that distribute tie load evenly across the sheathing): Walers run along the outside of the form panels, distributing the tie load evenly across the sheathing rather than letting it concentrate at individual fasteners. Strongbacks are the vertical members added over the walers on tall or heavily loaded forms. Spreaders and chairs serve entirely different purposes in footing forms and rebar placement.
  4. The correct answer is D (Strongbacks, vertical members that keep the wall form panels straight and rigid): Strongbacks are added over the walers on tall or heavily loaded forms to keep everything straight and true. They make the whole formwork assembly act as one rigid unit rather than each panel flexing independently under the pressure of the pour. Spreaders hold footing forms apart, chairs support rebar, and dowels tie separate pours together, none of which brace a wall panel.
  5. The correct answer is B (They are pulled out as the pour reaches each one): Spreaders are temporary blocks set between the form walls to hold the correct footing width until the concrete is placed. They are pulled as the pour reaches them, since leaving them embedded would create a weak spot with no continuous concrete section. Form stakes and diagonal bracing, not spreaders, are what resist the lateral pressure of the wet concrete.
  6. The correct answer is A (Undisturbed soil holds the stakes firmly so the forms cannot bow or blow out): Loose backfill gives a stake far less holding power than compacted, undisturbed soil, letting it shift under the lateral pressure of the wet concrete. Stakes driven into undisturbed soil, spaced closely and braced diagonally where needed, resist that pressure and keep the form walls straight. A bowed footing form gives an inconsistent footing width that can compromise the bearing capacity the footing was sized to deliver.
  7. The correct answer is D (It sets and strips faster while giving a more consistent finish): Prefabricated panel systems pin together on site and are reused hundreds of times before wearing out, which is why most commercial and larger residential work has shifted to them. They set and strip faster than built-in-place forms and give a more consistent finished surface. They still need release agent, ties, and bracing just like built-in-place forms; those requirements do not disappear.
  8. The correct answer is C (To spread the building's load over more soil area and stay within bearing capacity): Footing size is determined by the load the wall and everything above it delivers, divided by the allowable bearing capacity of the soil. Making the footing wider than the wall spreads that load over enough soil area to stay within the soil's safe bearing capacity. Weaker soils need proportionally wider footings than strong soils to carry the same load without excessive settlement.
  9. The correct answer is B (To keep the concrete from bonding to the form face and give a smoother finish): Form materials, especially plywood facing panels, should be cleaned of residue and coated with a release agent before every reuse. This keeps the concrete from bonding to the form face, protects the panel from moisture damage, and gives a smoother finished surface. Well-maintained forms treated this way can be reused dozens of times, which matters directly to material costs on a multi-unit job.
  10. The correct answer is D (Striking off excess concrete to establish a flat, level surface): Slab-on-grade formwork is generally just edge forms set to the finished floor elevation, used as a screed guide. A screed, a straight board or specialized tool, is dragged across the top of the edge forms to strike off excess concrete and establish a flat, level surface before finishing tools take over. A slump test happens before placement, and curing compound and dowels are separate steps unrelated to screeding.
  11. The correct answer is A (Any lean in a vertical element like a column shows immediately once poured): Column forms are typically round or square sonotubes or reusable steel and fibreglass forms that must be perfectly plumbed and braced. Any lean in a vertical, exposed element like a column shows immediately once the form is stripped, unlike a small deviation hidden inside a wall assembly. Plumbness has no effect on cure time, cover requirements, or the need for release agent.
  12. The correct answer is C (They must be built with tighter tolerances to hold the stepped profile accurately): Formwork for stairs is more specialized than a footing or wall form because it must hold concrete in a sloped, stepped configuration rather than a simple plane. Stringer and riser forms need to be built accurately and braced to hold that stepped shape true, since any drift distorts every rise and run in the flight. Volume, slump, and stripping time are not automatically reduced just because the shape is more complex.
  13. The correct answer is D (Pressure increases because the concrete stays fluid longer before it can begin to set): Form pressure increases with the rate of pour and decreases as temperature rises, because warmer concrete sets faster and behaves more like a solid sooner. A mass pour on a cold day, filled quickly with a pump, generates far higher pressure on the formwork than the same wall poured slowly on a warm afternoon. Formwork specified for one set of conditions can fail if the pour rate or temperature changes without adjusting bracing and tie spacing.
  14. The correct answer is A (An inconsistent footing width that can compromise the bearing capacity it was sized for): Form stakes need to be spaced closely and braced diagonally where needed to resist the lateral pressure of wet concrete without bowing or blowing out. A bowed footing form gives an inconsistent footing width, which can compromise the bearing capacity the footing was engineered to deliver. Neither strength, cure rate, nor the need for dowels changes because a section of form bowed outward.
  15. The correct answer is B (Adding strongbacks and adequate tie spacing to keep the panels rigid over the full height): A taller wall pour builds up more hydrostatic pressure over its height before the lower portion has a chance to stiffen. Strongbacks over the walers, combined with tie spacing suited to that pressure, keep the whole assembly acting as one rigid unit rather than flexing panel by panel. Anchor bolt count and the sill gasket have no bearing on the formwork's ability to resist that pressure, and raising slump only increases the pressure problem.
  16. The correct answer is C (Lock the wall to the footing as one unit against lateral and shear forces): A keyway, a groove formed into the top of the footing while still wet, and dowels projecting up from the footing both lock the wall to the footing. Together they make the wall and footing act as one unit and resist lateral and shear forces trying to slide the wall off its base. Weeping tile drainage, concrete quantity, and form stripping are unrelated to what a keyway accomplishes.
  17. The correct answer is A (A built-in-place form, framed with plywood sheathing nailed to studs and walers): Built-in-place forms use plywood sheathing nailed to a frame of vertical studs and horizontal walers, essentially built like a stud wall on each side of the wall location. Prefabricated panel systems instead use modular steel or aluminum panels that pin together on site rather than being framed from raw lumber. A slab edge form is simple perimeter lumber for flatwork, not a wall form at all.
  18. The correct answer is C (Within about 24 hours, once the concrete has taken its initial set): Footing forms, carrying no load beyond the concrete's own weight and no longer structural once the concrete has taken initial set, can often be stripped in about 24 hours under normal conditions. This is far shorter than the 28-day period used as the benchmark for full design strength, since stripping time depends on the load the form must support after removal. Actual stripping time should always be confirmed against the project's engineering requirements and local code, especially in cold weather.
  19. The correct answer is B (24 to 48 hours, long enough to resist minor handling damage): Wall forms are typically left in place for 24 to 48 hours in normal conditions, long enough for the concrete to support its own weight and resist minor handling damage. This is longer than a simple footing form needs but far shorter than the time required for shoring under a suspended slab that must carry construction loads. Stripping too soon, before the concrete reaches this point, can let the wall sag, crack, or lose its finished shape.
  20. The correct answer is D (The slab must gain enough strength on its own to safely span and carry load): A footing carries only its own weight once it sets, while a suspended slab must eventually span between supports and carry construction loads on its own strength. Shoring stays in place until the concrete develops enough strength to safely take over that job, which can take a week or more, longer still in cold weather. Sill plate installation and the general 28-day benchmark are unrelated to when shoring can safely come out.
  21. The correct answer is C (Extend the stripping time well beyond the normal summer numbers): Hydration is a chemical reaction that slows dramatically in cold weather, so concrete gains strength much more slowly than it does in summer conditions. Cold temperatures slow curing, so stripping times in winter need to be extended well beyond the normal summer numbers, not shortened or left unchanged. A carpenter should never strip forms early just to keep a schedule, since a form stripped too soon can let the concrete sag, crack, or lose its shape.
  22. The correct answer is B (Increase the footing width to spread the load over a larger soil area): Footing size is determined by the load divided by the allowable bearing capacity of the soil, so weaker soils like soft clay need wider footings to spread the same load over more area. Increasing wall height would add load rather than relieve it, and footing depth relates to frost protection and stripping time, not bearing area. Clay is actually especially prone to frost effects, so its frost depth requirement is never reduced.
  23. The correct answer is A (The bar's diameter in millimetres, roughly 16 mm for a 15M bar): Canadian rebar sizing uses a metric bar designation system such as 10M, 15M, 20M, and 25M, where the number approximates the bar's diameter in millimetres. A 15M bar is roughly 16 millimetres in diameter, larger than a 10M bar at roughly 11.3 millimetres. Yield strength, bar spacing, and concrete cover are all separate specifications called out independently on the engineered drawings.
  24. The correct answer is D (Cover, which protects the steel from moisture and corrosion): Rebar has to sit at the correct depth within the concrete, called cover, which protects the steel from moisture and corrosion while positioning it where it does the most structural good. Chairs and spacers hold the rebar cage at that specified height and keep it from settling during the pour. Slump, embedment of anchor bolts, and set time are all separate concepts unrelated to rebar cover.
  25. The correct answer is B (Inadequate cover and a weaker section than the design intended): Chairs and spacers hold the rebar cage up off the form face or the ground at the specified height and keep it from settling during the pour. If the rebar settles or rests directly on the form, it leaves inadequate cover and a weaker section than the design intended. Slump, cold joints, and footing size are unrelated to whether the rebar cage is properly supported at the correct height.
  26. The correct answer is D (Lifted into the middle or upper portion of the slab thickness): Welded wire mesh is commonly used in slabs-on-grade to control crack width, and it needs to be lifted into the middle or upper portion of the slab thickness during the pour. Mesh left lying on the ground does almost nothing structurally, since it ends up below the depth where it can resist cracking. It is not draped on edge forms or tied to footing dowels, which serve entirely different purposes.
  27. The correct answer is C (To tie the footing and wall together across the cold joint as one unit): Dowels are short lengths of rebar set to project out of one pour so they can be embedded into a second, adjoining pour. This ties the two pours together structurally and transfers loads across the cold joint, keeping the footing and wall acting as one continuous unit. Dowels have nothing to do with the sill plate, the mix's water-cement ratio, or substituting for a keyway, which is a separate detail dowels usually work alongside.
  28. The correct answer is A (Concrete is strong in compression but weak in tension): Concrete is strong in compression, resisting a crushing or squeezing force, but weak in tension, resisting a pulling or bending force. A plain concrete beam can crack and fail under a load that puts its underside in tension even though the concrete is more than strong enough in compression. This is exactly why steel rebar is embedded in concrete, to carry the tension loads the concrete alone cannot handle.
  29. The correct answer is D (A cold joint, the seam where an earlier pour had already hardened): A cold joint is the seam where an earlier pour has already hardened before the next pour is placed against it, such as a footing poured on one day and the wall poured on top of it later. Dowels are used to tie the two pours together structurally and transfer loads across that seam. Control joints and expansion joints are cut or formed into a single continuous slab to control cracking, and a chair is a rebar support, not a joint type.
  30. The correct answer is A (Steel is positioned near the tension face since it does the most structural good): Rebar has to sit at the correct depth within the concrete, called cover, positioning it where it does the most structural good, typically near the tension face. Since a footing bends with its underside in tension under soil pressure, the steel is placed near that bottom face to resist the pulling force concrete alone cannot handle. Form stripping and concrete quantity have no bearing on where the rebar is positioned within the section.
  31. The correct answer is B (The wall and footing may slide or shear apart since nothing locks them together): A keyway and dowels both lock the wall to the footing so the two act as one unit and resist lateral and shear forces trying to slide the wall off its base. Without either detail, the cold joint between the two pours has nothing tying it together beyond the friction and bond of the concrete alone. Slump, 28-day strength, and the sill gasket are unaffected by whether a keyway or dowels were used at the footing.
  32. The correct answer is C (Frost penetration depth varies significantly by region and is set locally by code): The National Building Code of Canada and provincial codes set minimum footing depths based on local frost penetration data, and these depths vary significantly across the country. Depths are relatively shallow in milder coastal regions and can be well over a metre in parts of the Prairies and the North. Frost depth is not one national number, so a carpenter needs to confirm the requirement for the specific municipality on every job.
  33. The correct answer is B (To collect groundwater and carry it away before it builds up hydrostatic pressure on the wall): Foundation drainage, commonly called weeping tile or drain tile, is a perforated pipe laid around the footing perimeter, wrapped in filter fabric and bedded in clear stone. It collects groundwater and carries it away before it can build up hydrostatic pressure against the wall. Anchor bolts, seismic reinforcement, and rebar cover are entirely separate details unrelated to a drainage system.
  34. The correct answer is C (A continuous waterproofing membrane rated for hydrostatic pressure): Damp-proofing is a bituminous coating that resists ordinary moisture migration, while a waterproofing membrane is a more robust, continuous barrier used where hydrostatic pressure or a high water table is expected. A site with a regularly high water table needs that continuous membrane rather than simple damp-proofing alone. Rigid insulation, mix slump, and footing width address heat loss, workability, and bearing capacity, not resistance to hydrostatic water pressure.
  35. The correct answer is D (The unbraced wall can be pushed in or cracked with no lateral support yet): A foundation wall poured and cured on its own has no lateral support at the top until the main floor system is framed on top of it. Backfilling against an unbraced wall too early, or too aggressively with heavy equipment, can push the wall in or crack it before that support exists. Standard practice is to brace the wall temporarily, or get the floor framing in place first, and backfill in stages with compaction.
  36. The correct answer is A (Insulated concrete form (ICF), foam units left in place as insulation): Insulated concrete form, or ICF, is a stay-in-place foam formwork system filled with concrete that also provides built-in insulation, commonly used to build the foundation wall on top of a strip footing. A preserved wood foundation instead uses pressure-treated lumber and plywood on a gravel pad, with no poured concrete wall at all. Pile foundations carry loads through individual driven or drilled elements, an entirely different foundation type.
  37. The correct answer is C (A pile foundation, driven or drilled down to a stable bearing stratum): Pile foundations use individual concrete, steel, or timber piles driven or drilled down to a stable bearing stratum, used where surface soils are too weak or unstable to support a conventional footing. Simply widening a strip footing does not solve the problem when the surface soil itself lacks adequate bearing capacity at any width. A slab-on-grade or preserved wood foundation still ultimately bears near the surface, which does not address weak, unstable surface soils.
  38. The correct answer is A (A slab-on-grade foundation with a thickened edge poured directly on grade): Slab-on-grade foundations skip the full foundation wall and basement, pouring a single thickened-edge or monolithic slab directly on grade. This is common where a basement isn't wanted, in warmer climates with shallower frost depths, or for garages and additions. A pile foundation and a preserved wood foundation use entirely different construction methods, and a full basement wall would extend well above the footing rather than sitting close to grade.
  39. The correct answer is D (Preserved wood foundation (PWF), framed lumber resting on a gravel pad): Preserved wood foundations use pressure-treated lumber and plywood, built much like a conventional framed wall, resting on a gravel footing pad rather than a poured concrete footing. They remain a recognized, code-permitted option, particularly in remote locations where trucking in ready-mix concrete is difficult or expensive. ICF, monolithic slab, and pile foundations all still depend on poured concrete, which is exactly the constraint a PWF avoids.
  40. The correct answer is B (Somewhere for water inside the concrete to expand into when it freezes): Air-entrained concrete has millions of microscopic air bubbles distributed through the mix, giving the water inside somewhere to expand into when it freezes. Without that relief space, ordinary concrete exposed to repeated freeze-thaw cycles, especially with de-icing salt present, will scale and spall within a few winters. Air entrainment is required by code for exterior flatwork, driveways, and exposed foundation walls across most of the country.
  41. The correct answer is A (Compressive strength decreases as the water-cement ratio rises above the design value): The water-cement ratio is the single biggest factor controlling both strength and workability, and a higher ratio weakens the final product even though it makes the mix easier to place. A carpenter should never let a crew add extra water to a load just to make it easier to pour, since every litre added beyond the design mix lowers compressive strength. Adding water raises slump rather than lowering it, and it does not shorten the curing time needed before stripping.
  42. The correct answer is D (Workability, indicating how stiff or fluid the mix is): Workability is checked on site with a slump test, and the amount the concrete settles after the cone is lifted tells you how stiff or fluid the mix is. A low slump suits flatwork that needs to hold a finish, while a higher slump flows more easily into narrow wall forms and around dense rebar cages. The slump test does not directly measure compressive strength, entrained air content, or the water-cement ratio, though those factors influence the result.
  43. The correct answer is B (A retarder, which slows set time in hot weather or long pours): Retarders slow set time in hot weather or on large pours needing extended finishing time, which is exactly the situation a hot summer afternoon pour with a large finishing crew creates. An accelerator does the opposite, speeding up set time, which is useful in cold weather rather than hot. Air-entraining agents address freeze-thaw durability and a water reducer alone addresses workability, neither of which buys the crew extra finishing time before set.
  44. The correct answer is C (About 1.13 cubic metres, the product of the three converted dimensions): Multiplying the three converted dimensions together, 12.19 x 0.457 x 0.203, gives approximately 1.13 cubic metres. This is the raw volume before a waste allowance of 5 to 10 percent is added to cover minor over-excavation, uneven subgrade, form leakage, and normal spillage. Running short mid-pour and forcing a second truck with a cold joint partway through the footing is worse than ordering slightly more than this bare minimum.
  45. The correct answer is D (The building sliding sideways under wind or seismic load): The sill plate transfers the building's weight into the foundation and resists the building sliding sideways under wind or seismic load. Anchor bolts, spaced and embedded per code requirements, tie the framing to the foundation so those lateral loads transfer properly down through the structure. Wall settlement, rebar shifting during the pour, and footing spread are unrelated to what the sill plate connection resists once framing is underway.
  46. The correct answer is B (It seals minor irregularities in the concrete and blocks air infiltration): A sill gasket, a foam strip, is laid along the top of the foundation wall to seal minor irregularities in the concrete surface and block air infiltration between the foundation and the wood framing above. It does not carry structural load and does not replace the requirement for pressure-treated lumber, since the sill plate still sits in direct contact with concrete and moisture. Frost depth protection is a separate footing detail, not something a sill gasket affects.
  47. The correct answer is A (On a footing sized for the load the post delivers to it): A post or column under a beam is sized for the load it carries and must land on a footing sized for that load, not on a slab or unsupported subfloor. This is the load path in miniature: floor load into the beam, beam load into the post, post load into the footing, footing load into the soil. Landing the post on subfloor sheathing, a rim joist, or unsupported slab breaks that chain at exactly the point the exam expects a candidate to recognize.
  48. The correct answer is C (A girder, the term used when a beam runs through the building's centre): A beam is sometimes called a girder when it runs through the centre of the building, providing the intermediate support floor joists need beyond a short span running wall to wall unsupported. A rim joist caps the ends of the field joists around the perimeter rather than supporting them from below. Jack studs and cripple studs are wall-framing members around openings, unrelated to floor joist support.
  49. The correct answer is B (They must match the exact nail or screw type and pattern the manufacturer specifies): Joist hangers and ledgers must be fastened with the exact nail or screw type and pattern the manufacturer specifies, since a connector is only as strong as its fasteners. Substituting the wrong nail reduces the hanger's rated capacity even if the connection looks solid, which is a common trap on the exam. Toe-nailing does not substitute for the specified hanger nailing, since metal connectors exist specifically because toe-nailing alone is far less reliable.
  50. The correct answer is D (A rim joist, sized the same as the joists it caps rather than treated as trim): A rim joist, or band joist, caps the ends of the field joists, ties the floor system together as a unit, and provides a nailing surface for sheathing and the wall framing above. It also transfers wall loads from the storey above into the joists and beam below, so it is sized the same as the joists it caps, not treated as trim. A ledger, header joist, and sole plate all describe different framing members that do not match this perimeter, load-transferring role.
  51. The correct answer is B (Staggering prevents a single weak point from running through the entire beam): Lining every joint up at the same point along the beam would create one location where none of the plies overlap, weakening the beam right there. Staggering the joints means at least one full-length piece is always continuous across any given point, so the beam acts as a single strong member. This staggered-joint logic is the same principle used in double top plates and subfloor sheathing.
  52. The correct answer is D (The post load has no footing to carry it into the ground): A post under a beam is only as good as what is beneath it, and the load path requires floor load into the beam, beam load into the post, and post load into a footing sized for that load. Landing a post on subfloor with no footing breaks that chain at the last link. The load has to reach the soil through a footing, not through unsupported sheathing.
  53. The correct answer is A (The substituted nails reduce the hanger's rated load-carrying capacity): A joist hanger's rated capacity is tested and published using the exact nail type and pattern the manufacturer specifies. Substituting a different nail, even one of similar length, changes the shear and withdrawal strength of the connection from what was tested. A connector is only as strong as its fasteners, and this substitution is a common trap on the exam.
  54. The correct answer is C (10 studs, one at each end of the run): A 12 foot wall is 144 inches long, and dividing by 16 inch on centre spacing gives 9 equal spaces, which requires 10 studs counting the one at each end. This end-to-end counting method, spaces plus one, is the standard way to lay out a straight run of wall from a chalk line. The same logic applies whether spacing is 16 or 24 inches on centre.
  55. The correct answer is B (The rim joist ties the floor system together and carries wall loads down): The rim joist caps the ends of the field joists, ties the entire floor system into one working unit, and provides a nailing surface for sheathing and the wall above. It also transfers the wall loads from the storey above down into the joists and beam below it. Because it carries real structural load, it is sized the same as the joists it caps, never treated as decorative trim.
  56. The correct answer is A (It keeps the joists from twisting or rolling sideways under load): Bridging and blocking run perpendicular between joists at intervals along the span specifically to prevent each joist from twisting or rolling sideways as load is applied. This lets the floor system act as one connected unit rather than each joist deflecting independently under a point load. Without this bracing, a floor can feel bouncy or uneven even when every joist is individually sized correctly.
  57. The correct answer is D (Solid blocking between the joists to give the wall a bearing surface): When a load-bearing wall above runs parallel to the joists but does not land directly on top of one, blocking is installed between the joists beneath it so the wall's load has solid wood to bear on. Without this blocking, the wall would be resting only on subfloor sheathing, which is not designed to carry that concentrated line load. This is the same load-path thinking that applies to beams landing on footings.
  58. The correct answer is C (16 studs, one at each end of the run): A 20 foot wall is 240 inches long, and dividing by 16 inch on centre spacing gives 15 equal spaces, requiring 16 studs when counting the stud at each end of the run. This spaces-plus-one method holds for any wall length that divides evenly into the chosen spacing. It is the same calculation a carpenter runs before ordering stud packs for a job.
  59. The correct answer is B (It closes small gaps and adds composite action between panel and joist): Construction adhesive run along the top of each joist before sheathing is set closes small gaps that would otherwise let the floor squeak underfoot. It also creates composite action between the sheathing and the joist, meaning the two work together structurally rather than the sheathing simply riding on top. This is why glue and screw, or glue and nail, is standard practice rather than fastening alone.
  60. The correct answer is D (The gap allows room for the panels to swell as they absorb moisture): Both plywood and OSB take on job-site moisture and swell slightly, so a small expansion gap is left between panel edges to give that swelling somewhere to go. Without this gap, panels can buckle or crown at the joints as they absorb moisture. This is separate from the staggered end joints, which is a different practice serving a different purpose.
  61. The correct answer is C (Load on the floor tends to straighten a properly crowned joist over time): Installing a joist's natural upward bow facing up means the weight of the floor and everything on it tends to press that bow flat over time rather than push an already-sagging joist further down. Installing a joist crown-down instead lets gravity add to the sag rather than correct it. Checking and orienting the crown before nailing off is a basic step every crew follows during joist layout.
  62. The correct answer is A (6 studs, one at each end of the run): A 10 foot wall is 120 inches long, and dividing by 24 inch on centre spacing gives 5 equal spaces, which requires 6 studs when counting the stud at each end. The same spaces-plus-one method used for 16 inch spacing applies here at the wider 24 inch spacing. Wider spacing simply means fewer total studs for the same wall length.
  63. The correct answer is D (A trimmer joist, doubled because it now carries extra shared load): The joist cut short by a floor opening becomes a trimmer joist along the sides, and it is doubled because it now carries load that would otherwise have been shared across the joist it replaced. This mirrors header framing in walls, where interrupting a structural member means something else must be added and sized for the tributary area it now picks up. The header joist across the ends is also doubled and hung from the trimmers on hangers.
  64. The correct answer is B (The anchored backspan balances the load hanging past the support): Cantilevered joists continue back into the supported floor as one continuous piece so the anchored backspan balances the load hanging out past the support, the way a seesaw needs weight on both sides of the pivot. Tacking on a separate short piece at the rim would provide no backspan to counterbalance the overhanging load. Cantilever length is limited by joist depth and backspan for exactly this reason.
  65. The correct answer is A (It keeps the joists from rolling sideways under the concentrated load): Blocking installed over the point of support on a cantilevered floor keeps the joists from rolling sideways where the load becomes concentrated at that bearing point. This is the same twisting and rolling protection bridging and blocking provide elsewhere in a floor system, applied specifically at the highest-stress point of a cantilever. Skipping this blocking leaves that concentrated load point unbraced.
  66. The correct answer is C (10 studs, one at each end of the run): An 18 foot wall is 216 inches long, and dividing by 24 inch on centre spacing gives 9 equal spaces, requiring 10 studs when counting the stud at each end. This same spaces-plus-one calculation applies regardless of whether the wall is bearing or non-bearing, as long as the spacing itself is appropriate for the wall's load. Confirming the spacing is correct for a load-bearing wall is a separate check from simply counting studs.
  67. The correct answer is D (It gives the crew a safe, level surface to build and tilt up walls): Platform framing builds a complete, flat deck at each storey before the walls for that storey go up, giving the crew a safe, level working surface instead of ladders or scaffolding. Wall sections are often built flat on that deck and tilted up, which is faster and more accurate than building in place. This flat-deck advantage is one of the main reasons platform framing became the Canadian standard.
  68. The correct answer is A (The floor platform between storeys acts as a firestop, slowing fire spread): Because each storey's walls sit on their own completed floor platform, that platform physically interrupts the wall cavity between storeys and acts as a firestop, slowing fire spread compared to a continuous open cavity. Balloon framing's continuous studs from basement to attic create exactly the open-cavity fire path platform framing breaks up. This fire-spread difference is one of the key reasons balloon framing was replaced.
  69. The correct answer is B (Balloon framing, the older method replaced by platform framing): Continuous studs running from the sill straight up to the roof line, with floor joists hung off the side of the studs rather than bearing on a completed lower wall, is the defining feature of balloon framing. It was replaced mainly because its continuous open stud cavities create a fire-spread risk and because long, straight studs are hard to source. Recognizing this older method matters mainly for renovation work in older buildings.
  70. The correct answer is C (22 studs, one at each end of the run): A 28 foot wall is 336 inches long, and dividing by 16 inch on centre spacing gives 21 equal spaces, requiring 22 studs when counting the stud at each end. Longer walls simply extend the same spaces-plus-one calculation used on shorter runs. A carpenter uses this method to order stud packs before framing begins.
  71. The correct answer is A (It anchors the wall's base and gives studs a consistent layout reference): The sole plate sits on the subfloor and is nailed down into the joists or blocking below it, anchoring the wall's base and giving every stud above it a consistent nailing surface and layout reference. Every stud location is measured and marked from the sole plate before the wall is framed. This makes accurate sole plate layout the foundation for the whole wall's accuracy.
  72. The correct answer is C (It ties wall sections together across their joints as a horizontal tie): A double top plate with deliberately offset joints ties intersecting and abutting wall sections together across their individual joints, letting the top plate act as a horizontal tie that spreads load from above across more than one stud bay. If both layers' joints lined up at the same spot, that location would have no overlap tying the sections together. This is the same staggered-joint logic used in built-up beams and subfloor sheathing.
  73. The correct answer is D (It gives a nailing surface for sheathing and drywall on both faces): At an outside corner, studs need to provide a nailing surface for the outside sheathing and the inside drywall of both intersecting walls, and a single stud cannot do that on all four surfaces. A three-stud corner uses a full stud plus two turned studs to create nailing surfaces on both faces of the corner. A California corner achieves a similar result with fewer studs but relies on drywall clips or blocking instead.
  74. The correct answer is B (Header, the horizontal member spanning across the opening): A header is the horizontal member above an opening that carries the load which would otherwise pass down through the missing studs, spanning between the king studs or jack studs that flank the opening. Its size depends on the opening's span and the load bearing down on it from above. This diagram shows the header positioned exactly where it belongs, directly over the door opening.
  75. The correct answer is C (It leaves more room in the cavity for insulation to be installed): A California corner uses drywall clips or blocking instead of a full three-stud corner, which leaves more of the corner cavity open for insulation rather than filled with solid wood. A solid three-stud corner is difficult to insulate effectively because the wood fills most of the cavity. This tradeoff between structural nailing surface and insulation space is a common consideration on energy-efficient builds.
  76. The correct answer is B (It gives drywall a solid nailing surface across the wall joint): Where a partition meets another wall, backing in the form of extra studs or blocking is framed in specifically so drywall has a solid surface to nail into across that intersection. Without this backing, the drywall edge at the joint would have nothing solid behind it to fasten to. This is a routine layout step at every T-intersection in a framed building.
  77. The correct answer is A (Cripple studs, short members maintaining layout spacing near openings): Cripple studs are the short studs installed above a header or below a window sill that maintain the regular stud spacing through that zone without running the wall's full height. They keep the layout consistent for sheathing and drywall fastening even though they are shorter than the full-height studs around them. King studs, by contrast, run the wall's full height and jack studs support the header directly.
  78. The correct answer is D (King stud, the full-height member on each side of the opening): A king stud runs full height from the sole plate to the top plate on each side of an opening, staying uninterrupted, and carries the header's load down to the sole plate and the floor below. It is positioned at the outer edge of the opening framing, outside the jack stud. This diagram shows exactly that full-height, uninterrupted position beside the window opening.
  79. The correct answer is B (The wider, more heavily loaded opening needs a deeper or stronger header): Header size depends on both the span of the opening and the load bearing down on it from above, so a wide patio door carrying a full storey and roof load needs a deeper or stronger header than a narrow closet doorway carrying only its own wall's weight. Treating every opening the same regardless of span or load ignores this relationship. Span tables are used to select the correct header for the specific combination of span and load.
  80. The correct answer is C (It carries the header's load down to the sole plate and floor below): A king stud's job is to take the header's reaction load and carry it, uninterrupted, all the way down to the sole plate and the floor framing below. The jack stud is what directly supports the header's ends, while the king stud carries that load onward to the foundation-side framing. Together they form the full load path around the opening.
  81. The correct answer is A (It directly supports the ends of the header above the opening): A jack stud, also called a trimmer stud, is nailed to the inside face of the king stud and runs from the sole plate up to the underside of the header, directly supporting the header's ends. The king stud beside it then carries that combined load the rest of the way down to the sole plate. Wider or more heavily loaded openings may call for doubled or tripled jack studs to spread that reaction load.
  82. The correct answer is D (Jack stud, the shorter member supporting the header's end): A jack stud, or trimmer stud, is nailed to the inside face of the full-height king stud and runs from the sole plate up to directly beneath the header, supporting its ends. It sits inside the king stud rather than running the wall's full height itself. This diagram shows that shorter member positioned exactly where a jack stud belongs beside the door opening.
  83. The correct answer is C (Doubled jack studs spread the header's reaction load over more bearing area): A heavier header carrying a wide opening's load transfers a larger reaction force down through the jack studs at each end, and doubling those jack studs spreads that reaction load over more bearing area than a single jack stud could handle. This does not change the required header depth, which is still selected from span and load. Doubled or tripled jack studs are called for specifically when the load at that point exceeds what one stud can safely carry.
  84. The correct answer is A (It allows clearance for shimming the unit plumb and level and for sealing): A rough opening is always built slightly larger than the window or door unit itself so there is room to shim the unit plumb and level during installation and to insulate and air seal around its perimeter afterward. An opening cut to the exact unit size would leave no room to adjust the unit into a true plumb and level position. An undersized or out-of-square rough opening is a common source of callbacks.
  85. The correct answer is B (A rough sill, supported on cripple studs beneath it): Below a window opening, a rough sill spans between the jack studs and is supported on cripple studs beneath it, giving the window unit a bearing surface at the bottom of the opening.
