It is a horizontal member, but distinct in name and location from the header above the opening. Cripple studs beneath the sill maintain the wall's regular stud spacing down to the sole plate.
  86. The correct answer is D (11.25 inches, matching the dimension labelled on the header): The diagram labels the header's depth directly on the drawing above the window opening, and reading that dimension as shown gives 11.25 inches. This is a reading task rather than a calculation, similar to reading the correct value straight off a printed span table on the exam. The other listed depths are plausible header sizes but do not match the dimension actually labelled on this diagram.
  87. The correct answer is A (Load-bearing, since it appears to carry structural load from above): A load-bearing wall is defined by carrying structural load from above, floor joists, roof framing, or another wall, down to the framing or foundation below it, and joists landing directly on top of a wall along its length is exactly that condition. Interior walls can absolutely be load-bearing, since bearing status depends on what lands on the wall, not on whether it is interior or exterior. This finding should be confirmed further before any renovation work proceeds, but it points clearly toward load-bearing.
  88. The correct answer is C (A partition wall, since nothing structural depends on it staying in place): A partition wall divides space but carries only its own weight, with nothing structural above it depending on the wall remaining in place, which matches a wall with no joists or roof framing bearing on it. This is different from a load-bearing wall, which carries real structural load down through it. Not every interior wall is load-bearing, and not every wall automatically requires a double top plate.
  89. The correct answer is B (A broken load path, since the load above has nothing to bear on): Loads generally travel straight down through the framing, so a second-storey bearing wall that does not line up with a bearing wall, beam, or adequately sized joist below it creates a broken load path even though both storeys look identical from a finished room. The load does not disappear when the path is misaligned, it finds another way down through framing that was never designed to carry it. Recognizing this kind of hidden misalignment is exactly what the exam tests when it asks about load path.
  90. The correct answer is D (6 feet 9 inches, matching the dimension labelled for the opening): The diagram labels the rough opening height directly on the drawing next to the door opening, and 6.75 feet reads as 6 feet 9 inches. This is a reading task, similar to reading a dimension straight off a printed drawing rather than calculating it from other values. The other listed heights do not match the dimension actually labelled on this diagram.
  91. The correct answer is C (The load path must be traced and a replacement beam and posts engineered first): Removing a load-bearing element correctly means identifying the full load path first, then engineering a replacement, typically a new beam and posts sized to carry that load to the foundation, before the original wall comes out. Simply removing the wall and patching drywall afterward ignores where the load above was actually going. A lack of visible cracking beforehand does not confirm the wall is safe to remove without engineering.
  92. The correct answer is B (Often OSB, providing high stiffness relative to the joist's weight and depth): An I-joist's thin web, often made from OSB, connects the solid or laminated flanges and gives the joist high stiffness relative to its own weight and depth compared to dimensional lumber. This is one reason engineered I-joists commonly show up on longer floor spans than dimensional lumber can achieve at a similar depth. The I-shape puts most of the material at the top and bottom flanges where bending stress is highest.
  93. The correct answer is D (A defect in one veneer layer is diluted across many other layers): LVL is built from many thin veneer layers glued together with the grain running the same direction, so a defect such as a knot in one veneer is diluted across the many other layers rather than concentrated the way a knot can weaken a single piece of solid-sawn lumber. This manufacturing process is what gives LVL its consistent, tested strength values. It is a different mechanism than fire resistance or the glue itself carrying the load.
  94. The correct answer is A (The design specifically calls for this spacing with lumber and sheathing sized for it): 24 inches on centre is permitted on a load-bearing wall only in situations where the design specifically calls for it, typically using engineered lumber and thicker sheathing able to span the wider gap. It is not the default assumption for a bearing wall, so the carpenter should confirm the design supports this spacing rather than assuming it is automatically acceptable. Standard 16 inch spacing remains the default unless the design says otherwise.
  95. The correct answer is C (PSL's manufacturing process suits it to heavily loaded beams and posts): Parallel strand lumber is made from long strands of wood glued together under pressure, and this manufacturing process produces a product specifically suited to heavily loaded beams and posts. It is not the lightest engineered wood option, and it still relies on properly detailed structural connectors like any other beam or post material. Its strength comes from testing rather than species and grade alone, the same shared advantage engineered wood products have over solid-sawn lumber.
  96. The correct answer is D (Glulam suits long spans and has a finished appearance when left exposed): Glue-laminated timber is built from layers of dimension lumber glued face to face, making it common for long-span beams that are also exposed and finished-looking, which suits a great room ceiling left visible. It does not need to be covered by drywall, and it is built from multiple glued layers rather than a single solid timber. Its combination of long-span capacity and finished appearance is exactly why architects specify it for visible structural beams.
  97. The correct answer is B (Removing material outside the manufacturer's guidelines can remove the structural work): Engineered members like LVL generally cannot be notched or drilled outside the manufacturer's published guidelines, because removing material from the wrong location can remove exactly the material doing the structural work. Solid-sawn lumber has more forgiving, code-based notching and drilling rules that do not directly apply to engineered products. LVL can still be cut and fastened within the manufacturer's allowed zones, it is not off-limits entirely.
  98. The correct answer is A (It aligns cleanly with standard 4-foot-wide sheet goods, giving consistent panel support): Standard 16 inch on centre spacing is the default choice because it works cleanly with standard 4-foot-wide sheet goods, giving panel edges consistent support without excess cutting. This is a sheathing-layout reason, not simply a reduction in the number of studs, since 16 inch spacing actually uses more studs than 24 inch spacing. Wider 24 inch spacing is permitted in some situations but is not the default assumption for a wall unless the design calls for it.
  99. The correct answer is C (The connection's actual capacity drops below what the design assumed): A connection is only as strong as its weakest fastener, so using fewer or smaller nails than the code-specified nailing schedule reduces the connection's actual capacity below what the structural design assumed, even if the framing looks solid. This drop in capacity happens regardless of whether the wall is load-bearing or not, since every specified connection is designed to a required strength. Following the exact nailing schedule is what keeps the built connection matching the design.
  100. The correct answer is B (A connector resists uplift forces that toe-nailing resists poorly): A rated metal connector such as a hurricane tie is designed to resist uplift forces the wind generates on a roof, a direction toe-nailing resists poorly because it relies on wood not splitting, a consistent angle, and friction in a small area of wood. Toe-nailing is more sensitive to installer technique and provides less reliable capacity in the uplift direction specifically. This is why rated connectors are now standard at truss-to-plate connections in high wind uplift regions.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (A gable roof, since every common rafter matches in length across the whole roof): Two sloped planes meeting at one ridge with triangular gable end walls is the defining shape of a gable roof, and because every common rafter spans the same run at the same pitch, they can be laid out and cut in identical batches. A hip roof instead slopes on all four sides with no gable ends. A shed roof has only one sloping plane and no ridge at all.
  2. The correct answer is A (The unit rise, the vertical rise in inches for every 12 inches of horizontal run): Roof pitch is always written as unit rise over the fixed unit run of 12 inches, so a 6/12 pitch rises 6 inches vertically for every 12 inches travelled horizontally. Total rise and total run describe the whole roof, not the single unit the pitch notation is built from. Rafter length is a separate value calculated from unit rise and unit run together.
  3. The correct answer is B (Approximately 12 feet 7 13/16 inches, found using the Pythagorean theorem with total run and rise): total rise equals 12 feet multiplied by 4 divided by 12, which is 4 feet, so rafter length equals the square root of 12 squared plus 4 squared, the square root of 160, about 12.65 feet. That converts to 12 feet 7 13/16 inches. Simply adding run and rise, or using run alone, both skip the actual diagonal distance the rafter travels.
  4. The correct answer is C (A hip roof, with hip rafters running from each corner up to the ridge): sloping on all four sides with diagonal hip rafters at each corner instead of flat gable ends is what defines a hip roof, and the added hip and jack rafters are why it takes more layout skill than a plain gable. A shed roof has only one plane and no ridge. A gable roof has vertical triangular end walls instead of sloped hip corners.
  5. The correct answer is D (The unit run, the fixed 12 inch horizontal reference marked on a framing square's body): the unit run is always 12 inches, a foot, and a carpenter marks that 12 inch increment off the framing square's body, which is 24 inches long, when stepping off pitch on site. Total run and total rise describe the whole roof's actual dimensions, not the fixed reference unit. Rafter length is calculated from unit rise and unit run together, not stated by the 12 itself.
  6. The correct answer is B (Approximately 13 feet 5 inches, found from the total run and the total rise): total rise equals 12 feet multiplied by 6 divided by 12, which is 6 feet, so rafter length equals the square root of 12 squared plus 6 squared, the square root of 180, about 13.42 feet, or 13 feet 5 inches. Doubling the run or using run alone both ignore the actual sloped diagonal the rafter follows.
  7. The correct answer is A (A gambrel roof, with a steep lower slope and a shallower upper slope per side): the two-slope-per-side profile, steep below and shallow above, is the classic barn shape and is what maximizes usable space in the attic or upper storey. A hip roof slopes on all four sides at one consistent pitch, not two different pitches per side. A shed roof has only a single plane and no such transition at all.
  8. The correct answer is D (15 feet, half the building span, from the wall plate to the ridge centreline): total run for a simple gable roof is always half the building span, since each common rafter only travels from the outside wall plate to the centreline of the ridge, not across the full width. Using the full span or doubling it would describe both roof planes together, not the run of one single rafter.
  9. The correct answer is C (A hip rafter, running diagonally from an outside corner up to the ridge): a hip rafter forms the sloped outside corner edge of a hip roof, running diagonally from an outside building corner to the ridge. A valley rafter is the mirror case at an inside corner, where two roof sections meet at a low point instead of a high corner. A common rafter runs square to the building, not diagonally.
  10. The correct answer is A (Approximately 14 feet 5 1/16 inches, from the square root of run squared plus rise squared): total rise equals 12 feet multiplied by 8 divided by 12, which is 8 feet, so rafter length equals the square root of 144 plus 64, the square root of 208, about 14.42 feet, or 14 feet 5 1/16 inches. Ignoring the rise or arbitrarily adding and doubling values does not follow the actual Pythagorean relationship.
  11. The correct answer is B (A shed roof, with a single sloped plane and no ridge board at all): a shed roof has only one sloping plane, so there is no ridge and no second plane to frame against, making it the simplest and fastest roof shape to lay out and calculate. A hip roof needs hip and jack rafters on four sloped sides. A gambrel roof needs two different slopes framed per side.
  12. The correct answer is D (A valley rafter, running from the inside corner where two roof sections meet): a valley rafter runs diagonally from an inside building corner, where two roof sections meet at a low point, up to the ridge, which is the mirror opposite of a hip rafter's outside-corner position. A common rafter runs square to the building rather than diagonally. A jack rafter is a shortened piece that lands against the hip or valley rafter, not the diagonal member itself.
  13. The correct answer is C (Hip jack rafters, shortened commons that step down in length toward the hip): a jack rafter running up to a hip rafter is a hip jack, a shortened common rafter that steps down in length by a consistent increment the further it sits from the full-length common beside it. Valley rafters and common rafters are different, longer diagonal or full-length members. A ridge board is a horizontal member at the peak, not a sloped rafter at all.
  14. The correct answer is B (Within the comfortable residential range of approximately 7 to 7.5 inches per riser): 108 inches divided by 15 risers gives exactly 7.2 inches per riser, which sits inside the comfortable neighbourhood of roughly 7 to 7.5 inches that residential stair practice targets. It is neither dangerously tall nor unusually short. Riser height paired with tread depth is exactly what sets how natural a person's stride feels on a staircase.
  15. The correct answer is A (A mansard roof, with a steep near-vertical lower slope on all four sides): the near-vertical lower slope on all four sides paired with a shallow or flat upper section is what defines a mansard roof, closely related to a four-sided gambrel. A hip roof slopes at one single consistent pitch on all four sides rather than two different slopes. A gable roof has flat triangular end walls, not sloped sides all around.
  16. The correct answer is D (7 feet, found by multiplying total run by unit rise, divided by unit run): total rise equals total run multiplied by unit rise, divided by the unit run of 12, so 14 feet multiplied by 6, divided by 12, equals 7 feet. The unit rise number alone, 6, is not the total rise for the whole run. Multiplying total run by unit rise without dividing by 12 badly overstates the actual rise.
  17. The correct answer is C (Approximately 12 feet 1/4 inch, from the square root of run squared plus rise squared): total run is half the 20 foot span, 10 feet, and total rise equals 10 feet multiplied by 8 divided by 12, about 6.67 feet, so rafter length equals the square root of 10 squared plus 6.67 squared, about 12.02 feet, or 12 feet 1/4 inch. Using the full span or adding run and rise directly both skip the actual diagonal calculation.

Leaving out the rise, or adding and doubling values, does not follow the true diagonal relationship.
  19. The correct answer is B (The hip rafter is longer than a common rafter at the same pitch): because a hip rafter covers more horizontal distance in plan view to reach the same total rise, the same Pythagorean relationship that governs a common rafter produces a longer diagonal length. A carpenter does not need a memorized constant for this, only the understanding that a longer run at the same pitch always produces a longer rafter. Rafter length is directly tied to the run it covers, not independent of it.
  20. The correct answer is D (The plumb cut, the angled cut at the top where the rafter meets the ridge): the plumb cut is laid out at an angle on the flat stock but ends up truly vertical once the rafter is tilted into position against the ridge board. The seat cut, or birdsmouth, is a different notch cut into the underside of the rafter at the wall plate, not at the top. The tail cut is at the far outer end, past the seat cut entirely.
  21. The correct answer is A (The seat cut, or birdsmouth, the notch where the rafter bears on the plate): the seat cut, commonly called the birdsmouth, has a level part resting flat on the plate and a shorter plumb part against the plate's outside face, and must be cut carefully so removing too much material does not weaken the rafter at its bearing point. The plumb cut is a separate cut at the rafter's top end. The tail cut is past the seat cut entirely, at the outer end.
  22. The correct answer is C (Steeper and more cramped, since 8.0 inches is a taller riser than 7.2 inches): 96 inches divided by 12 risers gives an 8.0 inch riser height, which is taller than the 7.2 inch comparison staircase, and a taller riser generally pairs with a shallower tread to keep proportion, producing a steeper, more cramped feeling stride. It would not feel longer or more relaxed. Riser height directly affects how natural a stride feels, so the two stairs are not equivalent.
  23. The correct answer is B (A level cut, one of several forms a plumb, level, or combination tail cut can take): the tail cut at the rafter's outer end can be plumb, level, or a combination of the two depending on how the fascia and soffit are meant to finish at that eave. A ridge cut is not a recognized rafter cut term; the plumb cut at the ridge is simply called the plumb cut. The birdsmouth is a separate notch located at the wall plate, not at the rafter tail.
  24. The correct answer is D (The ridge board should be at least as wide as the rafter's full plumb cut depth): sizing the ridge board at least as wide as the full depth of the rafter's plumb cut lets the entire cut face bear against the board while leaving enough material for proper nailing. Matching it to the wall plate width or making it narrower than the rafter would leave part of the plumb cut unsupported. The rafter's plumb cut depth is exactly what drives this sizing decision.

Using the run alone, or arbitrary sums and doublings, ignores the true diagonal path of a 12/12 pitch rafter.

  28. The correct answer is B (An engineered roof truss, a factory-built triangulated component sized to the job's load): an engineered truss is factory-built and triangulated, designed by an engineer or truss designer to span the full building width without interior bearing walls, with webs and chords sized to that specific job's load and span. A common rafter is stick-framed and cut individually on site, not factory engineered. A ridge board is simply a horizontal peak member, not a triangulated spanning component.
  29. Treads never outnumber risers or exceed the riser count, and they are not simply half the riser count either.
  30. The correct answer is A (Temporary bracing, holding the trusses upright and spaced until sheathing is applied): temporary bracing is what holds a freshly set truss run upright, plumb, and correctly spaced during installation, before sheathing ties the whole roof together, and missing or undersized bracing is a real hazard that can let an unbraced run topple. Permanent bracing per the truss drawings is installed once the roof is complete, not as a substitute for temporary bracing during installation. Trusses need bracing during this stage, not none at all.
  31. The correct answer is D (It is not permitted without stamped engineering approval, since one web supports the whole system): truss members, chords and webs alike, cannot be cut, notched, or otherwise modified in the field without stamped engineering approval, because a truss is a complete triangulated system and cutting even one web can remove load path the whole assembly depends on. Webs carry real structural load too, not just the chords. Real roof failures have resulted from exactly this kind of unapproved field cut.

  33. The correct answer is A (Trusses go up faster and free the floor plan of load-bearing walls below): a whole truss roof can be craned into place and braced in a day instead of cut and assembled piece by piece, and trusses free the floor plan below of load-bearing walls dictated by a ridge or interior support, which is why they are the default on most production work. Material cost is not the advantage being described here. Trusses actually need more care around field modification than stick framing, not less.

Running panels parallel to the trusses would have each sheet bear on far fewer members. Orientation does matter, even with correct nailing, because it affects how many members each sheet spans.

This applies to every riser along the full run, not only the top and bottom ones. It also applies regardless of whether the stringer is open or closed.

Neither practice is about nail pops, cost savings, or cosmetics. The gap manages expansion, not moisture control on its own, which is handled separately through ventilation and underlayment.

Two soffits or two ridge vents alone do not create that low-to-high flow. Insulation alone does not manage attic moisture without airflow.

Ridge board sizing and sheathing nailing pattern have no bearing on this thermal pattern. Pitch alone does not cause ice damming without the ventilation and heat-escape factor behind it.

Jumping straight to 2x12 skips the smaller size that already satisfies the job, and spacing clearly does affect the listed span.
  90. The correct answer is C (A deeper joist has greater section depth, increasing its bending resistance): a taller member resists bending under the same load far more effectively than a shallower one of the same species and grade. Depth is not tied to lumber grade, and a deeper joist does not weigh less, if anything it weighs more. A deeper joist also does not require closer spacing to perform, since its greater bending resistance is exactly what allows longer spans at the same spacing.
  91. The correct answer is B (D.Fir-L No.2, rated for the longer of the two listed spans): the table lists D.Fir-L No.2 at 12 feet 4 inches versus S-P-F No.2 at 11 feet 8 inches for the identical 2x8 size and spacing, showing D.Fir-L allows the greater span. The two spans are not equal, ruling that option out. Roof pitch affects rafter length calculations elsewhere but is not needed to compare two rows within the same span table.
  92. The correct answer is D (The No.3 grade permits more defects, giving it lower design strength values): lumber grading allows a lower grade to contain more knots, wane, and other defects, which reduces its allowable design values and shortens the safe span for the same size and spacing. Grade does not change the species or the actual milled dimensions of the board. On-centre spacing in a table row is a separate variable the framer chooses, not something the grade itself requires to be wider.
  93. The correct answer is A (12 feet 6 inches, the value listed for 24 inch spacing): reading directly across the row for 24 inch on-centre spacing in the table shown gives a maximum allowable span of 12 feet 6 inches. The 12 inch and 16 inch spacing rows list longer spans of 16 feet 6 inches and 15 feet 3 inches, since closer spacing allows a longer span for the same size and grade. Selecting the wrong row is the most common error when reading a table like this under exam time pressure.
  94. The correct answer is C (That species group generally has greater inherent structural strength and stiffness): different wood species groups have different inherent bending strength and stiffness values, which is why span tables list them separately even for the identical member size, grade, and spacing. Insect resistance is a durability property, not what a span table is measuring. Grading systems and spacing requirements are consistent across species within the same table, not tied to which species shows the longer span.
  95. The correct answer is D (2x8, since it is rated for 13 feet 4 inches at this spacing): the table shows 2x8 at 16 inch on-centre spacing is rated for 13 feet 4 inches, which meets and exceeds the required 12 foot span, while the 2x6 row is only rated for 10 feet 2 inches. Ceiling joists are structurally sized by span exactly like floor and roof framing members. Since the 2x8 row already satisfies the requirement, no size beyond what is listed is needed.
  96. The correct answer is B (The joist size is adequate, since its rated span exceeds the required span): a joist whose listed maximum span is longer than the actual required span has margin to spare, so the size satisfies the job. There is no need to move up to a larger size when the current one already clears the requirement. Roof load is unrelated to a floor joist span check, and deliberately undersizing a member to match the required span more closely is not standard trade practice.
  97. The correct answer is A (2x10, since it is rated for 16 feet 2 inches at this spacing): the table shows 2x10 pressure treated S-P-F No.2 at 12 inch on-centre spacing is rated for 16 feet 2 inches, which meets and exceeds the required 15 foot span, while the 2x8 row is only rated for 12 feet 9 inches. A 2x10 is a normal, common choice for exterior deck framing when the table supports it. Pressure treated exterior lumber carries a wet-service reduction compared to the same size and grade used dry indoors, so its allowable span is set slightly lower than an equivalent interior floor joist table.
  98. The correct answer is C (Built-up 2-2x12, rated for a span of 9 feet 2 inches): the table shows the built-up 2-2x12 header is rated for 9 feet 2 inches at the 6 foot supported width, which meets and exceeds the required 8 foot opening. The 2-2x10 falls short at 7 feet 9 inches, and the 2-2x8 falls further short at 6 feet 2 inches, so neither smaller size is adequate. Supported width clearly affects the listed span, since a header carrying a wider tributary load is rated for a shorter span at the same size.
  99. The correct answer is D (8 feet 6 inches, the value listed for 24 inch spacing): reading directly across the row for 24 inch on-centre spacing in the table shown gives a maximum allowable span of 8 feet 6 inches. The 12 inch and 16 inch spacing rows list longer spans of 11 feet 4 inches and 10 feet 4 inches, since wider spacing at 24 inches shortens the allowable span for the same size and grade. Reading the wrong row is the most common error when working with a table like this.
  100. The correct answer is B (The No.3 grade permits more defects, giving it lower design strength values): the table shows the No.3 row rated for 13 feet 1 inch versus 15 feet 3 inches for the No.1/No.2 row, because the lower grade allows more knots and other defects, reducing its allowable design values. The actual milled dimensions and species group stay the same across grade rows for the same size. On-centre spacing is a separate variable chosen by the framer, not something the grade itself dictates.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Roof framing to bearing walls, to floor framing, to foundation, to soil): Load travels downward through every connected structural member in sequence. Roof load transfers into the bearing walls below it, those walls pass combined roof and wall load into the floor framing, and the floor framing carries it to the foundation and ultimately the soil. Skipping the floor framing or routing load through non-structural members like sheathing breaks this chain.
  2. The correct answer is C (The removed wall was actually load bearing and its load path was never replaced): A wall that looks like a simple partition can still be carrying structural load from above. Removing a load bearing element without first engineering a replacement, typically a new beam and posts, leaves that load with nowhere planned to go, and it finds another path through framing never designed to carry it. The sagging ceiling is the visible sign of that broken load path, not a sheathing or fastening issue.
  3. The correct answer is B (Sixteen studs, one at each end plus one every sixteen inches along the run): The wall is 240 inches long, and dividing by the 16 inch spacing gives 15 equal spaces. Placing a stud at each end of every space requires one more stud than the number of spaces, for a total of 16 studs. This count excludes any additional studs needed at corners, partitions, or openings.
  4. The correct answer is D (A king stud running full height with a jack stud carrying the header's ends): The king stud runs uninterrupted from the sole plate to the top plate, tying the opening into the wall framing and carrying wall load above the header down to the sole plate. The jack stud is nailed to the inside face of the king stud, runs up to the underside of the header, and is the member that directly bears the header's reaction load, transferring it down to the sole plate. Together they transfer the header's reaction load to the sole plate, which is why wider or heavier openings sometimes call for doubled jack studs.
  5. The correct answer is A (6 feet, since a 6:12 pitch rises 6 inches for every 12 inch run): A 6:12 pitch means the roof rises 6 inches vertically for every 12 inches of horizontal run. Over a 12 foot run, that ratio applies twelve times, giving a total rise of 72 inches, or 6 feet. The overhang extends the roof surface but is not part of the run used to calculate rise to the ridge.
  6. The correct answer is B (12.5 feet, found by applying the 9:12:15 triangle ratio across the 10 foot run): A 9:12 pitch forms a right triangle where the rafter length per foot of run is 15 divided by 12, or 1.25 feet of rafter for every foot of run. Multiplying that 1.25 factor by the 10 foot run gives a common rafter length of 12.5 feet, not counting the overhang or any ridge and tail cuts. The rise alone or the run alone cannot substitute for this diagonal calculation.
  7. The correct answer is C (Wind generated uplift forces trying to lift the roof framing away from the wall): A hurricane tie connects a rafter or truss to the wall's top plate specifically to resist the uplift forces wind can generate on a roof. Toe nailing resists this uplift direction poorly, since it relies on friction and shear that wind loads can overcome. A rated metal connector engages a full pattern of fasteners engineered for that specific load direction, unlike a connection that only looks adequate.
  8. The correct answer is D (Solid blocking between the joists so the wall has solid wood to bear on): When a load bearing wall runs parallel to the joists below it but does not land directly on one, the joist bays under that wall need solid blocking installed between them. This blocking gives the wall's load a solid bearing surface instead of resting on unsupported sheathing spanning between joists. An extra rim joist, added sheathing layer, or a full steel beam are not the standard fix for this specific condition.
  9. The correct answer is B (Water vapour diffusing from the warm interior air condensing on the cold sheathing): In most of Canada the interior air is warmer and holds more moisture than the exterior air for much of the year, so vapour naturally moves from inside toward outside through the wall assembly. A vapour barrier stops that diffusion before it reaches a cold surface like the back of the sheathing, where it would otherwise condense into liquid water inside the wall. Bulk rainwater and wind pressure are handled by other layers, not the vapour barrier.
  10. The correct answer is A (Air leakage can carry large amounts of moisture even when the vapour barrier is intact): Air sealing and vapour control are related but genuinely separate concerns, and moving air can carry far more moisture through a small gap than diffusion ever could through solid material. An intact vapour barrier does not guarantee an intact air barrier if penetrations like electrical boxes are left unsealed. The unsealed penetration, not the drainage plane on the exterior, is the likely source of this specific damage.
  11. The correct answer is D (It sheds bulk water while still remaining permeable enough to let vapour dry outward): House wrap is a specific engineered product chosen because it stops bulk liquid water from reaching the sheathing while still allowing any vapour that does get into the wall cavity to dry outward. A solid plastic sheet would block that outward drying entirely, trapping moisture inside the wall. Cost and window specific use are not what distinguishes house wrap from an ordinary poly sheet in this role.
  12. The correct answer is C (It gives any water past the cladding a path to drain and dry): The rain screen air gap created by strapping gives any water that gets past the cladding a clear path to drain down and out at the bottom rather than sitting trapped against the sheathing. It also allows the wall to dry by ventilation, which is standard practice in many climate zones. It does not replace house wrap, reduce fastener counts, or change the vapour barrier's rating.
  13. The correct answer is A (Each upper layer overlaps the layer below it so water sheds outward and down): House wrap courses, roof shingles, and step flashing pieces are all installed shingle lap, meaning each upper course or piece overlaps the one below it. This ensures water running down the surface always meets an overlap that sheds it outward rather than a seam that directs it behind the material. None of these materials are installed starting from the top, and the shared logic is about overlap direction, not adhesive or primer.
  14. The correct answer is D (Tape is what actually seals the seams against wind driven rain infiltration): Staples or cap nails hold the house wrap in place, but the fasteners themselves do not seal the seams. Every horizontal and vertical seam needs to be taped with a compatible sheathing tape once the wrap is fastened, since tape is what actually keeps wind driven rain from working its way through the overlaps. Skipping this step leaves the drainage plane vulnerable regardless of how well the wrap is nailed off.
  15. The correct answer is B (Water running down the wrap will be directed into the seam and behind it): House wrap has to be installed shingle lap, with each higher course overlapping the one below it by at least 6 inches, so water always runs down and over the layer beneath. Reversing that order, with an upper course tucked behind a lower one, sends every drop of water running down the wrap straight into the seam and behind the barrier. Taping after the fact does not fully correct a fundamentally reversed lap direction, and this error has nothing to do with the interior vapour barrier.
  16. The correct answer is C (The flaps to fold into the opening, covering the sill, jambs, and head): The modified I shaped cut with diagonal relief cuts at the corners lets the carpenter fold the resulting flaps into the rough opening so the sill, jambs, and head are all covered by house wrap before any flashing tape goes on. This preparation happens before the window itself is installed. It has nothing to do with reusing material, trimming the window, or skipping strapping at openings.
  17. The correct answer is A (Increase the overlap to at least 6 inches on both horizontal and vertical seams): Each horizontal roll of house wrap needs to overlap the one below it by at least 6 inches, and vertical seams need the same minimum 6 inch overlap. A 2 inch overlap does not provide reliable protection against wind driven rain finding its way into the seam. Relying on the siding or caulking instead of a proper overlap is not an acceptable substitute for correct house wrap installation.
  18. The correct answer is B (The cap nails hold the wrap in place while tape seals the seams): Staples or cap nails hold the house wrap in place against the sheathing, but they are not what seals the seams. Tape applied over the shingle lapped seams is what actually keeps wind driven rain out. The cap nails do not create the rain screen gap on their own, and house wrap itself is the water resistive barrier rather than a replacement for it.
  19. The correct answer is D (The tear creates a gap in the drainage plane where bulk water can reach the sheathing): House wrap forms the drainage plane that stops bulk water from reaching the sheathing, and a tear breaks the continuity of that plane at exactly the point where it occurs. Siding covering the tear does not restore its function, since siding is not itself the primary water resistive barrier. The location of the tear relative to an opening does not change the need to repair it, and tears do not self seal.
  20. The correct answer is C (It completes the shingle lap so water sheds over the head flashing, not behind it): Folding the house wrap down over the head flashing last continues the same shingle lap logic used throughout the wall, so water running down the face of the wall meets an overlap that sheds it outward rather than a seam that funnels it behind the barrier. It is a separate step from caulking the frame perimeter or sealing the gap with foam, and it is not related to future window removal.
  21. The correct answer is B (Side jamb flashing overlapping the sill pan and running up both sides of the opening): The correct flashing sequence is sill pan first, then the window unit set into it, then side or jamb flashing overlapping the sill pan and running up both sides, and finally head flashing last, overlapping the top of the side flashing. Caulking and spray foam are separate steps in the process, not the next flashing layer to install. Skipping the side flashing before the head flashing would break the shingle lap water shedding sequence.
  22. The correct answer is A (It drains water that reaches the sill out the front rather than into the wall): A proper sill pan has back and end dams so that any water sitting on the sill drains out the front over the exterior wall surface rather than leaking backward into the wall cavity or sideways past the jambs. This detail does not change the rough opening size, does not remove the need for side flashing above it, and has no relationship to checking the opening for square.
  23. The correct answer is C (The opening is out of square, which will cause the window to rack once fastened): Checking that an opening is square by measuring both diagonals is standard practice, and matching diagonals confirm a square opening. Diagonals that do not match indicate the opening is out of square, and a window fastened into an out of square opening will rack and will not operate properly even if it appears fine sitting in the hole. This measurement does not test overall opening size, sill pan height, or house wrap preparation.
  24. The correct answer is D (The curing foam will generate enough pressure to bow the frame inward): Standard expanding foam can generate enough pressure as it cures to bow the window frame inward, causing the sash to bind and not operate smoothly. Low expansion window and door foam is formulated specifically to avoid this problem and should be applied in a thin bead rather than filling the whole cavity in one pass. This issue is about frame distortion, not rough opening size, house wrap performance, or the sill pan's drainage function.
  25. The correct answer is A (At the corners and at mid span points along the wider window's jambs): Shims go at the corners and mid span points on a wider window, and fasteners go through the shim locations so the frame is fully supported rather than fastened at random points along the jamb. Shimming only at the centre of the sill or only at the head corners would leave large sections of the frame unsupported. Fastener placement is directly tied to where the shims are located, not independent of it.
  26. The correct answer is B (Water that gets past the door sweep will pond against the framing below): A door sill needs its own pan flashing exactly like a window, because the threshold is a horizontal surface exposed to standing water and foot traffic wear. A sloped sill pan, sometimes combined with a drainage gap or weep holes, moves water that gets past the door sweep back outside instead of letting it pond against the framing. This issue is specific to threshold drainage, not the door slab, the king studs, or the rain screen gap.
  27. The correct answer is D (There will be no room left to shim the window plumb, level, and square): Rough openings are framed slightly larger than the actual unit, typically by about half an inch in width and height, specifically to allow room for shimming and adjustment. A rough opening cut to the exact window dimensions leaves no clearance to plumb, level, and square the unit during installation. This sizing issue is unrelated to the sill pan's dam function, house wrap preparation, or header sizing.
  28. The correct answer is C (The error will telegraph up the wall, becoming more visible with every course): Every course of siding above the starter strip locks into and builds on the one below it, so a starter strip that is out of level by even a small amount will telegraph that error up the entire wall. The problem becomes more visible with every additional course rather than self correcting or staying limited to the first course. J channel at the corners does not compensate for an out of level starter strip.
  29. The correct answer is A (The panel will have nowhere to expand and will buckle or warp visibly): Vinyl siding expands and contracts significantly with temperature, so the nailing hem uses slotted holes and the panel is meant to hang loosely on the nail rather than being pulled tight. Over-driving pins the panel in place, and when temperature swings cause the vinyl to expand, it has nowhere to go and will buckle or warp visibly. This is a panel movement problem specific to over-driven nails, not a J channel or starter strip issue.
  30. The correct answer is B (It allows the panel to expand and contract with temperature without buckling): Leaving about a nail's width of play, roughly 1/32 inch, between the nail head and the panel lets vinyl siding move as it expands and contracts with temperature. Driving nails straight and level through the centre of the slot keeps the panel aligned while still permitting this movement. This gap is about thermal movement, not nail count, starter strip level, or J channel overlap.
  31. The correct answer is C (A pre-formed corner post installed first, plumb, before any siding courses are hung): Vinyl and many engineered products use pre-formed inside and outside corner posts that the siding courses tuck into, with the corner post installed first, plumb, before any siding courses are hung. Wood and fibre cement siding are more commonly mitred at outside corners or run into corner boards instead, which is a different approach suited to those materials. Skipping a corner treatment and relying only on caulking is not standard practice for either material.
  32. The correct answer is D (It restricts the expansion room the siding and channel are designed to allow): J channel gives the cut ends of siding panels a finished, weathertight edge to tuck into, but like the siding itself it needs to allow for expansion rather than being caulked rigid all the way around, particularly with vinyl. Sealing it completely removes the room the material needs to move with temperature. This problem is about restricted movement, not the sill pan, the header, or the rain screen gap.
  33. The correct answer is B (It keeps every course's exposed reveal consistent and lands courses correctly at openings): A story pole is a length of scrap material marked out with the reveal spacing for the full wall height, used to keep every course consistent and make sure courses land correctly at window sills and door heads without an odd sliver course at a critical location. Consistent reveal is what separates an amateur looking job from a professional one. It has no connection to house wrap overlap, shingle nailing, or the rain screen gap depth.
  34. The correct answer is A (It installs faster and is more forgiving of minor irregularities in the framing): Wood and fibre cement siding are commonly mitred at outside corners for a clean finished look, or run into corner boards that the siding butts against, which is faster to install and more forgiving of minor irregularities in the framing than a tight mitred joint. This method still requires a starter strip and a rain screen gap like any other siding installation, and the corner boards still need to be installed plumb.
  35. The correct answer is D (It may not extend past the point above the interior wall line for ice damming): Ice and water shield is required at the eaves in the areas most vulnerable to ice damming, extending up from the edge to at least a point above the interior wall line, so that ice dams forming at the cold overhang cannot back water up under the shingles. A short run limited to the first 12 inches may not reach that point on many roofs. Ice and water shield is also required in valleys, not only at eaves, and this issue is unrelated to drip edge sequencing.
  36. The correct answer is C (Poor attic ventilation is letting heat escape and melt snow unevenly across the roof): Ice damming happens when heat escaping through the roof melts snow on the upper, warmer part of the roof while the eave stays cold, and the meltwater refreezes at the eave, building a dam. A cold, well ventilated attic that keeps the whole roof deck at a consistent temperature is the actual long term fix, with ice and water shield serving only as a backup layer under the shingles. Drip edge sequencing, starter course orientation, and exposure width do not address the underlying heat loss causing the dam.
  37. The correct answer is B (Drip edge typically goes under the underlayment at the eave and over it at the rakes): Drip edge installs at the eaves and rakes before or after the underlayment depending on the edge, typically under the underlayment at the eave and over it at the rakes, per manufacturer instructions. This sequencing directs water off the roof edge and away from the fascia rather than letting it wick back underneath. Underlayment is still required at the eave even where ice and water shield is also present, and drip edge is required at both edges, not only the rakes.
  38. The correct answer is A (The portion of each shingle left visible to the weather above the overlap): Shingle exposure describes how much of each shingle is left visible to the weather, with the rest overlapped by the course above, and standard three tab shingle exposure is commonly 5 inches, though the wrapper always specifies the correct figure for that product. Exposure is unrelated to ice and water shield width, drip edge projection, or nail count, which are each separate specifications on the same roof.
  39. The correct answer is D (It under-secures the shingle since the nail misses the course above's catch point): Nails must be placed within the nailing zone marked on the shingle, since nailing above it under-secures the shingle because it does not catch the course installed above the way the design intends. Nailing below the zone is also a problem, since the nail heads can show through and fail to catch that course properly. This nailing zone issue has no direct effect on exposure width, drip edge installation, or ice and water shield adhesion.
  40. The correct answer is C (It prevents water from finding a straight line path down through the layers): Offsetting the joints, the vertical cutouts between tabs, between successive courses so that no two courses in a row have a cutout lined up at the same location prevents water from finding a straight line path down through the layers. Manufacturer instructions specify the offset pattern, commonly a 6 inch offset from one course to the next. This detail does not change shingle count, exposure width, or the need for step flashing elsewhere on the roof.
  41. The correct answer is A (It cannot accommodate the differential movement between the roof and the wall over time): Roof to wall intersections and chimneys rely on step flashing, individual small pieces woven in with each course of shingles, because a continuous piece of flashing without step flashing looks fine when new but will leak eventually since it cannot accommodate the differential movement between the roof and the wall. Each step flashing piece overlaps the one below it and is overlapped by the shingle course above, maintaining the shingle lap logic. This failure mode is about long term movement, not exposure, drip edge, or the valley membrane.
  42. The correct answer is B (It provides a secondary water barrier beneath the shingles across the whole deck): Underlayment goes down over the roof sheathing before any shingles are installed, providing a secondary water barrier across the whole roof, while ice and water shield adds extra protection only in the areas most vulnerable to ice damming and concentrated water flow. Underlayment does not set the nailing zone, the course offset pattern, or the eavestrough slope, which are each determined separately.
  43. The correct answer is D (Water will pond in the trough and can freeze there over the winter): Eavestroughs need a slight slope toward the downspout outlet, roughly 1/4 inch of drop per 10 feet of run, so water does not pond and freeze in the trough over winter. A level trough has no consistent path for water to reach the downspout, leaving it standing in place. This ponding issue is about slope, not fascia strength, soffit ventilation, or the drip edge above it.
  44. The correct answer is C (Basement moisture problems and foundation heaving in a freeze thaw climate): Downspouts need to discharge well clear of the foundation, typically with an extension or splash block directing water at least 4 to 6 feet away, because water dumped right at the foundation is one of the most common causes of basement moisture problems and foundation heaving in a freeze thaw climate. This discharge distance issue is unrelated to the eavestrough's own slope, the wall's rain screen gap, or step flashing at the roof.
  45. The correct answer is B (It gives the eavestrough a solid mounting surface along the roof edge): Fascia boards cap the ends of the roof rafters or trusses at the eave, giving the eavestrough a solid mounting surface and closing off the roof edge. This is a separate function from the vapour barrier, shingle exposure, or the rain screen gap behind wall siding, each of which is handled by a different part of the assembly.
  46. The correct answer is A (It is the intake half, letting air flow along the underside of the roof deck): Soffit ventilation, whether continuous vented soffit or individual vent openings, is the intake half of the attic ventilation system, working together with ridge or roof vents to let air flow continuously along the underside of the roof sheathing. The ridge vent, not the soffit, serves as the exhaust half of that same system. Soffit venting does not replace ice and water shield, and it has no sealing role between the fascia and eavestrough hangers.
  47. The correct answer is D (Water will run down behind the flashing into the rim joist and framing): Ledger flashing typically includes a metal flashing cap over the top of the ledger, integrated with the house's water resistive barrier in the same shingle lap order, with the wrap over the flashing rather than under it, so water sheds out and away from the ledger to house connection. Reversing that order directs water down behind the flashing into the rim joist and framing, one of the most common sources of hidden structural rot in residential decks. This problem is about water direction, not joist hanger capacity, guard height, or joist spacing.
  48. The correct answer is C (Between 36 and 42 inches, depending on the elevation and jurisdiction involved): Guard height for any deck or porch elevated more than a code specified height above grade is regulated by the National Building Code of Canada and provincial building codes, generally requiring a minimum guard height in the range of 36 to 42 inches depending on the elevation and jurisdiction. Baluster spacing is also restricted so a sphere of a specified diameter cannot pass through. The lower ranges and the fixed 30 inch figure in the other options do not match this requirement.
  49. The correct answer is B (16 inches on centre, the common spacing for typical residential decking material): Joist spacing for deck framing follows span tables based on joist size, species, and spacing, commonly 16 inches on centre for typical residential decking, though wider spacing is possible with larger joists per the applicable span tables. This matches the same standard spacing commonly used in floor framing elsewhere in the house. The other spacing figures listed are not the standard default for typical residential decking.
  50. The correct answer is A (A sphere of a specified diameter must not be able to pass between the balusters): Guard height requirements come with baluster spacing restrictions so that a sphere of a specified diameter cannot pass through the guard, a detail meant to prevent a young child from being able to fit through the opening. This spacing rule is independent of the joist spacing below the deck, the ledger board's fastener pattern, and any siding starter strip, which each serve entirely different purposes elsewhere in the structure.
  51. The correct answer is B (The water-resistive barrier, commonly house wrap, over the sheathing): the water-resistive barrier sits directly outside the structural sheathing and forms the drainage plane that sheds bulk water while still letting vapour dry outward. The vapour barrier belongs on the interior warm side of the insulation, not outside the sheathing. Soffit panels and ice and water shield are roofing and trim components, not general wall layers.
  52. The correct answer is D (Water running down the wall will be directed into the seam and behind the barrier): reversing the shingle-lap direction means water flowing down the face of the wrap meets an upper course tucked behind a lower one, funneling it straight into the seam. This defeats the entire purpose of the drainage plane and lets bulk water reach the sheathing. The error has nothing to do with vapour diffusion or the rain screen gap itself, only with the direction water is shed at the seam.
  53. The correct answer is A (Sill pan flashing, with back and end dams, set into the rough opening): the sill pan goes in first because the sill is where any water that gets past the window collects, and every later flashing piece has to overlap onto it. The window unit then sits in the sill pan, followed by side flashing overlapping the sill pan, then head flashing last. Installing head or side flashing before the sill pan would break the shingle-lap water-shedding order.
  54. The correct answer is C (Centre nails in the slot, leaving about a nail's-width of play): the slotted nailing hem is designed so the panel hangs loosely on the nail, roughly 1/32 inch of play, allowing the panel to slide as it expands and contracts with temperature. Driving nails flush or to one end of the slot pins the panel in place. When temperature swings then occur, a pinned panel has nowhere to go and will buckle or warp.
  55. The correct answer is C (5 inches of each shingle exposed to the weather): standard three-tab shingles are typically installed with a 5 inch exposure, with the remainder of the shingle overlapped by the course above. Exposure does vary by manufacturer and product line, which is why the shingle wrapper always states the correct figure for that specific product. Using an exposure that is too large reduces the overlap and weakens the roof's resistance to wind-driven rain.
  56. The correct answer is A (Water volume from both roof planes concentrates and flows through the valley): a valley collects runoff from two intersecting roof planes at once, so it carries far more water than a typical field area and needs the extra protection of ice and water shield underneath. Ice damming can occur at the eave too, not only in valleys, so option D overstates the case. Sun exposure and nailing method are not the reasons valleys get this membrane.
  57. The correct answer is D (About 1/4 inch of drop for every 10 feet of run): eavestroughs need a slight slope toward the downspout outlet, roughly 1/4 inch of drop per 10 feet of run, so water flows to the outlet instead of standing and freezing in the trough. A level trough with no slope will pond water and is prone to ice buildup over winter. A steeper slope than needed is unnecessary and can look visibly crooked along the fascia.
  58. The correct answer is B (The house wrap should lap over the top of the ledger flashing): the metal flashing cap goes over the top of the ledger, and the house wrap is then integrated so it laps over the flashing, not under it, keeping the same shingle-lap order used everywhere else in the envelope. Water running down the wall then sheds out and away from the ledger-to-house connection rather than behind it. Cutting the wrap away or leaving a gap would create an unprotected path for water to reach the rim joist.
  59. The correct answer is D (Between 36 and 42 inches, depending on jurisdiction): guard height for a deck or porch elevated more than the code-specified height above grade generally falls in the 36 to 42 inch range, with the exact figure depending on elevation and jurisdiction. Baluster spacing is separately restricted so a sphere of a specified diameter cannot pass through the guard. The lower ranges given in the other options fall short of what most provincial codes actually require at this elevation.
  60. The correct answer is B (It is the intake half, letting outside air enter along the roof edge): soffit ventilation, whether continuous vented soffit or individual vent openings, works together with ridge or roof vents by supplying the intake air that flows continuously along the underside of the roof sheathing. Ridge and roof vents provide the exhaust half of the same system, not the soffit. Soffit venting is a ventilation component, not a vapour or air barrier control layer.
  61. The correct answer is C (It directs water off the roof edge and away from the fascia): drip edge is formed metal flashing installed at the eaves and rakes specifically to direct water clear of the roof edge and away from the fascia, rather than letting it wick back underneath. It is installed under the underlayment at the eave and over it at the rakes, per manufacturer instructions, but that sequencing serves the same water-directing purpose. It is not what anchors valley membrane or seals a plane-to-plane joint.
  62. The correct answer is A (It accommodates differential movement between the roof and the wall over time): individual step flashing pieces, each overlapping the one below and the shingle course above, can flex slightly relative to each other, which lets the assembly handle differential movement between roof and wall. A continuous piece looks fine when new but cannot accommodate that movement and will eventually leak. Step flashing is woven in as the roof is installed, not added afterward, and it does not eliminate the value of proper counter-flashing at masonry.
  63. The correct answer is A (Every course above locks into the one below, so any error is telegraphed upward): because each siding course above the starter strip locks into and builds on the course below it, even a small out-of-level error in the starter strip becomes more visible with every course added above it. This applies to vinyl, wood, fibre cement, and engineered siding alike. The starter strip is not a structural load-bearing element, and it is not the only piece needing caulking.
  64. The correct answer is C (Allow the J-channel to accommodate expansion rather than caulking it rigidly): like the siding itself, J-channel needs to allow for expansion and contraction, particularly with vinyl, so it should not be caulked rigid all the way around. It still needs proper mechanical fastening, not adhesive alone, and it trims out siding around the full perimeter of an opening, not just the bottom. Caulking it rigidly would defeat the expansion allowance the material is designed with.
  65. The correct answer is B (Before any siding courses are hung, installed plumb from the start): the corner post is installed first, plumb, before any siding courses are hung, because every course of siding on both walls tucks into that post as it is installed. Installing it after courses are already up, or tying its timing to J-channel or eavestrough work, would make it impossible to properly tuck the siding ends into the post. Getting the post plumb at the outset also keeps the whole corner looking straight.
  66. The correct answer is D (A story pole, used to keep every course's reveal consistent): a story pole is a length of scrap material marked out with the reveal spacing for the full wall height, and it keeps every course consistent while making sure courses land correctly at window sills and door heads without an odd sliver course at a critical location. A plumb bob and chalk line serve different layout purposes and do not track reveal spacing over an entire wall. A speed square checks angles, not reveal consistency.
  67. The correct answer is C (The shingle is under-secured because the nail misses the intended holding zone): placing nails above the marked nailing zone under-secures the shingle since the fastener is no longer catching the intended holding area, weakening wind resistance. Nailing below the zone is the error that can show through on the exposed face and fail to properly catch the course above, which is a different problem than nailing too high. Neither error directly affects the adhesive strip's ability to seal.
  68. The correct answer is D (It prevents a straight-line water path, with a common offset of about 6 inches): offsetting the cutouts between successive courses prevents water from finding a straight-line path down through the layers, and manufacturer instructions commonly specify roughly a 6 inch offset from one course to the next. This is a functional water-management detail, not simply a cosmetic choice, so it is not left to a random amount. The exact offset pattern always follows the shingle manufacturer's instructions for that product.
  69. The correct answer is A (They keep water on the sill draining out the front instead of backward or sideways): a proper sill pan has back and end dams so that any water sitting on the sill drains out the front over the exterior wall surface, rather than leaking backward into the wall cavity or sideways past the jambs. The sill pan is a water-management detail, not a structural support element or an air-sealing gasket. It also has no role in setting the reveal spacing of siding below the window.
  70. The correct answer is B (It gives water a path to drain down and out, and lets the wall dry by ventilation): the rain screen gap gives any water that gets past the cladding a clear path to drain down and out at the bottom, rather than sitting trapped against the sheathing, and it also allows the wall to dry by ventilation. It does not stop all bulk water from reaching the barrier, since some water does get past the cladding by design assumption. It is not a substitute for the water-resistive barrier and has no role in vapour diffusion control.
  71. The correct answer is B (By explaining that a wall can leak air through unsealed penetrations even with an intact vapour barrier): air sealing is a related but genuinely separate concern from vapour control, and a wall can have an intact vapour barrier yet still leak enormous amounts of air, and the moisture riding on that air, through unsealed penetrations, sill plates, and top plates. The same polyethylene sheet with taped seams often does double duty as both an air barrier and a vapour barrier, so the materials are not always different. Vapour barriers remain a standard requirement in most of Canada.
  72. The correct answer is A (At least 4 to 6 feet away from the foundation): downspouts need to discharge well clear of the foundation, typically with an extension or splash block directing water at least 4 to 6 feet away, because water dumped right at the foundation is one of the most common causes of basement moisture problems and foundation heaving in a freeze-thaw climate. A distance of only 1 to 2 feet is not far enough to reliably prevent that problem. Discharging directly at the foundation wall is the exact error this detail is meant to avoid.
  73. The correct answer is D (It gives the eavestrough a solid mounting surface along the eave): fascia boards cap the ends of the rafters or trusses at the eave and give the eavestrough a solid surface to be mounted onto, in addition to closing off the roof edge. Vapour barrier and attic intake ventilation are separate systems handled by the polyethylene sheet and the soffit, not the fascia. Fascia also does not eliminate the need for drip edge flashing, which still directs water off the roof edge.
  74. The correct answer is C (Fold them into the rough opening so sill, jambs, and head are covered): the flaps created by the modified I-shaped cut are folded into the rough opening so that the sill, jambs, and head are all covered by house wrap before flashing tape goes on around the opening. Cutting the flaps away entirely or leaving them loose would leave the rough framing unprotected right where flashing needs continuous coverage. Stapling them flat against the exterior face, rather than folding them in, would not cover the inside of the opening at all.
  75. The correct answer is D (16 inches on centre for typical residential decking, per span tables): deck joist spacing commonly runs 16 inches on centre for typical residential decking, though wider spacing is possible with larger joists according to the applicable span tables for that joist size and species. The spacing is not a single fixed number independent of joist size, which is why the other options are described as regardless of size. Span tables, not a rule of thumb alone, ultimately govern the final spacing on a given job.
  76. The correct answer is C (It provides a secondary water barrier under the shingles): underlayment goes down over the roof sheathing before any shingles are installed, providing a secondary water barrier for the roof assembly. It is not a structural layer tying trusses together, and it is not the finished visible surface, since shingles are installed over it. Underlayment does not replace ice and water shield, which is still required as extra protection at eaves and valleys.
  77. The correct answer is B (Corner boards that the siding courses butt against): wood and fibre cement siding are more commonly mitred at outside corners for a clean finished look, or run into corner boards that the siding butts against, which is faster to install and more forgiving of minor irregularities in the framing. Pre-formed vinyl corner posts are specific to vinyl and many engineered products, not typically used with wood or fibre cement. J-channel trims siding around openings and terminations, not outside corners, and an unfinished caulk-only corner would not be a professional detail.
  78. The correct answer is A (At the corners and at mid-span points along the frame): shims go at the corners and mid-span points on a wider window, and fasteners go through those shim locations so the frame is fully supported rather than fastened at random points along the jamb. A door instead gets shims at its hinge and lock points, which is a different fastening pattern for a different unit. A single centre-top shim or an interior drywall reference point would leave much of a wide frame unsupported.
  79. The correct answer is A (Standard foam can generate enough curing pressure to bow the frame inward): standard expanding foam can generate enough pressure as it cures to bow the window frame inward, causing binding, which is why low-expansion window and door foam is what a professional installer uses, applied in a thin bead rather than filling the whole cavity at once. Paintability and cost are not the reasons for the substitution, and both foam types do provide insulation value at the perimeter gap.
  80. The correct answer is D (Head flashing is installed last, overlapping the top of the side flashing): head flashing goes on last, overlapping the top of the side flashing on both jambs, and the house wrap at the top of the opening is then folded down over the head flashing last of all. Sill pan flashing is never installed a second time later in the sequence, and side flashing is not doubled up. Leaving the opening with no head flashing at all would break the shingle-lap order at the top of the window.
  81. The correct answer is C (Heat escaping through the roof melts snow that refreezes at the cold eave): ice damming happens when heat escaping through the roof melts snow on the upper, warmer part of the roof while the eave stays cold, and the meltwater then refreezes at the eave, building a dam that backs water up under the shingles above it. It is not caused by wind-driven rain freezing on contact or by the eavestrough overflowing. The long-term fix is a cold, well-ventilated attic that keeps the whole roof deck at a consistent temperature.
  82. The correct answer is B (Drip edge goes under the underlayment at the eave, and over it at the rake): drip edge typically installs under the underlayment at the eave and over the underlayment at the rakes, per manufacturer instructions, directing water off the roof edge and away from the fascia at each location. It is not the same sequence at both edges, and it is not omitted at the rake, since both edges need this metal flashing. The exact sequence should always be confirmed against the specific manufacturer's instructions for that product.
  83. The correct answer is B (About half an inch larger than the unit in width and height): rough openings for windows and doors are typically framed about half an inch larger than the actual unit in width and height, to allow room for shimming and adjustment. Framing the opening exactly the same size as the unit would leave no room to plumb, level, and square the frame during installation. Oversizing by 2 or 4 inches goes well beyond what is needed and complicates flashing and shimming.
  84. The correct answer is C (So the adhesive strip sits at the eave edge under the first visible course): the starter course, whether a manufactured starter strip or full shingles with the tabs cut off installed upside down, is arranged so the adhesive strip sits at the eave edge, giving the first visible course a solid, sealed base to butt against. It is not about protecting granules from sunlight or aligning with the fascia, and a starter course is a permanent part of the roof assembly, not a reusable piece.
  85. The correct answer is A (It cannot accommodate the differential movement between the roof and the wall): a continuous piece of flashing without step flashing looks fine when new but will eventually leak because it cannot accommodate the differential movement between the roof and the wall over time. It is compatible with asphalt shingles in the sense that it is still a roofing flashing detail, just the wrong one at this location. It has no direct effect on soffit ventilation intake or on ice and water shield adhesion elsewhere on the roof.
  86. The correct answer is D (To move water that gets past the door sweep back outside instead of ponding): a sloped sill pan, sometimes combined with a drainage gap or weep holes at the threshold, moves any water that gets past the door sweep back outside instead of letting it pond against the framing. This detail is about water management, not structural load capacity or fastener count. It works together with the door sweep rather than replacing the need for one.
  87. The correct answer is D (The nails were over-driven, pinning the panel tightly against the sheathing): over-driving the nails pins the vinyl panel in place instead of letting it hang loosely on the nail, and when temperature swings then cause the vinyl to expand, it has nowhere to go and buckles or warps visibly. An out-of-level starter strip would show up as a wandering reveal line up the wall, not summer buckling. J-channel choice and reveal inconsistency are separate installation issues that do not cause this specific heat-related symptom.
  88. The correct answer is A (A minimum 6 inch overlap, with the seam taped using compatible sheathing tape): vertical seams in house wrap get a minimum 6 inch overlap, the same as horizontal seams, and every seam, horizontal and vertical, gets taped with a compatible sheathing tape once the wrap is fastened. Staples or cap nails hold the wrap in place, but tape is what actually seals the seams against wind-driven rain, so staples alone are not sufficient. Construction adhesive in place of proper sheathing tape is not the specified detail for this seam.
  89. The correct answer is B (The two diagonal measurements from opposite corners of the opening): checking that the opening is square is done by measuring both diagonals; if they match, the opening is square. Comparing top and bottom widths, or the two jamb heights, can miss a racked opening that still measures equal on those particular pairs of dimensions. If the diagonals do not match, the window will rack when fastened and will not operate properly even if it looks fine sitting in the hole.
  90. The correct answer is C (A mitred joint at the top of a window head can trap water in the joint): joints around windows and doors get installed with joints designed to shed water rather than trap it, and a mitred joint at the top of a window trim head can trap water in the joint, which is why many installers prefer a sloped drip cap over the window head trim instead. This is a water-shedding preference rather than a strict universal code requirement or a material-savings decision. A drip cap does not remove the need for quality caulking at trim joints elsewhere.
  91. The correct answer is C (About 3/4 inch of total drop over the run): at roughly 1/4 inch of drop per 10 feet of run, a 30 foot eavestrough run needs about three 10-foot increments worth of slope, which works out to 3 times 1/4 inch, or about 3/4 inch of total drop from the high end to the downspout outlet. Using only 1/4 or 1/2 inch total would undershoot the recommended slope for a run this length. Doubling the correct figure to 1 and 1/2 inches would create noticeably more slope than the standard guideline calls for.
  92. The correct answer is B (The fascia is the solid surface the eavestrough hangers are fastened into): fascia boards cap the ends of the rafters or trusses at the eave and give the eavestrough a solid mounting surface, which is why sound, properly capped fascia matters before any hangers are fastened into it. Eavestrough slope is set by the carpenter positioning the hangers correctly, not determined by the fascia itself. Soffit ventilation intake and shingle exposure spacing are separate systems that fascia condition does not control.
  93. The correct answer is D (Flexible flashing membrane, rigid pan flashing, or built-up compatible flashing tape): sill pans can be formed from flexible flashing membrane, rigid pan flashing, or built up from compatible flashing tape, and the back and end dam function has to be there regardless of which material is chosen. Limiting the answer to only one of these three material options excludes acceptable alternatives that achieve the same water-management result. No single one of these three options is discontinued or unusable for forming a proper sill pan.
  94. The correct answer is A (Hidden structural rot developing in the ledger and rim joist area over time): a ledger board attached without proper flashing is one of the most common sources of hidden structural rot in residential decks, since water running down behind the ledger reaches the rim joist and framing where nobody sees it until the damage is expensive. Missing ledger flashing has no direct connection to guard height, baluster spacing, or joist spacing, which are separate code requirements governed by different details of the deck design.
  95. The correct answer is A (As a maximum sphere diameter that must not be able to pass through): baluster spacing is restricted so a sphere of a specified diameter cannot pass through the gap between adjacent balusters, which is how the code expresses the maximum allowable opening. It is not expressed as a fixed baluster count per linear foot, a percentage of open guard area, or a distance tied to the guard height itself. This sphere rule works alongside, and separately from, the required guard height range.
  96. The correct answer is B (It is the final line of defence against wind-driven rain behind the finish): caulking at trim joints, siding terminations, and any point where dissimilar materials meet is the final line of defence against wind-driven rain finding its way behind the finish materials. The water-resistive barrier, not caulking, is the primary drainage plane, and the polyethylene sheet handles vapour diffusion control. A good quality sealant supplements the house wrap at terminations, it does not replace the need for it.
  97. The correct answer is D (J-channel trims cut ends at openings and terminations, while posts receive corner ends): J-channel trims out the siding around window and door openings, around soffits, and at any point where the siding terminates against another material, while corner posts are the pre-formed pieces that inside and outside corner siding courses tuck into. J-channel is not restricted to inside corners only, since it is used broadly at openings and terminations. Neither product replaces the starter strip or the story pole, which serve entirely different functions in the job.
  98. The correct answer is C (Four to six nails driven into each individual shingle): nailing pattern matters for wind resistance, and four to six nails per shingle, placed within the nailing zone marked on the shingle and driven flush without cutting into the shingle surface, is the commonly specified count. Two to three nails, or a single centred cluster of three, would under-secure the shingle against wind uplift. Eight to ten nails goes well beyond the typical specification and is not the standard pattern used.
  99.
