The correct answer is B (The permanent beam and posts must be confirmed to carry the load correctly.): Temporary shoring stays in place for the entire time the permanent structure is open and only comes out once the new permanent beam and its posts are installed, fastened, and confirmed to be picking up the load correctly. Simply having the beam physically in position is not enough on its own. Removing shoring before this confirmation risks the load transferring to nothing.
  64. The correct answer is C (Notches near mid-span or the top and bottom edges can seriously weaken the joist.): A joist's strength depends heavily on its full cross-section, particularly along the top and bottom edges where bending stresses are highest. A notch or hole cut near mid-span or in the outer thirds of the top or bottom edge can seriously weaken the member even though it looks like a small cut. Building codes and span tables restrict where and how large a notch or bored hole is permitted for exactly this reason.
  65. The correct answer is A (Install a properly fastened ledger, new beam, or blocking sized for the load.): New floor or roof framing tying into an existing structure requires the new members to bear on something adequate, such as a properly fastened ledger, a new beam, or blocking added to receive the new joists. The connection must transfer load the way a continuous framing member would, using proper fasteners or hardware sized for the load. Simple toe-nailing into whatever is convenient does not meet this standard.
  66. The correct answer is D (Alarm requirements typically apply to the renovated area and can trigger alarms elsewhere too.): Smoke alarm and carbon monoxide alarm requirements typically apply to the specific area being renovated and often trigger additional alarm locations elsewhere in the home depending on the scope of work. This is part of the general principle that the renovated area needs to meet current code even if the rest of the home is grandfathered. The carpenter should expect this to be part of a permitted renovation's requirements.
  67. The correct answer is B (Establish a new level reference line, then shim or scribe new work to it.): Practical approaches to out-of-level or out-of-plumb existing structure include establishing an independent new reference line and shimming or scribing new work to meet the old surface. A gradual condition can also be split across a run of new material rather than concentrated in one visible spot. Where the discrepancy is severe, the underlying cause, such as a settled footing, should be addressed rather than just building crooked framing to match crooked framing.
  68. The correct answer is A (Work affecting structure or electrical systems generally requires a permit and inspection.): Renovation work that affects structure, electrical systems, or plumbing systems generally requires a permit, and permitted work is inspected against the current provincial building code and applicable electrical and plumbing codes. Wall removal and new electrical circuits both fall squarely into this category. A carpenter should never treat prior original construction as justification for skipping current code in a permitted scope.
  69. The correct answer is C (Moisture can become trapped inside the assembly with no way to dry out.): Adding a vapour-impermeable material on the exterior side, or doubling up vapour barriers on both sides of an assembly, can trap moisture inside the wall with no way to dry out. This leads to rot and mould over time even though the work may have looked like a straightforward efficiency upgrade. In most of Canada's climate, the vapour barrier belongs on the warm interior side only.
  70. The correct answer is D (Air sealing interacts with moisture management, since airflow paths also carry moisture.): Air sealing gaps around penetrations, sill plates, and window and door openings is a routine part of energy retrofit work, and it interacts directly with how the wall assembly manages moisture, since air movement also carries moisture vapour. The carpenter needs to think through where moisture can get in and where it needs to be able to get out. Sealing gaps without considering this can change how an assembly dries.
  71. The correct answer is A (The rebuilt guard needs to meet current code guard height requirements.): A stair or deck guard being rebuilt or significantly altered needs to meet current guard height and baluster spacing requirements, not the shorter or more widely spaced original guard. This follows the general principle that the specific area being renovated is brought up to current code. Reproducing the original shorter height would not satisfy that requirement.
  72. The correct answer is C (A doubled joist directly above a wall strongly suggests that wall is its bearing point.): Looking for a beam, a doubled or built-up joist, or a girder truss sitting directly above a wall is the second of several checks used to identify a load-bearing wall. Finding one directly above the wall in question is a strong sign the wall below is its bearing point. This check is used together with framing direction and tracing the load path down through the structure.
  73. The correct answer is B (Shoring on an unsupported subfloor passes the load to something that may not hold it.): Shoring needs to bear down onto something solid, such as a beam, a foundation wall, or ground-level bearing with proper footing plates, rather than resting on a subfloor that is itself only supported by the joists below. If the shoring does not carry down to adequate support, the load simply transfers to whatever the shoring is sitting on. That underlying surface may not be able to carry it either.
  74. The correct answer is D (Custom-mill or match the profile from salvage so it reads as part of the house.): Older trim profiles are sometimes no longer stocked and need to be custom-milled or matched from salvage so the addition reads as part of the house rather than an obvious patch. Careful measurement and sample matching before ordering material pays off, since discovering a mismatch after installation means redoing finished work. Using an unrelated modern profile would create a visible mismatch.
  75. The correct answer is C (Stop work, flag the concern, and arrange testing and qualified abatement if confirmed.): When there is reason to suspect asbestos or lead is present and work will disturb it, the correct step is to stop, flag it to the contractor or client, and arrange for testing and, if needed, abatement by a qualified professional. A carpenter should never sand, cut, or otherwise disturb suspected asbestos-containing material as routine practice. That work belongs to people trained and certified for it.
  76. The correct answer is A (Home age flags likely hazardous materials, outdated wiring, and older plumbing types.): Determining the approximate age of a home is part of assessing existing conditions because it flags the likelihood of hazardous materials, outdated wiring methods, and older plumbing types the renovation may encounter. This informs how the project is planned, priced, and sequenced. Ignoring the home's age can leave a carpenter unprepared for conditions that are common for that construction era.
  77. The correct answer is D (Cover flooring with rosin paper or ram board and protect cabinetry before starting.): Protecting existing finishes that are staying in place is part of the scope even when it is not written on the drawings, using tools like rosin paper or ram board on finished floors and covering cabinetry before demolition starts. This prevents avoidable damage to work that has nothing to do with the actual demolition. Skipping this step risks damaging finishes that were never meant to be part of the work.
  78. The correct answer is B (Never remove a structural member until its load is confirmed or supported.): This is a core rule of selective demolition sequencing: a structural member is never removed until the load it carries has been accounted for, either by confirming it carries no load or by installing temporary support first. Removing it first and assessing afterward risks a real structural failure. This applies to interior and exterior structural members alike.
  79. The correct answer is A (Whether the wall lines up with a wall, post, or footing below it.): Tracing a wall down through the structure means checking whether it lines up with a wall, post, or footing in the storey below, or with a foundation wall. A load path that runs continuously from roof to footing is a load-bearing path, and a wall anywhere along that path is carrying load. This is the third check used together with framing direction and beam location to identify bearing walls.
  80. The correct answer is D (Involve an engineer or qualified person to confirm the load path and beam size.): When the load-bearing status of a wall is still unclear after the standard checks, the correct move is to involve an engineer or other qualified person to size the replacement beam and confirm the new load path, rather than estimating it on site. Guessing at a bearing wall removal is one of the few mistakes in carpentry that can genuinely put people at risk. Knowing when to call for engineering input is part of a journeyperson's judgment.
  81. The correct answer is C (Some older plaster, joint compound, pipe wrap, and older ceiling tile can also be sources.): Asbestos-containing materials showed up in a range of older building products beyond attic insulation, including some plaster and joint compound, vinyl floor tile and its adhesive, pipe wrap, and some older ceiling tile. Any of these materials should be treated with the same awareness before being disturbed in an older home. Recognizing the full range of potential sources, not just attic insulation, is part of a proper pre-demolition assessment.
  82. The correct answer is B (A negative air machine exhausted outdoors should control the volume of dust.): Where demolition work is heavy, such as sanding, drywall demolition, or concrete cutting, negative air machines exhausted outdoors are used along with poly sheeting and zipper doors to keep the rest of the house liveable. This also keeps dust out of the home's HVAC returns. Poly sheeting and zipper doors alone are not always sufficient for this volume of dust.
  83. The correct answer is D (Split the gradual out-of-level condition evenly across the run of new material.): Splitting a gradual out-of-level condition across a run of new material, rather than concentrating it in one visible spot, is one of the practical approaches for tying new work into an existing structure that is not true. This keeps the discrepancy from being obviously visible at any single point. It is used alongside establishing an independent reference line and shimming or scribing new work to the old surface.
  84. The correct answer is A (It should be disposed of through the proper channel, never mixed with general waste.): Any material identified or suspected as hazardous needs to be handled and disposed of through the proper channel, never mixed into general construction waste. This is distinct from ordinary debris, which can be bagged or bin-loaded as it is generated. Disposal overall needs to follow local requirements for both general and hazardous material streams.
  85. The correct answer is B (The shoring must stay until the beam and posts are fastened and confirmed to carry load.): Shoring stays in place for the entire time the permanent structure is open and only comes out once the new permanent beam and its posts are installed, fastened, and confirmed to be picking up the load correctly. Simply setting the beam into position, without confirming the posts are fastened and functioning, is not enough. Removing shoring too early risks the load having nowhere reliable to go.
  86. The correct answer is C (Sister an additional joist alongside it or install headers and trimmers around it.): When an existing joist must be cut beyond what permitted notch and hole limits allow, the correct approach is to sister an additional joist alongside the cut one, or install proper framing such as headers and trimmers to carry the load around the interruption. This restores the load-carrying capacity that the oversized cut would otherwise remove. Making the cut without this compensation risks a weakened joist.
  87. The correct answer is D (Flag the extent of contamination and call for a qualified remediation professional.): Depending on the extent of contamination, discovered mould may call for a qualified remediation professional, following the same awareness-and-refer principle used for asbestos and lead. Simple surface cleaning or painting over the area does not address contamination that has spread through the framing. The underlying cause and the affected material both need to be properly dealt with before the wall is closed up.
  88. The correct answer is B (Adding the new electrical circuits affects electrical systems, requiring a permit.): Renovation work that affects electrical systems generally requires a permit, while repainting and moving a confirmed non-structural partition typically do not carry the same trigger. The new circuits for the kitchen island are the clearest reason this scope needs a permit. A carpenter should recognize which categories of work, structural, electrical, or plumbing, are the ones that trigger this requirement.
  89. The correct answer is A (Exterior walls are almost always load-bearing, unlike many interior partitions.): Exterior walls are almost always load-bearing, while interior walls need the full set of checks described in this chapter because plenty of them are non-structural partitions that just happen to look identical to a bearing wall from the room side. This general rule helps focus the more detailed tracing work on the interior walls where the answer is genuinely uncertain. Exterior walls rarely require the same level of investigation.
  90. The correct answer is C (Communicate the discovered condition to the client and contractor promptly.): Plumbing with no shutoff, discovered mid-renovation, is exactly the kind of scope creep condition that needs to be communicated to the client and contractor promptly rather than covered up or ignored. Fixing it silently without informing anyone bypasses the proper process for pricing and scheduling discovered work. Leaving it in place and framing over it would repeat the mistake the chapter warns against.
  91. The correct answer is B (Debris should be bagged or bin-loaded as generated, for housekeeping and safety.): Debris should be bagged or bin-loaded as it is generated rather than left to accumulate, both for general housekeeping and because a cluttered demolition area is a trip and fall hazard. Waiting until the end of a phase or a week allows debris to build up unnecessarily. Managing debris continuously keeps the work area safer throughout the demolition.
  92. The correct answer is D (On the warm, interior side, stopping moisture before it reaches a cold surface.): In most of Canada's climate, a vapour barrier belongs on the warm-in-winter, interior side of the insulation, so that moisture-laden interior air is stopped before it reaches a cold surface where it would condense. Placing it on the exterior side instead, or doubling up barriers on both sides, can trap moisture inside the wall with no way to dry out. This placement is a key building science principle for retrofit work.
  93. The correct answer is C (Address the discovered rot rather than covering it, since that returns on the carpenter.): Covering a known defect back up because it is not technically part of the original scope, or to stay on schedule, is a decision that comes back on the carpenter later. The correct response to discovered rot at a window sill is to address it, communicating the added scope and schedule impact to the client. Delaying the fix to some future unrelated project leaves the underlying problem unresolved.
  94. The correct answer is A (Swinging a bar without confirming what is inside the wall risks hitting live wiring.): Older homes can have wiring routed in unexpected places, run along a wall that looks purely decorative, or dropped through a chase that is not obvious from the plan. A carpenter swinging a demolition bar into such a wall without confirming what is inside it is taking an unnecessary risk with personal safety and the systems that keep the house running. Locating and confirming circuits before demolition avoids this risk.
  95. The correct answer is D (Where moisture can get in and out around the opening, sealed and flashed properly.): Window replacement is a common energy retrofit item that interacts directly with the building's moisture management, since a poorly sealed or flashed opening can let water in or trap moisture that cannot escape. The carpenter needs to think through where moisture can enter and where it needs to be able to leave around the new opening. This is the same building science thinking applied to insulation and air sealing work.
  96. The correct answer is C (The connection needs shimming or packing out so surfaces end up flat and in plane.): Because original true-dimension lumber measures larger than modern dressed lumber of the same nominal size, a new wall framed at modern dimensions will not line up flush against an original wall without adjustment. The connection needs to be shimmed, packed out, or otherwise adjusted so the finished surfaces end up flat and in plane. Skipping this step leaves a step in the drywall that no amount of mudding fixes cleanly.
  97. The correct answer is B (Recognize the hazard and arrange testing and qualified abatement rather than disturbing it.): The carpenter's responsibility with suspected lead-painted material, like suspected asbestos, is awareness and recognition rather than remediation. The correct step is to arrange for testing and, if needed, abatement by a qualified professional before the material is disturbed. Sanding, painting over without testing, or removing it with standard tools all bypass this proper process.
  98. The correct answer is A (It is the fastest way to spot load-bearing walls against visible framing conditions.): Reading a floor plan against the framing above and below it is described as the fastest way to spot load-bearing walls, since it connects the layout on paper to what is actually visible in an attic or basement. This comparison helps a carpenter narrow down which walls need the more detailed tracing checks. It applies to multi-storey homes just as much as single-storey ones.
  99. The correct answer is D (The rebuilt guard needs to meet current guard height and baluster spacing requirements.): A stair guard being rebuilt or significantly altered needs to meet current guard height and baluster spacing requirements, not the shorter or more widely spaced original guard. This follows the general renovation principle that the specific area being altered is brought up to current code. Reproducing the original wider spacing would not satisfy this requirement.
  100. The correct answer is C (It sets up the same failure again and can seal a hazard inside the wall.): Covering damaged or mould-affected material without fixing what caused it, and without remediating material that needs to be removed and replaced, only sets up the same failure to happen again behind new finishes. It can also leave a health hazard sealed inside the wall. Installing new drywall over the problem does nothing to address the underlying moisture source or the contaminated material itself.

Practice Test 10 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Joists ending and splicing directly over the wall shows it carries their load.): when floor or roof framing runs perpendicular to a wall and terminates or splices directly above it, that framing is transferring its load down into the wall below. This is one of the strongest visual clues for identifying a bearing wall, alongside tracing the wall down to a continuous load path. A wall's parallel relationship to the foundation, its ceiling height, or the presence of outlets says nothing about whether it is carrying structural load.
  2. The correct answer is A (Temporary shoring bearing down onto solid support goes in on both sides of the wall.): shoring must be set up before any permanent structural member is removed so the existing load has somewhere to go while the wall is open. The shoring has to bear down onto solid support, such as a beam or foundation, not just rest on a subfloor. Stripping drywall, posting a drawing, or relocating the homeowner do nothing to actually carry the structural load during the work.
  3. The correct answer is D (Bore a hole centred in the joist's depth, kept clear of the top and bottom edges.): bending stresses in a joist are highest along its top and bottom edges, so bored holes are restricted to the middle of the joist's depth where those stresses are lowest. Notching, especially at mid-span or through the full depth, removes material from the high-stress zone and seriously weakens the member. Keeping the hole centred and away from the edges is what protects the joist's remaining strength.
  4. The correct answer is B (Stop work in the area, flag the material, and arrange testing before it is disturbed further.): a carpenter's responsibility around suspected asbestos-containing material is awareness and recognition, not remediation. Loose, pebble-like grey attic insulation in a home from this era is a recognized possible source and should be treated as suspect until tested. Continuing to work in the space, disturbing the material with water, or assuming it is not a concern all risk exposure before the material has even been assessed.
  5. The correct answer is A (Treat the surface as a possible lead paint source until it is proven otherwise.): lead paint was commonly used until it was phased out around 1980, so any pre-1980s painted surface should be assumed to be a possible lead source. Sanding disturbs the coating and can generate hazardous dust, so this needs to be addressed before work begins rather than assumed away. Heritage status and the surface being interior trim rather than siding do not change the age-based risk.
  6. The correct answer is C (The narrower modern dimension will create a visible step in the finished drywall surface.): lumber sold before roughly the 1960s was commonly true-sawn, so an original 2 by 4 measures a full 2 inches by 4 inches, while modern dressed lumber of the same nominal size measures 1-1/2 by 3-1/2 inches. Framing a new wall at modern dimensions directly against an original true-dimension wall without shimming or packing out creates a mismatch that shows up as a step in the finished surface. It is not a strength, fastener spacing, or code-compliance issue on its own.
  7. The correct answer is B (The renovated bedroom's window generally must meet current egress size and sill requirements.): the area actually being renovated is expected to be brought up to current code even though the rest of the house may remain legally grandfathered. Since the bedroom itself is the scope of this renovation, its window needs to meet current egress size and sill height requirements rather than reproducing the original undersized opening. This requirement is not limited to basements and applies regardless of whether structural walls are also being changed.
  8. The correct answer is D (It can trap moisture inside the wall assembly with no path left to dry out.): in most of Canada's climate, the vapour barrier belongs on the warm-in-winter interior side of the insulation. Adding a second vapour-impermeable layer on the exterior side traps interior moisture that reaches the assembly with no way to escape, which leads to rot and mould over time. This is a moisture management failure, not primarily an R-value loss or a warranty issue on the sheathing product.
  9. The correct answer is A (Identify and correct the ventilation problem, then remediate the affected material properly.): covering damaged or mould-affected material without fixing what caused it only sets up the same failure to happen again behind new finishes, and can seal a health hazard inside the wall. Because the contamination here is confined to a single stud bay with no visible spread, this stays within the scope of the carpenter's own work: correct the underlying ventilation problem and remediate the affected material before the assembly is closed up again. More extensive contamination spreading through the framing beyond a small, contained area would instead call for a qualified remediation professional, the same threshold used elsewhere for widespread mould.
  10. The correct answer is D (This scope, affecting structure, electrical, and plumbing, generally requires a permit.): renovation work that affects structure, electrical systems, or plumbing systems generally requires a permit, and this scope touches all three categories. Permitted work then gets inspected against the current provincial building code and applicable electrical and plumbing codes. Homeowner presence and whether square footage is added do not change whether this combined scope triggers a permit requirement.
  11. The correct answer is C (Communicate the discovery to the client and contractor promptly before proceeding further.): scope creep in the form of unexpected discovered conditions, like live knob-and-tube wiring, needs to be communicated to the client and contractor promptly rather than handled unilaterally. A journeyperson should never simply cover a known defect back up to stay on schedule. Leaving it undisturbed and closing the wall, or quietly rewiring it without authorization, both bypass the communication step that discovered conditions require.
  12. The correct answer is B (Ceiling finishes first, then wall finishes, then framing only once loads are addressed.): selective demolition generally proceeds from the top down and from finishes toward structure, removing ceiling finishes before wall finishes and never removing structural framing until any load it carries has been accounted for. This sequence keeps falling debris away from cleared areas and lets the carpenter see conditions before working under them. Removing framing early or mixing structure with finish demolition increases risk unnecessarily.
  13. The correct answer is D (Installing a negative air machine exhausted outdoors to control the volume of dust.): where the volume of dust is significant, such as with heavy drywall demolition, a negative air machine exhausted outdoors is used alongside poly sheeting and zipper doors to keep the rest of an occupied home liveable. Running the central HVAC fan would actually spread dust through the ductwork, and simply opening windows or shifting the schedule does not provide the same level of containment.
  14. The correct answer is A (Involve an engineer or other qualified person to confirm the load path and size the beam.): when the load path through a suspected bearing wall cannot be clearly confirmed, the correct move is to bring in an engineer or other qualified person to confirm it and size the replacement beam, rather than estimating on site. Removing the wall and watching for movement afterward, or oversizing a beam by guesswork, both risk a slow structural failure that may not show up right away.
  15. The correct answer is B (The rebuilt guard generally must meet current guard height and baluster spacing requirements.): a stair or deck guard being rebuilt or significantly altered needs to meet current guard height and baluster spacing requirements, not the shorter or more widely spaced original design. The fact that the deck structure itself is not new does not exempt a rebuilt guard from current requirements, and increasing only height or only spacing individually would still leave the guard non-compliant.
  16. The correct answer is C (Whether the ledger uses fasteners or hardware sized for the actual load being transferred.): a ledger connection needs to transfer load the same way a continuous framing member would, which depends on proper fasteners or hardware, such as a correct ledger fastening schedule, sized for the actual load. Matching paint colour or lumber species and the installation sequence relative to the finished flooring do not affect the structural adequacy of the connection itself.
  17. The correct answer is A (Establish a new level reference line independent of the old surface and shim to meet it.): a practical approach to tying into out-of-level existing structure is to establish a new, independent level or plumb reference and shim or scribe the new work to meet the old surface. Matching the new framing exactly to the existing slope would carry the error forward, and concentrating the correction in one visible spot rather than spreading it across the run makes the discrepancy more noticeable, not less.
  18. The correct answer is D (Smoke and carbon monoxide alarm requirements typically apply and may add locations elsewhere.): smoke alarm and carbon monoxide alarm requirements typically apply to the renovated area and often trigger additional alarm locations elsewhere in the home depending on the scope of work. Relocating the furnace, installing a fire-rated separation door, or mandating a sump pump are not general requirements that follow automatically from finishing a basement recreation area and bedroom.
  19. The correct answer is C (A centred bored hole avoids the high-stress top and bottom edges that a notch removes.): a joist's strength depends heavily on its top and bottom edges where bending stresses are highest, and a centred bored hole stays away from those edges, unlike a notch cut into the top or bottom of the member. Boring is not necessarily faster, and both notches and bored holes remain subject to code-permitted size and location limits regardless of which method is chosen.
  20. The correct answer is B (Sequence the work, protect pathways, and communicate daily plans so key rooms stay usable.): working in an occupied home means scheduling around the household, protecting pathways with floor coverings, and communicating clearly about what each day's work involves so that essential spaces like a kitchen and bathroom remain usable. Asking the client to leave, or only protecting the immediately adjacent rooms, does not reflect the coordination that occupied-home renovation work actually requires.
  21. The correct answer is A (Remove the cracked plank and replace it with a sound one before work begins.): A cracked plank can fail without warning under a worker's weight, and schedule pressure never justifies accepting that risk on a scaffold. Full planking free of defects is a basic requirement, not a preference to weigh against the calendar. Turning the crack face down or lightening the load does not restore the plank's actual strength.
  22. The correct answer is A (Use a personal fall arrest system regardless of the exact trigger height for this eave.): When the exact trigger height is uncertain, a carpenter should choose the safer option rather than argue the measurement is just below the line. A harness and lanyard anchored to a rated point actually arrests a fall, while a ground spotter or a hard hat does nothing to stop one. Setting up fall arrest before truss work begins protects the worker without depending on a borderline number.
  23. The correct answer is A (Stop using it and locate the safety data sheet, or ask the supervisor, first.): An unlabelled container with no accompanying safety data sheet is exactly the situation WHMIS training exists for, since the hazard stays unknown until the product is identified. Guessing at the contents, smelling it, or discarding it into general waste all risk exposure or an improper disposal. Stopping work and confirming what the product actually is protects both the carpenter and anyone else working nearby.
  24. The correct answer is C (Reset the ladder to a 4:1 angle on stable footing, then haul shingles up by rope.): A steep, unstable ladder setup is a setup problem that a helper holding the base cannot fully fix, and carrying a bundle up by hand breaks the three-points-of-contact rule regardless of who is steadying below. Correcting the angle and footing addresses the actual hazard, and hauling material up separately keeps both hands free for climbing safely. A stepladder is not a practical substitute for reaching a second-storey roof edge on this job.
  25. The correct answer is A (The saw kerf is not being accounted for consistently when the cut line is marked.): A consistent, repeatable error across many identical cuts points to a habit in the marking or cutting method rather than a random cause, and forgetting to allow for the blade kerf on the waste side of the line produces exactly this kind of steady offset. A defective tape would usually show up on other measurements too, and humidity swings or a loose fence would produce inconsistent results rather than the same 1/16 inch every single time. Marking consistently on the same side of the kerf line resolves the pattern.
  26. The correct answer is C (Release the trigger, let the blade stop, and add support under the board before continuing.): A board supported only at the ends sags in the middle as it is cut, closing the kerf onto the blade and causing exactly this binding, which is a classic kickback setup. Forcing the cut through, pulling the spinning blade backward, or cutting deeper all increase the chance of a violent kickback toward the operator's body. Stopping, letting the blade come to rest, and re-supporting the material near the cut removes the cause before the cut resumes.
  27. The correct answer is B (Sequential trip mode, since bump mode raises the risk of an accidental double-fire nearby.): Bump mode fires a nail every time the nose contacts the work while the trigger stays depressed, which increases the chance of an unintended discharge in a crowded work area with other trades close by. Sequential trip mode requires a fresh trigger pull for every single nail, giving the operator deliberate control over each individual shot fired. Disconnecting the air hose between nails is impractical on a real job and does not answer which trigger mode is actually safer here.
  28. The correct answer is C (Wait for a second worker, or use a hand truck, rather than carrying it alone.): A full sheet of 3/4 inch plywood is awkward and heavy enough that good lifting form alone does not make a solo carry safe, since the risk comes from the size and weight of the load itself, not just technique. Dragging the sheet risks damaging it and still strains the back over that distance, and twisting while lifting is one of the specific movements that causes back injuries on site. Getting a second person or a hand truck removes the hazard rather than trying to manage it with technique alone.
  29. The correct answer is B (Treat it as a potential confined space, and arrange air testing and a standby attendant.): A tight crawl space with restricted entry and a musty smell suggesting poor air movement matches the description of a potential confined space, which requires air testing and a standby attendant before anyone actually enters. A short task duration does not remove the hazard of a bad atmosphere, and propping the hatch open or wearing a dust mask does not substitute for confirming the air is safe to breathe first. Following confined space procedure here protects the carpenter from a hazard invisible from outside.
  30. The correct answer is B (Isolate the saw's power source, lock it out, and tag it before starting.): Lockout and tagout exists precisely because unplugging or switching off a machine can be undone by someone else without realizing a blade change is underway at that moment. Asking a coworker to watch is not a substitute for physically isolating and locking the actual energy source itself. Locking out and tagging the saw removes the chance of it being powered up while a hand is near the blade during the change.
  31. The correct answer is A (Flag the discrepancy to the site supervisor and confirm the correct number before framing.): A mismatch between the individual dimensions and the overall callout means one of the numbers on the drawing is wrong, and guessing which one to trust risks building the error directly into the framing. Averaging the two numbers or picking one without confirmation both still risk landing on the wrong figure. Raising it with the supervisor while it is still just a discrepancy on paper costs nothing compared to correcting it after material is already cut.
  32. The correct answer is C (Check the contract's order of precedence and confirm with the site supervisor first.): When drawings and specifications conflict, the contract usually states which document governs, and that stated precedence, not a personal rule of thumb about which document feels more current, decides the outcome. Defaulting automatically to either document, or choosing based on cost, risks building to a standard the contract never actually intended for this wall. Confirming with the supervisor resolves the conflict correctly before any lumber for the wall is even ordered.
  33. The correct answer is C (Adjust the string line until the diagonal reads exactly 10 feet at that corner.): A diagonal reading longer than the target length means the angle between the two legs is not a true 90 degrees, and the fix is to swing the string line until the diagonal reads exactly 10 feet. The 6-8-10 ratio and the 3-4-5 ratio are the same relationship scaled up, so switching between them changes nothing about the actual measured error at this corner. Accepting an out-of-square corner lets that error compound into every wall and sheet of material built from this line.
  34. The correct answer is A (An error has crept into one of the building lines despite every corner checking out.): Each corner being individually square does not guarantee the overall shape is a true rectangle, because a small error in a line's length or direction between two corners can still throw the diagonals off even when every local angle checks out fine. Equal diagonals are the actual test for a true rectangle, so a real difference between them signals a real layout problem rather than a normal variation to expect. Elevation differences at the batter boards affect level, not the horizontal squareness the diagonal check measures.
  35. The correct answer is A (Check the structural drawings to confirm bearing status rather than trusting the line weight.): Line weight on a floor plan is a helpful visual convention, but the chapter is explicit that it is not a reliable way to confirm bearing status alone, especially when another clue like the wall above lining up suggests otherwise. Guessing based on appearance, adding a header without understanding why, or asking a non-structural trade for an opinion all skip the actual verification step needed here. The structural drawings settle the question with certainty before any framing decision gets made on this wall.
  36. The correct answer is B (Order a few extra studs to cover corners, partition tie-ins, and later openings added.): The simplified calculation deliberately leaves out corners, partition intersections, and the extra studs every opening needs, so treating 19 as the final number would leave the crew short as soon as real drawn conditions get framed. Doubling the order wastes material and money for no real benefit on this run. Ordering piece by piece during framing slows the crew down waiting on deliveries, while a modest allowance above the calculated count matches how this kind of estimate is meant to be used.
  37. The correct answer is B (Request the missing detail sheet before framing that section, rather than guessing from the plan.): A detail callout exists because the floor plan does not have room to show the blocking, headers, and dimensions that particular condition actually needs, and framing without it means guessing at information the designer intended to specify precisely elsewhere. A detail borrowed from an unrelated past job may not match this building's dimensions or structural design at all in practice. Getting the correct sheet before cutting any material avoids rework on a complex, highly visible feature of the house.
  38. The correct answer is D (Confirm the benchmark against another fixed, known-elevation reference before relying on it further.): Every elevation on the job is measured from the benchmark, so if the benchmark itself has moved, every elevation derived from it since would be wrong without anyone realizing it right away. Averaging an uncertain reading with another reference does not resolve the uncertainty, it just blends a possible error into a brand new number. Checking the benchmark against a separate fixed reference confirms whether it is still reliable before more layout work depends on it going forward.
  39. The correct answer is C (Wait for the framing inspection to be completed before closing in the walls.): A framing inspection has to happen while the structural framing, sheathing, and rough-in work are still visible, and enclosing the wall before that inspection removes the inspector's ability to actually see and approve the work done. Photographs and a removable covering are workarounds that do not carry the legal weight of a completed inspection and could still require the wall to be opened back up later. Waiting for the inspection protects the schedule far better than risking a stop-work order or forced rework.
  40. The correct answer is C (Get the change documented and approved as a change order before making it.): A verbal instruction given after construction has started is exactly what a change order exists to formalize, protecting both the tradesperson doing the work and the project's approved documentation going forward. Acting on a verbal instruction alone leaves no record if a dispute arises later about who authorized the deviation from the approved drawings on file. Refusing outright or splitting the difference are both overreactions that ignore the simple, correct step of documenting the change before it gets built.
  41. The correct answer is A (Verify the door's swing arc will clear the window trim, adjusting jamb layout if needed.): When a door and a window sit close together near the same corner, the door's swing arc can strike the window casing or trim even though each opening is individually dimensioned correctly on its own. Assuming the drawing already accounts for that conflict skips the exact check this crowded corner is prompting the carpenter to make. Relocating the window or shrinking the door width without confirming with the designer solves the wrong problem and could violate required code minimums.
  42. The correct answer is A (Cover and insulate the concrete, or arrange heat, before nightfall rather than risk freezing.): Concrete that freezes before it gains enough strength suffers permanent damage from expanding ice crystals disrupting hydration, so the risk has to be addressed before the temperature actually drops overnight, not after the fact. Fresh concrete does generate some heat from hydration, but that alone is not reliable protection against a hard overnight freeze on this job. Adding extra water weakens the mix rather than protecting it, and waiting until morning means any damage has already happened by then.
  43. The correct answer is A (Reject the load, or have the batch adjusted, rather than accept an off-spec slump.): A slump outside the specification usually means a lower final strength than the mix was actually designed to deliver, and finishing technique cannot restore strength that the water-cement ratio has already given up in the batch. Accepting the load simply because it pours easily trades long-term durability for short-term convenience on the driveway. Adding cement on site is not a controlled or reliable way to fix the ratio and could easily make the problem worse instead of better.
  44. The correct answer is C (Stop the pour, brace or reinforce the bowing section, and confirm it before resuming.): A form starting to bow signals the bracing is not adequate for the pressure being generated at that height, and continuing to add concrete only increases that pressure on an already weakening section of formwork. Slowing the pour elsewhere on the wall does nothing for the section already showing signs of failure right now. Stopping to reinforce the bowing area before resuming is the only option that actually addresses the cause rather than hoping it holds until the pour finishes.
  45. The correct answer is D (Have the discrepancy assessed against the engineered footing design before building further.): A footing's width was sized to spread the building's load within the soil's bearing capacity, so an undersized footing is a structural question, not a cosmetic one, and needs to be checked against the design rather than assumed fine. Chipping concrete away elsewhere does not add bearing area where it is actually needed under this narrowed section. Pouring an unplanned addition without checking the design could create a poor bond or still fall short of what is required for this footing.
  46. The correct answer is C (Stop and reposition the rebar to the correct height with proper chairs first.): Rebar is placed at a specific depth, called cover, so it sits where it actually does the structural work while staying protected from moisture, and settling to the bottom of the form defeats both purposes even though it stays surrounded by concrete overall. Adding more steel on top does not fix the wrong position of the steel that is already placed incorrectly in the form. Repositioning it with proper chairs before the pour continues is the only way to get the cover the design assumed for this wall.
  47. The correct answer is D (Brace the wall temporarily, or wait for the floor framing, before any backfilling begins.): A foundation wall poured and cured on its own has no lateral support at the top until the floor system is framed on top of it, so backfilling too early, especially with heavy equipment, can push the wall inward or crack it. Backfilling from the interior side is not how a below-grade foundation wall is actually built, and it does not solve the missing top support problem either. Scheduling convenience for the excavator does not outweigh the risk of damaging a wall that is not yet braced properly.
  48. The correct answer is B (Plan to fog the surface, shade the work, or use a retarder to slow moisture loss.): Hot, windy conditions cause rapid moisture loss that can crack the surface before finishing is even complete, so the plan needs to address that moisture loss directly rather than just extend the finishing schedule alone. Adding water weakens the mix and does not solve evaporation actually happening right at the surface as the slab sets. Waiting until the surface feels fully dry actually makes cracking more likely, since finishing needs to happen while the surface is still workable, not once it has already dried out.
  49. The correct answer is B (Have the mix corrected to an air-entrained design before pouring the driveway slab.): Air-entrained concrete has microscopic air bubbles that give expanding freezing water somewhere to go, and without it, exterior flatwork exposed to freeze-thaw cycles and de-icing salt will scale and spall within a few winters regardless of its raw compressive strength number. A water reducer addresses workability, not the freeze-thaw protection that air entrainment specifically provides for this kind of exterior slab. A surface sealant applied later cannot substitute for a property that needed to be built into the mix itself from the start.
  50. The correct answer is B (Wait the appropriate stripping time for the actual weather conditions before form removal.): Cold temperatures slow curing significantly, so stripping times need to be extended beyond the usual guideline rather than shortened to meet a schedule, and stripping too soon can let the concrete sag, crack, or lose its shape. A fixed 24-hour rule ignores that the actual required time depends on the real weather conditions that week, not a generic summer default figure. Partially stripping a form does not avoid the risk either, since the whole wall still needs to support itself once any bracing is removed.
  51. The correct answer is D (Confirm the depth against the local municipality's own frost depth requirement directly.): Frost depth requirements are set locally and can vary significantly even within the same municipality's zoning map, and the reason a footing needs to reach below frost depth has nothing to do with how much load it carries, since even a lightly loaded footing above the frost line will still heave. Copying the house's footing depth without checking assumes it was designed for identical conditions, which is not guaranteed for a separate garage structure. There is no single frost depth number that applies uniformly everywhere across the country.
  52. The correct answer is D (Round up to the next available increment, 2.0 cubic metres, rather than run short.): Rounding down to 1.8 cubic metres falls short of the 1.92 cubic metres already calculated as needed, which risks running out mid-pour and creating an unwanted cold joint partway through the footing itself. A supplier delivering in fixed increments will not typically break from that system for a fraction of a cubic metre on one small job. Ordering the next full increment above the calculated amount, 2.0 cubic metres, guarantees enough concrete without leaving the crew short during the pour.
  53. The correct answer is B (Consult the engineer, or add an approved alternative connection, before pouring the wall.): A keyway and dowels exist specifically to lock the wall and footing together as one unit, resisting lateral and shear forces at that cold joint between the two separate pours. A plain butt joint between two separate pours does not provide that same mechanical connection on its own, no matter how well the surfaces bond. Drilling dowels in after the wall is already poured cannot recreate the connection that needed to be built into the original footing pour from the start of the job.
  54. The correct answer is D (Contact the inspector or engineer, since the footing sits above the frost depth line.): The diagram shows the footing sitting entirely above the frost depth line rather than reaching below it, which risks frost heave cracking the footing and everything built on top of it once the ground freezes and thaws each winter. A generic residential footing depth number does not override the actual local frost depth requirement shown clearly in this specific cross-section. Detached garages are not exempt from frost protection requirements, so this shallow footing needs to be reviewed rather than simply built over as is.
  55. The correct answer is B (Stop and have a proper footing installed under the post before proceeding further.): A post has to carry its load down to a footing sized for that exact load, and landing on subfloor sheathing, no matter how well fastened it is, breaks the load path at exactly that point in the structure. Shimming or adding blocking around the post changes nothing about what is actually bearing that load underneath it right now. The load does not disappear just because the sheathing looks solid, it simply finds an unintended and unengineered path down through the rest of the framing.
  56. The correct answer is D (Flag it for engineering review, and add proper support below, before proceeding further.): A stamped drawing set can still contain an error that was not caught during review, and a misaligned bearing wall breaks the load path exactly the same way regardless of whether it was approved on paper beforehand. Extra nailing addresses the connection between the wall and the floor, not the missing structural support that is actually needed below that floor. Subfloor sheathing is designed to carry distributed floor loads spread across joists, not a concentrated line load coming down from an unsupported bearing wall above it.
  57. The correct answer is D (Check the cantilever length and backspan against the design, since a short backspan cannot balance it.): A cantilever relies on the anchored backspan to balance the load hanging past the support, the way a seesaw needs weight on both sides of its pivot point, so an unusually short backspan here is a real warning sign rather than a normal variation to expect. More fasteners at the rim joist do not add the leverage that a longer backspan would actually provide to the assembly. Reducing joist depth changes the section's stiffness but does not address the fundamental balance problem this short backspan creates for the whole cantilever.
  58. The correct answer is D (Confirm the header size for this opening's actual span and load before framing it.): Header size depends directly on the span of the specific opening and the load actually bearing down on it, so a wider opening carrying the same storey and roof load needs a header sized for that greater span, not whatever size was reused elsewhere. Wider jack studs support the header's ends but do nothing to increase the header's own capacity to span across this particular opening safely. Adding king studs strengthens the sides of the opening but does not change what the header itself needs to carry across that gap.
  59. The correct answer is C (Do not allow the notch as cut, and have it repaired per the manufacturer's guidelines.): Engineered I-joists cannot be notched or drilled outside the manufacturer's guidelines, because removing material from the wrong location can remove exactly the part of the flange or web doing the structural work, regardless of how strong the joist seems overall at a glance. Filling the notch with adhesive does not restore the removed structural material that was cut away by the plumber. Relocating an out-of-guideline notch to a different part of the span does not make it compliant either, since the guideline itself defines both the allowed size and the allowed locations.
  60. The correct answer is D (Confirm with the header schedule whether doubled jack studs are required, and add them.): Jack studs carry the header's reaction load straight down to the sole plate, and a wide opening carrying a second-storey and roof load often needs doubled or tripled jack studs on each side to spread that reaction over enough bearing area at the base. A deeper header addresses the header's own span capacity but does not change how much bearing area is actually available underneath its two ends. Adding a king stud to only one side does not address the jack stud count needed on either side of this opening.
  61. The correct answer is B (Valley rafters run a longer diagonal horizontal distance, so a longer rafter is expected): a valley rafter runs on a diagonal in plan view rather than square to the building, so it covers more horizontal run than a common rafter on the same structure. Covering more run at the same pitch always produces a longer rafter by the same Pythagorean relationship that governs common rafter length. This is expected behaviour, not a sign of a cutting error.
  62. The correct answer is A (Order a wider ridge board so the entire plumb cut face bears against it): the ridge board should be at least as wide as the full depth of the rafter's plumb cut so the entire cut face bears against it, with enough material left for proper nailing. Notching the rafters or using an undersized ridge reduces bearing surface and weakens the connection at the peak. Ordering correctly sized stock before fastening avoids a rework later.
  63. The correct answer is D (Re-lay out the winders so the walking line keeps a consistent stride): winders need careful individual tread layout because each has a different shape, and the walking line across a run of winders has to be checked for a safe, consistent stride. A cramped feel signals the walking line was not properly checked or spaced during layout. Re-laying it out to keep the stride consistent is the correct fix rather than living with an uneven feel underfoot.
  64. The correct answer is C (Remark and recut the stringer so every riser height is consistent): consistency across every riser is what keeps a stair safe, since even a small height difference is something every person using the stair will feel and can trip over. A 3/8 inch outlier is well beyond a tight, acceptable tolerance and cannot be safely masked with a shim or by adjusting unrelated risers. Remarking and recutting the stringer restores a uniform riser height on every step.
  65. The correct answer is B (Reframe the opening to correct the tight spot found partway up the run): minimum headroom is checked along the entire slope of the stair, not just one point, since an opening framed a little short can leave adequate headroom at one end and dangerously little elsewhere. Accepting the opening because the bottom measurement passed ignores the actual tight point that governs safety. Reframing the opening to fix the tight spot along the run is the correct response.
  66. The correct answer is D (Refuse to cut it until the truss manufacturer approves the modification): truss members, chords and webs alike, cannot be cut, notched, drilled beyond what the manufacturer allows, or otherwise modified in the field without stamped engineering approval for that specific change. A truss is a complete triangulated system, and cutting even one web member can remove load path the whole assembly depends on. Refusing until proper approval is obtained is the correct and safe response.
  67. The correct answer is A (Order or rebuild the stringer grooved for the correct as-built rise): a housed stringer has grooves routed for a specific riser layout, so it cannot be adjusted for a different total rise without disturbing that fixed geometry. Shimming the bottom or changing only one riser height would produce an inconsistent stair, which compromises safety. Ordering or rebuilding a stringer grooved for the actual as-built rise keeps every riser consistent.
  68. The correct answer is C (Flag the missing exhaust vent so intake and exhaust airflow both exist): the basic ventilation strategy pairs intake at the soffit with exhaust higher up at a ridge or roof vent, moving air continuously from low to high to carry moisture out of the attic. Soffit intake alone with no exhaust path does not complete that airflow loop and leaves the attic prone to trapped moisture and ice damming. Flagging the missing exhaust vent before the roof is closed in is the correct response.
  69. The correct answer is D (Change to an L-shaped or U-shaped stair using the available landing): an L-shaped or U-shaped stair uses a landing to change direction, which is useful exactly where a straight run would be too long for the available space. Forcing unusually short treads or tall risers to fit a straight run sacrifices the walking comfort a proper riser-to-tread relationship is meant to provide. Using the available landing with a direction change solves the space problem without distorting stride comfort.
  70. The correct answer is B (Set that rafter aside and cut a replacement from new stock): the seat cut, or birdsmouth, has to be cut carefully, since removing too much material weakens the rafter at its bearing point. A noticeably over-deep seat cut has already compromised that bearing point, and no amount of extra nailing or flipping the rafter restores the lost material. Setting the rafter aside and cutting a replacement is the correct response to a compromised structural member.
  71. The correct answer is A (Water reaching the sill could drain backward or sideways into the wall cavity): a proper sill pan has back and end dams so any water sitting on the sill drains out the front over the exterior wall surface rather than leaking backward into the wall cavity or sideways past the jambs. A flat piece of membrane with no dams provides no barrier against that backward or sideways path. The most likely consequence of installing over it as is water finding its way into the wall.
  72. The correct answer is C (Correct the lap so the upper course overlaps the lower course properly): house wrap has to be installed shingle-lap so water always runs down and over the layer beneath rather than behind it. An upper course tucked behind a lower one directs water running down the wrap straight into that seam and behind the barrier, and taping alone does not reliably fix a reversed lap direction. Correcting the lap before siding covers the area is the correct next step.
  73. The correct answer is B (The panels will buckle or warp as they try to contract and cannot move): vinyl siding expands and contracts significantly with temperature, so the nailing hem is designed with slotted holes and the panel is meant to hang loosely rather than being pulled tight against the sheathing. Over-driving the nails pins the panel in place, so when temperatures drop and the vinyl tries to contract, it has nowhere to go and buckles or warps visibly. This is a common real-world callback item precisely because it shows up seasons after installation.
  74. The correct answer is D (Temporarily cover each rough opening to keep wind-driven rain out overnight): house wrap alone is not a substitute for proper window flashing at an open rough opening, and leaving openings unprotected invites wind-driven rain into the wall assembly overnight. Installing windows without proper flashing just to beat the rain creates a bigger, longer-term water problem than a temporary cover would. Temporarily protecting each opening is the correct short-term action before the crew leaves.
  75. The correct answer is A (Hold that section of siding until step flashing is woven in first): step flashing is woven in with each course of shingles as the roof is installed, and a continuous piece of flashing added after the fact, or siding installed with no step flashing behind it, cannot accommodate the differential movement between the roof and the wall the way properly woven step flashing does. Holding the siding until the roofer completes the step flashing preserves the correct shingle-lap water-shedding sequence at that intersection.
  76. The correct answer is C (Install proper ledger flashing integrated with the house's water barrier): a ledger board attached without proper flashing is one of the most common sources of hidden structural rot in residential decks, even when the framing currently looks sound. Ledger flashing needs a metal flashing cap integrated with the house's water-resistive barrier in the same shingle-lap order so water sheds out and away from the connection. Installing proper flashing on the new ledger, rather than repeating the unflashed original detail, is the correct step.
  77. The correct answer is B (Remove the felt in the valley and install proper ice and water shield): ice and water shield, a self-adhering rubberized membrane, is required in the areas most vulnerable to ice damming and concentrated water flow, and every valley is one of those areas because water volume concentrates there. Standard felt does not provide the same self-sealing protection against ice damming and wind-driven water in a valley. Removing the felt and installing proper ice and water shield before shingling is the correct step.
  78. The correct answer is B (Water discharged right at the foundation is soaking back into the basement): downspouts need to discharge well clear of the foundation, since water dumped right at the foundation is one of the most common causes of basement moisture problems and foundation heaving in a freeze-thaw climate. A downspout ending right against the foundation wall at the damp corner is a strong, direct match to the reported symptom. This is the most likely contributing cause among the options given.
  79. The correct answer is A (Airflow from soffit to ridge is blocked, so baffles should be added): soffit ventilation is the intake half of the attic ventilation system, and baffles are what keep loose attic insulation from drifting over and blocking that intake path at the eave. Insulation covering the soffit openings from inside breaks the continuous low-to-high airflow the ventilation system depends on, even though the vented soffit panel itself is still in place outside. Adding baffles restores the intended airflow path.
  80. The correct answer is C (Remove and reinstall the flashing so side laps over sill and head laps over side): the correct flashing order is sill pan first, then side flashing overlapping the sill pan, then head flashing last overlapping the top of the side flashing, so water running down the wall always meets an overlap that sheds it outward rather than one that funnels it behind the barrier. Installing the head flashing first and tucking side flashing underneath it reverses that shedding logic at the top corners. Removing and reinstalling in the correct order is the only real fix.
  81. The correct answer is B (Re-check the story pole and gradually adjust reveal so the sill course lands correctly): a story pole is marked out with the reveal spacing for the full wall height specifically to keep courses consistent and to make sure courses land correctly at window sills and door heads without an odd sliver course at a critical location. Letting the drift continue or forcing a single narrow filler strip creates a visible inconsistency right at the window. Re-checking the story pole and gradually correcting the reveal keeps the wall looking uniform while still landing correctly at the sill.
  82. The correct answer is D (Open the cavity and install proper backing before the wall gets closed in): backing has to happen before the drywall closes the cavity, because there is no practical way to add it afterward without cutting the wall open again. Relying on drywall anchors for a grab bar load, or simply noting the gap for a future job, does not address the fact this specific wall is about to be closed permanently. Opening the cavity now and installing proper backing before drywall is the correct action.
  83. The correct answer is A (Install a horizontal ladder of blocking across a wider band of the wall): the safest approach when the exact backing location is not confirmed is to run a horizontal ladder of blocking across a wider band of the wall so the eventual installer has flexibility. Guessing a single standard height risks missing the actual bracket location once it is finally confirmed, and delaying the whole schedule is not necessary when a wider blocking band solves the uncertainty. This proactive approach avoids a callback without stalling the job.
  84. The correct answer is C (Flag that any penetration needs a listed firestop system coordinated with the design): any penetration through a rated wall or ceiling needs to be coordinated with the design and closed with a listed firestop system, not just packed with insulation and closed up. A rated wall or ceiling depends on every layer, including how penetrations are sealed, matching the tested design exactly, and an uncoordinated hole can compromise the fire and smoke separation the building depends on. Flagging the correct firestop requirement is the correct response.
  85. The correct answer is B (Locate the actual stud positions and adjust fastening or add blocking there): cabinets, especially loaded wall cabinets, need to be fastened into solid framing rather than just drywall anchors, so a mismatch between the drawings and the actual framing has to be resolved against what is really behind the wall. Fastening into drywall alone or moving the whole cabinet run to match incorrect drawings both risk an inadequately supported cabinet. Locating the real stud positions and adjusting fastening or adding blocking is the correct approach.
  86. The correct answer is A (Delay installation and address the moisture source until levels are acceptable): wood flooring will absorb moisture from a damp subfloor and can cup, crown, or buckle after installation, so moisture testing confirms the subfloor is dry enough before flooring goes down. Installing on schedule and hoping on-site acclimation fixes an already-failed subfloor test, or adjusting the expansion gap instead, does not address the underlying moisture problem. Delaying installation until the moisture source is addressed and levels pass is the correct action.
  87. The correct answer is D (The head or strike jamb was shimmed slightly out of square): once the hinge side is confirmed plumb and fixed, the head jamb and the strike side jamb are shimmed to match it, and dragging or binding at the top corner opposite the hinges is a sign the jamb has been shimmed slightly out of square relative to the fixed hinge side. The hinge side was already confirmed plumb, which rules it out as the cause here. This makes an out-of-square head or strike jamb the most likely explanation for binding only at that one corner.
  88. The correct answer is C (Wood movement opened the mitre; coping resists that gap over time): a mitred inside corner is only tight the day it is installed, and as the walls settle slightly or the wood shrinks, a mitred inside corner opens up into a visible gap because both pieces rely on the two cut faces staying in perfect contact. A coped joint stays tight even as the wood moves slightly, since the coped piece's profile rides against the flat face of the first piece instead of against a matching angled cut. Coping rather than mitring at that inside corner would have prevented the gap.
  89. The correct answer is B (That wall is slightly out of square compared to the rest of the room): if a spring angle jig or saw setting were the actual problem, the error would show up consistently at every corner cut with those same settings, not repeatedly at just one particular wall. A wall that is slightly out of square compared to the others in the room requires a different actual cut angle at that corner even though the fence and jig settings look identical. That localized, wall-specific mismatch is the most likely explanation for an error confined to a single corner.
  90. The correct answer is A (Add proper support before the stone top is set to prevent cracking): longer unsupported spans and cantilevered overhangs, especially under a heavier stone top, often need additional support such as corbels, brackets, or blocking built into the cabinet or wall framing to carry the load and prevent the counter from cracking or sagging over time. Proceeding without support or relying on adhesive alone risks exactly that cracking or sagging once the stone is loaded and used. Adding proper support before the stone is set is the correct fix.
  91. The correct answer is A (Sound bridges through those fasteners, defeating the channel's decoupling purpose): resilient channel is meant to decouple the drywall from direct contact with the framing, and drywall screws driven through the channel into the stud behind it reconnect that direct path, letting sound bridge straight through at each of those fastener points. A single stud bridging both rows of a staggered wall or screws through resilient channel into the stud behind it defeats the whole point of the assembly at that weak point. The wall's acoustic performance is compromised even though everything else about the assembly may be correct.
  92. The correct answer is C (Install and caulk the casing first, then have the painter finish around it): once trim is installed, gaps and joints are caulked with a paintable acrylic caulk to give a seamless, paint ready line, which only works if the casing goes up and gets caulked before final painting covers those joints. Painting the walls first and installing casing afterward would leave unpainted, unfinished trim and require cutting in paint around new trim edges. Installing and caulking the casing first, then letting the painter finish around it, is the correct sequencing.
  93. The correct answer is B (Install temporary shoring on both sides before removing the wall): joists ending directly over a doubled joist above a wall is a strong sign that wall is the bearing point for that framing, since perpendicular joists ending or splicing over a wall generally indicate a load-bearing condition. Once a wall is confirmed or strongly suspected load-bearing, temporary shoring goes in on both sides before any permanent framing comes out. Removing the wall first, or assuming only exterior walls carry load, ignores that strong bearing signal and risks a real structural failure.
  94. The correct answer is A (Report the finding and repair the rotted section before closing the wall): once walls are opened up, it is common to find conditions like rot that were never part of the original scope, and covering a known defect back up because it is not technically part of the scope is a decision that comes back on the carpenter later. The correct approach is to communicate the discovered condition promptly and repair it before the wall closes, not to hide it or defer it to another year. Reporting and repairing the rot now is the correct next step.
  95. The correct answer is D (Bring the replaced window up to current egress and sill height requirements): the specific area being renovated typically needs to be brought up to current code, even though the rest of the house may be legally grandfathered under whatever code applied when it was originally built. This means a bedroom window being replaced needs to meet current egress size and sill height requirements even if the rest of the house keeps smaller original windows. Matching the old undersized window or leaving it as is would reproduce a condition that does not meet current code in a permitted renovation scope.
  96. The correct answer is C (Stop work in that area and confirm it is dead before proceeding): electrical systems need to be identified and shut off, or at minimum confirmed dead, before demolition happens anywhere near them, especially older wiring not shown on any drawing. Cutting the wiring immediately or continuing demolition around it without confirming its status takes an unnecessary risk with personal safety. Stopping work and confirming the wiring is dead before proceeding is the correct response.
  97. The correct answer is A (Involve an engineer to confirm the load path before making any changes): no single check, framing direction alone or an aligned post and footing alone, is proof on its own, and when the signals conflict the correct move is to involve an engineer or other qualified person rather than picking one clue as decisive. Guessing at a bearing wall removal, or treating one conflicting signal as automatically stronger than the other, is one of the few mistakes in carpentry that can genuinely put people at risk. Getting qualified confirmation before making any changes is the correct next step.
  98. The correct answer is B (Sister an additional joist or frame around the interruption instead): building codes and span tables restrict notches and bored holes in specific locations, generally away from the outer thirds of a joist's depth near mid-span, because that area carries the highest bending stresses. When a required hole location falls outside those permitted limits, the correct approach is to sister an additional joist alongside the cut one or install proper framing such as headers and trimmers to carry the load around the interruption. Drilling anyway or simply relocating the hole within the same joist does not resolve the underlying limit violation.
  99. The correct answer is D (Shim or pack out the new framing so the surfaces end up flat): true-sawn lumber from older construction genuinely measured 2 inches by 4 inches, while modern dressed lumber of the same nominal size measures 1-1/2 inches by 3-1/2 inches, so a new wall framed at modern dimensions will not line up flush against an original true-dimension wall without adjustment. Reframing the entire original wall or relying on thicker drywall alone are impractical or unreliable fixes for this dimensional mismatch. Shimming or packing out the new framing so the finished surfaces end up flat and in plane is the correct fix.
  100. The correct answer is C (Identify and address the moisture cause, and remediate affected material first): when moisture or mould is discovered, the correct response is to identify and address the underlying cause before closing the assembly back up, since covering damaged or mould-affected material without fixing what caused it sets up the same failure to happen again behind new finishes. Because this contamination is limited to one section of drywall and the framing directly behind it, with no wider spread, it stays within the scope of the carpenter's own work rather than requiring a qualified remediation professional, unlike more extensive contamination spreading through the framing. Addressing the cause and remediating the affected material first is the correct response before new finishes go on.
